#3730. Nopales Benefits: Why You Might Add Cactus to Your Diet

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Key takeaways

  • Nopales, the edible pads of the prickly pear cactus, contain antioxidants like ascorbic acid and phenolics that may help fight inflammation in your body.
  • Research suggests nopales may help manage blood sugar levels, though evidence is mixed. A small study found lower blood sugar after eating nopales with a high-carb meal, but larger reviews note insufficient evidence for diabetes prevention.
  • You can eat fresh nopales by boiling, grilling, or sautéing them, drink prickly pear juice, or take supplements containing 500 to 650 milligrams per day. Some people may experience mild nausea, diarrhea, or fullness when starting out.

Looking at a cactus with its threatening spikes and tough skin, you might not immediately think, “Yep, that’s a thing I could eat.” But guess what? Some forms of cacti are edible, tasty, and quite good for you!

Check out why it might be a good idea to pal around with nopales, the pads of the prickly pear cactus.

What is a nopal cactus?

The nopal cactus, also known as the prickly pear or the opuntia, is a shrub-like, flowering cactus native to parts of Mexico, Arizona, the Mediterranean, Australia, and other desert regions. It might be recognized by its multitude of connected pads with flowering blooms. (A pad in bloom looks a little like a flat, green paw.)

Nopales flowers are filled with a viscous, seeded substance often used in jams, jellies, pies, cakes, and other sweets. But the stems, leaves, and entire pad (with spines removed, obvs) are also edible. Traditional Mexican preparations include boiling, grilling, and sautéing.

Benefits of nopales cactus

Science is still examining the possible benefits of nopales, so some touted wellness perks are fairly tentative. Here’s a look at what we know about these desert fruits.

Has anti-inflammatory properties

Almost any time you see bright colors in edible fruits, it’s a sign they contain antioxidants. This is def true for Nopales!

Older studies have found that the fruit inside their flowers is rich in antioxidants like ascorbic acid, phenolics, and a mixture of betacyanin and betaxanthin. These compounds help fight inflammation by kicking harmful free radicals out of your cells.

Might improve joint mobility

Since they combat inflammation, the antioxidants in prickly pear could improve joint mobility. In a 2020 study, people who drank 3 ounces of prickly pear juice daily for 8 weeks had better range of motion and greater ease with daily activities than a control group who drank a placebo.

Could help manage blood sugar

Research suggests nopales may help steady blood sugar. A 2014 study found that people with type 2 diabetes who ate nopales with a high-carb breakfast had significantly lower blood sugar and insulin levels compared to those who didn’t. However, this study only included 14 participants.

In 2019, a larger systematic review assessed the fruit’s potential for blood sugar regulation. While it found insufficient evidence to support using nopales to reduce the risk of type 2 diabetes, the authors noted it might significantly reduce blood glucose levels.

Could support liver health

Showing your liver some love might involve adding nopales (or a glass of nopal juice) to your diet. According to research from 2023, this has to do with prickly pear’s effects on triglycerides. By helping reduce triglyceride accumulation, nopales could relieve stress on the liver.

Contains electrolytes

Whether you’ve overdone it on booze or are down with a stomach bug, it’s important to rehydrate with a drink that contains electrolytes. Consider prickly pear juice as your back-on-the-wagon beverage. It’s got potassium, magnesium, and calcium to help rebalance your electrolyte levels.

Forms and doses of nopal cactus

If you can track down fresh nopales where you live, cooking it yourself is a fun (if somewhat complicated) way to take advantage of its benefits. If not, look for prickly pear products, such as juices, being sure to check for added sugars.

You can also take a supplement. Nopales supplements typically contain 500-650 milligrams (mg) per day.

Nopal cactus side effects

While a prickly pear smoothie or nopales salad can be a healthy addition to your diet, consuming this cactus fruit may have some less-than-desirable side effects, like:

  • nausea
  • mild diarrhea
  • feeling full

If you’re new to nopales, take it slow at first to see how your body responds.

Takeaway

Don’t let the “prickly” in prickly pear keep you away from this desert gem. When prepared correctly, nopales are unique, nutritious food. Check ’em out when your fruit basket needs something a little different.

#1167. El Nino is a 'postcard from the future' — and could cause 451,000 excess heat deaths

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The current El Nino could cause 451,000 excess heat deaths. Researchers warn it offers a glimpse of temperatures that may become normal within 20 years.

Peruvian fishermen gave El Nino its name — "the little boy" or Christ Child — after noticing unusually warm Pacific waters around Christmas that caused the fish they depended on to suddenly become scarce.

But what those fishermen observed locally can have consequences thousands of kilometers away. El Nino can shift temperature and rainfall patterns around the world, bringing extreme heat and drought to some regions and heavy rain and flooding to others.

Researchers now estimate that the unusually high temperatures linked to the current El Nino could cause around 451,000 excess deaths worldwide from June 2026 to February 2027.

The event is a "postcard from the future," Tamma Carleton, head of research at the University of Chicago's Climate Impact Lab, told DW. Land temperatures over the coming months are forecast to run about 1.2 degrees C (2.1 F) above normal.

"For the average person, that's roughly the warming climate change is projected to bring about 20 years from now, arriving all at once," Carleton said.

Normally, winds blowing west across the Pacific push warm surface water towards Asia and Australia, allowing colder water to rise near South America. During El Nino, which occurs erratically every two to seven years, those winds weaken. Less cold water then rises, and much more of the Pacific stays unusually warm.

The warmer water heats the air above it and fuels rainfall over the central and eastern Pacific. The rising air spreads high in the atmosphere, sending waves that disrupt weather worldwide.

"It is like throwing a stone into a lake," climate researcher Fred Kucharski of the Abdus Salam International Centre for Theoretical Physics in Trieste, Italy, told DW. "Initially the surface perturbation is local, but then the energy spreads all over the lake through wave propagation."

Kucharski said the effects can cross the tropics within one week and reach higher latitudes in about two. Where the waves travel determines whether a region becomes hotter, cooler, wetter or drier.

The current El Nino developed in June and has strengthened rapidly since. It is now on course to become an exceptionally strong — sometimes called "super" — El Nino.

The Climate Impact Lab says its estimate covers only the first nine months of El Nino, as far as the current seasonal forecasts allow. The event is expected to continue into Northern Hemisphere spring, and its effect on global temperatures could last longer.

"We expect the final number to be meaningfully higher than what we are currently reporting here," Carleton said.

The estimate of 451,000 excess deaths covers deaths linked to the direct health effects of unusually high temperatures associated with El Nino.

Extreme heat strains the heart and kidneys, makes it harder for the body to maintain a safe temperature and can worsen cardiovascular, respiratory and other illnesses. As a result, the underlying condition is often recorded as the cause of death rather than the heat that aggravated it.

"Most heat deaths never show up in official statistics as heat deaths," Carleton said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

El Nino can also alter rainfall, contributing to drought in some regions and heavy rain and flooding in others.

Floods can kill, contaminate drinking water and spread disease. Drought can damage harvests and cause water and food shortages.

Deaths from these other indirect effects are not included in the 451,000 projection.

The Climate Impact Lab expects some of the largest increases in deaths in the Sahel, South and Southeast Asia and parts of Latin America. The highest numbers of fatalities are predicted for Nigeria, Sudan, Niger, Chad, India, Indonesia and Brazil.

Many are projected to occur between December and February, meaning much of the expected toll still lies ahead.

"Heat headlines usually focus on Europe and North America," Carleton said. "This event's toll is overwhelmingly in lower-income, already-hot countries, where people have the fewest ways to adapt and where these deaths are least likely to be counted."

Some people who die during extreme heat are already frail. This raises an important question: Is the heat cutting their lives substantially short, or merely bringing forward deaths that would have occurred days or weeks later — an effect known as mortality displacement?

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The underlying research uses annual rather than daily death rates, which largely filters out deaths brought forward by only days or weeks.

Ben Armstrong, an emeritus professor at the London School of Hygiene and Tropical Medicine, said he could not verify the 451,000 figure. But because the estimate is based on annual mortality data, it largely excludes deaths brought forward by only days or weeks. If the figure is correct, he said, most of those counted "would have lived at least six months longer."

Scientists have long estimated the death toll from extreme temperatures retrospectively. Better seasonal forecasting is increasingly allowing researchers to look ahead instead.

To produce the estimate, researchers used earlier work showing how death rates change during hot weather in different parts of the world. They combined it with seasonal forecasts showing how hot each place is expected to become.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"Putting the two together lets us estimate the human cost of this event while there's still time to do something about it, rather than counting the deaths after the fact," Carleton said.

Heat warnings, cooling centers and hydration stations, home visits to vulnerable people, protections for outdoor workers and additional health care capacity could all reduce mortality.

The researchers believe targeted interventions could save tens of thousands of lives.

For Carleton, the forecast is a warning to prepare now for the heat that climate change could make commonplace.

"This El Nino gives us a look at future weather in the 2040s," Carleton said. "These temperatures will become normal in 20 years, giving us time and reason to begin investing in adaptive capacity now."

#955. Nobelitis — How winning a Nobel Prize can give you delusions of grandeur

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After being chosen for one of the world's most prestigious awards, some Nobel laureates lose touch with reality and suddenly start spouting unscientific nonsense. They're afflicted by what some have called "Nobelitis."

Linus Pauling won not one, but two Nobel Prizes — the first, for chemistry, in 1954 for his research into the nature of the chemical bond, and the Peace Prize in 1962 for his work in the anti-nuclear weapons movement.

The American chemist not only won two Nobel Prizes, but won them on his own, a rare feat for any scientist. Such an honor often turns laureates into global superstars overnight, with millions hanging on their every word.

That instant fame can be dangerous, said Nils Hansson, a professor in the Department of History, Theory and Ethics of Medicine at Heinrich Heine University in Düsseldorf. Especially, he said, when these world-renowned experts "start spreading crazy, unproven scientific theories" — ideas that, when disseminated by Nobel laureates, risk being taken seriously.

That's what happened with Pauling. Beginning in the mid 1960s he began claiming that vitamin C was a universal remedy, a cure for everything from cancer to cardiovascular conditions and degenerative diseases like Alzheimer's disease, multiple sclerosis or osteoarthritis.

Pauling developed an alternative treatment he called orthomolecular medicine, based on specific doses of vitamins and minerals for each patient. To this day, some 60 years later, evidence for his methods remains inconclusive.

Pauling's case is a classic example of "Nobelitis," or "Nobel disease" — not an official diagnosis, but an informal term used to describe how some laureates begin to suffer delusions of grandeur later in life. It was first coined by Paul Nurse, president of The Royal Society, the UK's national academy of sciences, and himself a winner of the Nobel Prize.

"Be skeptical of everything, including yourself and your own ideas," Nurse said in a 2019 interview with KU Leuven University in Belgium, when asked what made someone a good scientist. In cases of Nobelitis, that self-skepticism is the first to go.

That was also the case with virologist Luc Montagnier, who was jointly honored in 2008 for his discovery of the human immunodeficiency virus, HIV, with colleague Francoise Barre-Sinoussi in the early 1980s.

Montagnier would later go on to support homeopathic medicine, another alternative treatment of disease and claim that HIV transmission in Africa could be diminished through better hygiene and nutrition.

During the COVID-19 pandemic, Montagnier said the SARS-CoV-2 virus had been created in a lab with parts of the HIV genetic code. "It's difficult for a practicing virologist to say that a Nobel laureate in virology is spreading nonsense," said German virologist Christian Drosten on NDR's Coronavirus Update podcast in May 2020. "But that is utter nonsense."

James Watson, who was never known for his modesty, also let fame go to his head. In 1962, he was awarded the Nobel Prize along with Maurice Wilkins and Francis Crick for their discovery of the structure of the DNA molecule. But in the early 2000s, Watson repeatedly spread racist theories that Black people were less intelligent than white people.

For medical historian Hansson, Watson is a prime example that the Nobel Prize is not automatically awarded to a person who should be considered as a role model.

"You may have made a fantastic discovery, but that doesn't make you a universal genius," he said.

But the great expectations of an admiring public may also contribute to the development of Nobelitis. Paul Nurse, who won the Nobel Prize in physiology or medicine in 2001, soon found himself getting daily requests for interviews on all sorts of topics.

"As if suddenly I was acknowledgeable about all workings of the world and of human beings," he told DW.

Nurse had this advice for researchers: "Be careful quite what you say. Talk about things that you do know something about that would be fine. And if you don't know so much or you're not sure about something, be really clear that you're not sure."

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Hansson has also noticed that the media eagerly report whatever Nobel laureates say — even when prize winners stray far from their area of expertise.

Nobelitis sufferers are leaders in their fields and rightly honored for their work with the Nobel. But they're also regular individuals, not immune to megalomania and other behavioral problems — meaning that homeopathy, orthomolecular medicine and other pseudosciences don't automatically become credible just because they're backed by a Nobel Prize winner.

This article was originally written in German.

#938. Suspected pneumonic plague in Siberia: What we know about the disease

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After a worker at a Russian plague research institute in Siberia died October 2, 2026, nearly 200 of her contacts were under medical observation. But authorities there denied pneumonic plague had caused the death.

UPDATE: Following initial reports raising concerns that a lab worker had died accidentally of pneumonic plague, the European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control (ECDC) said it was now also "closely monitoring the situation."

However, in a statement published October 6, 2026, the ECDC said that "based on the limited information currently available, there are no reports of secondary cases and no evidence of sustained human-to-human transmission that would cause further spread of the disease."

Following the death of a Russian laboratory worker, there is speculation about an outbreak of pneumonic plague, a highly contagious bacterial infection of the lungs. Russia denies there is a broader risk to the community.

Russian officials have said the laboratory worker's death was not caused by a plague pathogen. The 28-year-old died of "pneumonia of unknown origin," according to Russia's Interfax news agency, citing Rospotrebnadzor, Russia's disease-control agency. According to that agency, samples taken from the woman showed no microorganisms "related to her professional activities." This contradicts earlier information.

Local media had previously reported that the woman, who worked at an institute dedicated to fighting plague, had dropped a test tube.

She reportedly died on Friday, October 2, 2026, at the district hospital in Shelekhov, a town near Irkutsk in Siberia.

According to official Russian figures, authorities placed two hospitals and a children's clinic under quarantine. Local reports said staff at the institute were being quarantined at their workplace. The US news site Axios has reported that around 200 people who had been in contact with the woman had been placed under observation. There were no further reported infections.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Even so, distrust of how Russia handles information persists. On one of DW's Russian-language social media channels, a user wrote: "It wouldn't surprise me if this turns into a disaster now, both in terms of the spread and the quarantine measures."

All that is known for certain, at the time of writing and first publication (October 5, 2026), is that pneumonic plague is highly contagious.

Pneumonic plague is a form of plague that infects the lungs. Like every form of the disease, it is caused by Yersinia pestis, a bacterium usually found in small mammals, such as rodents and in the fleas that feed on them.

The World Health Organization describes the pneumonic form as the most virulent type of plague.

Its incubation period can be as short as 24 hours, and it is the only form that passes easily from person to person through respiratory particles released when a sick patient coughs. This is why, according to the WHO, it can trigger severe epidemics.

Symptoms appear quickly: high fever, headache and weakness, followed by a rapidly worsening pneumonia with shortness of breath, chest pain and a cough that often brings up blood.

A person can catch pneumonic plague by breathing in the bacteria directly, or by developing it when bubonic and/or septicemic plague go untreated and spread to the lungs.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Pneumonic plague is not a separate disease. It is one of three forms of plague: bubonic, septicemic and pneumonic.

What sets them apart is where the infection settles in the body, and that shapes how the disease spreads and how deadly it is.

Bubonic plague is the most common form and the one most people think of when they hear the word "plague." It usually follows a flea bite. The bacteria travel to the nearest lymph node, which swells into a painful lump known as a bubo. According to the WHO, it rarely spreads between people.

If bubonic plague goes untreated, the bacteria can move further through the body. If they spread into the bloodstream, the disease can become septicemic plague, which causes bleeding under the skin, tissue death in the fingers and toes, and organ failure. If the bacteria reach the lungs, the illness becomes pneumonic plague, the form that can pass from person to person through the air. Both septicemic and pneumonic plague can also develop directly, without a bubo appearing first.

Left untreated, bubonic plague kills between 30-60% of patients. The septicemic and pneumonic forms are always fatal without treatment.

Pneumonic plague is curable, but only if treatment starts quickly. The WHO warns that without treatment it can kill within 18 to 24 hours of the first symptoms.

Common antibiotics can cure the infection, and recovery rates are high when patients are treated within about a day of falling ill.

Speed is what decides the outcome. Doctors are advised not to wait for laboratory confirmation and to start antibiotics as soon as plague is suspected, according to the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC).

The drugs themselves are not rare or specialized. They are standard, widely used antibiotics, and patients usually need a course of up to two weeks. To stop the infection spreading, patients are kept in isolation, and wearing masks helps protect those around them, the WHO says.

People close to a patient are treated as well, even if they have no symptoms. Both the WHO and the CDC recommend a seven-day course of preventive antibiotics for close contacts of anyone with pneumonic plague.

The WHO does not recommend a plague vaccine for the public. It reserves vaccination for high-risk groups, such as laboratory staff who work with the bacterium and health workers.

#891. Early-onset cancer rising worldwide: Causes, risk, prevention

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Across Latin America, early-onset cancer rates have risen, especially of bowel, breast and cervical cancers. But it is a worldwide trend.

The numbers are striking. Some cancer rates have doubled in the past 20 years. It's just not yet clear whether we're seeing a rise in actual cases or whether we're seeing more early-stage cases because of better awareness, diagnosis and treatment in hospitals.

Take, for example, colorectal (or bowel) cancer: In 2001, about one in every 26,000 people under 50 in England were diagnosed with it each year. By 2023, the rate of diagnoses was about one in 12,000. Twice what it was at the turn of the century.

That's according to the World Cancer Research Fund (WCRF), an international network of charities, which analyzed registry data from England, the United States and the Netherlands. It found the number of new cancer cases had risen for seven cancers in England alone.

So, rates have doubled, but the numbers overall are still small, said Amy Berrington de Gonzalez, a professor of clinical epidemiology at the Institute of Cancer Research in London.

"Cancer is still very uncommon among young people," Berrington de Gonzalez told DW. "Three times a small number is still a small number."

But she and other scientists still want to know why this is happening, because it is a worldwide trend. Latin America is a case in point.

A 2025 study analyzed registry data from 50 countries and territories up to 2017. It found early-onset colorectal cancer rising in 27 of those regions.

In Latin America, rates rose in Chile, Argentina, Ecuador, Costa Rica and Puerto Rico. Chile's rise — at almost 4% per year — was among the highest.

In Brazil, the evidence comes directly from hospitals. At the A.C. Camargo Cancer Center in Sao Paulo, the number of colorectal cancer patients under 50 grew by 7.6% per year between 2000 and 2023. That's a five-fold increase.

The number of patients over 50 grew even faster — at 8.1% — or about six-fold.

But since these figures came from a single hospital, they do not show whether cases are rising across Brazil. They may only reflect that this one hospital is treating more patients.

"We need better cancer registration systems," said Berrington de Gonzalez, "especially in countries like Brazil and Mexico, where obesity rates have also been increasing."

Marc Gunter, professor of cancer epidemiology at Imperial College London, said he expected Latin America to follow richer countries as diets there become more Westernized and lifestyles more sedentary. "I think it's already happening," he said.

In another study in 2025, involving 42 countries, Berrington de Gonzalez compared data from two age groups — 20 to 49 years and 50 years and older — between 2003 and 2017.

Six cancers — thyroid, breast, colorectal, kidney, uterine and leukemia — had risen among the younger groups in most countries. All but colorectal cancer were seen to rise in older adults, too.

In fact, colorectal cancer stood out. In about a third of the countries, it rose faster in younger adults than in older ones. "It is the one cancer that seems to be increasing in younger people whilst decreasing in older people," said Berrington de Gonzalez.

This has consequences for research. If cancers are rising in all age groups, the causes are likely to be widespread, so studies looking only at young people risk chasing complicated explanations with too little data to test them, she said.

A US study of pancreatic cancer in adults aged 15 to 39 years found the rise was almost entirely made up of early-stage tumors.

The authors said this suggested that instead of the number of cancer cases going up, it was the number of early diagnoses that had gone up. And that can be explained by better awareness in the community, with people getting checked more often.

But the data for colorectal cancer looks different: Some US studies show it rising in advanced stages of the disease as well, which suggests an increase in the number of cases, not just the number of diagnoses.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Obesity is an established cause of at least 13 cancers, according to the WHO's International Agency for Research on Cancer (IARC).

According to a 2021 review of existing studies, young adults with obesity were almost twice as likely to develop colorectal cancer before the age of 50.

Obesity has also spread fastest among the young. The WHO reports that obesity rates have doubled among adults since the 1990s and quadrupled among adolescents.

Some also say ultra-processed foods and microplastics cause cancer. But Gunter said the evidence was inconclusive.

"There's a lot of hype," Gunter said, adding that more research was needed. Berrington de Gonzalez agreed: "Diets have changed, but we don't yet know the role they might play."

Gunter said individuals should not be blamed for living in societies built around convenience foods and desk jobs, and that lasting change needed public health policy.

But he also said that young people could lower their cancer risk by maintaining a healthy weight, moving more, eating more fiber and cutting back on processed meat.

"Going for a walk, being more physically active, eating more fruits and vegetables," he said. "Those things are maybe not so trendy, but they are really important."

For a 30-year-old reading this, Berrington de Gonzalez's message was one of reassurance: Cancer at a young age remains relatively rare.

#800. How green is Europe's urban public transport? A data-driven reality check

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Greener buses and cleaner fleets are reshaping public transport across Europe. But are cities moving fast enough to meet their zero-emission goals?

An electric plug icon on their windows makes them easy to spot in cities like Brussels and Madrid. Quietly navigating the streets, electric buses have become an increasingly common sight on Europe's streets in recent years.

Michela Le Pira, assistant professor of transportation at Italy's University of Catania, says removing tailpipe emissions — the gases and particles generated when a combustion vehicle is running — might be "the easiest strategy to improve public transport."

Most Europeans live in urban settings, and road transport harms air quality. In 2023, almost 300,000 deaths across the EU were caused by air pollution levels exceeding the World Health Organization's (WHO) recommended thresholds.

Of all fine particulate matter (PM2.5) concentrations in European urban areas, about 14% come from road transport, including public, private and freight transport. Additionally, towns and cities account for nearly a quarter of the European Union's transport greenhouse gas emissions (GHG).

DW and the European Data Journalism Network evaluated more than a decade's worth of data from more than 100 reports and sources on public transit and the emissions it causes, and identified 148 sustainability commitments by local authorities and transport operators in 33 cities across Europe. The analysis focuses on motorized mass transit, leaving out bikes and pedestrians as well as taxis, shared vehicles and e-scooters.

The EU wants to become the first climate-neutral continent by 2050. A key part of that plan is cutting transport-related GHG emissions by 90% from 1990 levels.

As DW's analysis shows, Copenhagen is the only EU capital that has almost electrified its entire bus fleet — its vehicles do not have combustion engines and therefore do not produce tailpipe emissions. In cities like Rome, Lisbon and Nicosia, more than 90% of buses still run on diesel, and metros and trams account for less than 10% of their public transport fleet.

Although cars still dominate European streets, "electric buses are one way to reduce local air pollution," said Martin Baierl, data analyst with Transport & Environment, an advocacy non-profit for clean transport.

EU funds enabled many cities to increase their electric and alternative fuel bus fleets from 2021 on.

Electric buses comprise 20% of the total fleet in EU capitals and selected cities. Their number increased by 55% between 2021 and 2025 — equivalent to 1,234 more such vehicles on the road. The number of diesel-only buses has been declining since then, although they still outnumber those running on alternative fuel.

In addition to decarbonizing the bus fleet and helping to reduce air pollution, embracing electric models can help to lower noise and public transport operating costs. All the more so if the city produces its own electricity, explains Baierl. However, the outlay for e-buses is higher than for diesel models. In 20 out of the 31 cities with data to analyze, over 50% of the bus fleet is made up of traditional vehicles.

Brussels, for example, was an early adopter of moving away from traditional diesel-powered buses, switching to hybrids instead. Now the Belgian capital is in the process of electrifying its fleet, but at a slower pace. "It's not easy to know what the right technology will be in the end," Baierl said.

Another example is hydrogen-powered buses, which also produce zero emissions but are more costly to operate than their electric counterparts.

Transportation is the only major sector where greenhouse gas emissions remain above 1990 levels, despite the EU's ambition of a 90% reduction by 2050.

On average, public transport — including buses, trams and metros — is used for more than a quarter of trips in the 10 largest EU capitals, according to recent data from the non-profit group CDP (formerly the Carbon Disclosure Project). In Stockholm, the share is 49%, while in the smaller Slovenian capital of Ljubljana, it's 11%. DW analysis of transport sustainability pledges reveals that only four cities have explicitly committed to achieving zero emissions in buses or all public transport.

Yannis Papadimitriou lives 85 kilometers (about 53 miles) away from Athens, the EU capital with the highest air pollution mortality risk. He travels to work by commuter rail, a means of transport he'd like to see promoted more.

"For me it is a very conscious choice to use electric public transport, especially trains, which are the safest, most environmentally friendly and quickest way of transport."

Though trains, trams and metros run on electricity, there are more buses within city boundaries, and in many urban areas the majority still run on diesel.

In Northern Europe, 15 kilometers away from Copenhagen, Poul Kattler also used to commute to work. He is now retired and takes the bus more frequently with his grandchildren. Despite Copenhagen having an almost completely fossil-fuel-free public transport system (only one bus runs on biomethane), the metro expansion, which is responding to the city's growth, comes with its own set of concerns.

"The whole construction phase [of the new metro line] is extremely environmentally demanding," Kattler said. If the construction emissions are considered, "the CO2 balance would be very poor for the metro now."

But in the long run, the benefits of a new metro line in reducing CO2 emissions outweigh its construction-related emissions, Le Pira said.

Combustion or exhaust emissions are not the only way transport pollutes the air and the environment. Indirect emissions, like those coming from electricity generation or fuel production, add to transport's CO2 footprint. Brakes and wheels release non-exhaust particle matter when wagons or vehicles slow down.

Since 1992, buses and any vehicle running on fossil fuels in the EU have been subject to stricter emissions standards. Fine particle emissions from buses and heavy-duty vehicles had dropped by 92% in 2022 according to the European Environment Agency. Yet overall road transport non-exhaust emissions from tire wear have increased 58% in two decades, as cars have become heavier.

With a few exceptions, EU member states are not close to cutting greenhouse gas transport emissions by 90%, as envisioned by the EU's 2050 target.

Zero-emission public transport is one step toward decarbonizing transport in cities and meeting GHG reduction goals. The next challenge is to get people out of their cars.

"If we talk about sustainable mobility in general and not only clean public transport, the main problem is the modal shift from private cars to public transport," Le Pira said.

On average, private automobiles account for more than a third of transport in the largest EU capitals, according to CDP data.

Cheaper ticket prices for groups, like students, and disincentives for car use, such as low-emission zones, are two complementary ways of attracting more passengers while reducing private transport.

In her research work, Le Pira investigates how public transport can be more efficient and appealing for city dwellers, for example, by combining transport modes where people step off their bikes and onto a train. This kind of solution could be particularly relevant for small cities "where cars are still the main transport mode."

According to Baierl, places with good public transport draw people in. "Cities where not everybody has to use a car are just more livable," he said.

Edited by: Gianna-Carina Grün and Sarah Steffen

#642. Brazil's elections matter for the planet. This is why

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Brazil is heading back to the polls, but the impact could be felt worldwide. With the Amazon vital to the health of the planet, the election could help shape its future.

Few national elections carry as much environmental weight as Brazil's presidential race. And this one is being watched from far beyond the country's boundaries.

Home to the largest section of the Amazon rainforest, Brazil is guardian of a carbon sink of vital importance, making the country's conservation policies globally relevant.

Environmentalists have already voiced alarm at Flávio Bolsonaro — son of right-wing former president Jair Bolsonaro — coming out 2% ahead of incumbent Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva in the first round of elections.

"It's going to be a second coming of Bolsonaro, therefore a more radical Bolsonaro," said Claudio Angelo, international policy coordinator at Climate Observatory, a network of Brazilian NGOs.

"At the risk of sounding alarmist, I would say Lula's re-election is the only thing standing between us and the end of the Amazon."

The Amazon rainforest stores huge amounts of the carbon dioxide (CO2) released into the atmosphere from the burning of fossil fuels. As a result, it helps regulate the Earth's climate.

For decades, the tropical forest has acted as one of nature's most powerful defenses against climate change. But it has also suffered from widespread deforestation.

During Jair Bolsonaro's presidency, from 2019 until 2023, environmental agencies were weakened and enforcement efforts curtailed, meaning tropical forest was cleared at unprecedented rates.

"The environment agenda was disregarded and the level of control the government had on pollution overall, but specifically on deforestation, was reversed," said Claudio Forner, head of climate policy at Berlin-based think tank Climate Analytics.

Over Bolsonaro's four-year term, an estimated 45,586km2 of the Amazon was destroyed, an area bigger than the size of Denmark. And the forest is not as resilient as it once was, with scientists warning that parts of it are now emitting more carbon than they absorb.

Lula came to office with a message of protection for the Amazon. Besides calling for an end to deforestation by 2030, he increased monitoring of the forest and strengthened Brazil's environmental enforcement agency IBAMA.

Deforestation has plummeted under his leadership, with 2026 set to be its lowest level in a decade. That represents a 41% drop overall compared to Jair Bolsonaro's tenure.

Experts have already criticized Flávio Bolsonaro's record on forest protection. As a senator in 2019, he authored draft legislation that could have stark impacts for the Amazon. While landowners are currently required to preserve 80% of their property for the growth of native plants, the bill would allow them to use their holdings as they please.

"The signal from the president will say 'now you can deforest' or 'it's harder to deforest,'" said Ana Yang, director of the Environment and Society Center at London-based think tank Chatham House.

"The moment that you decrease enforcement capability, deforestation grows," she told DW.

Bolsonaro has also pledged to end illegal deforestation by 2029, a year before Lula. But Claudio Angelo is critical of that message.

"He will end illegal deforestation not by enforcing the law, but by making it legal," he said.

More generally, environmental experts fear Bolsonaro might dismantle many of the protections strengthened under the current president.

"Unfortunately, climate change has become a partisan issue, so rather than offering a solution from their perspective, the right pictures climate change as an invented problem...that regulation goes against economic growth or it goes against competitiveness, that it's the aim of the left to control the means of production," Forner said.

In an interview with Brazilian media in August, Bolsonaro said he regarded extreme weather events to be cyclical and did not believe they were solely linked to human influence.

A Bolsonaro win is likely to see Brazil move away from its position as a climate leader, a role the country embraced last year as it hosted the COP30 climate conference in the Amazonian city of Belem.

But even a Lula victory would not represent a straightforward climate success story, say experts. While the current administration has overseen significant reductions in deforestation, it has also backed plans to expand oil production.

Angelo said the decision to allow exploratory drilling was a "betrayal" of Lula's mandate but added that the president's overall record on climate had been "quite impressive".

"Lula is the world's worst climate leader, except for all the others," he told DW.

The current president will face a runoff election with Bolsonaro on October 25. But while experts say the outcome could determine the fate of the Amazon and the wider efforts to curb global warming, for many Brazilians climate is not high on their list of priorities.

"Ultimately what people care about is access to education, sanitation and health," said Yang. A road built through the Amazon to access those services, while criticized from outside Brazil for destroying parts of the rainforest, would be desirable to voters, she added.

"They don't have time to care. Not because they don't want to. They care about their own survival," she said.

Tax rules can be so bad that essentially noone invests. There is no culture of DIY investing or saving at all. Most investing is in houses.

France can be very good for Americans, except for inheritance rules.

#405. The autistic brain processes faces differently than scientists thought

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A major new study has uncovered important differences in how the brains of autistic children respond to human faces. Published in Nature Mental Health, the research is among the most extensive investigations yet into how face perception works in autism.

Some autistic people tend to look at faces less frequently or make less eye contact. They may also find it more difficult to remember faces or interpret facial expressions compared with neurotypical individuals. Scientists have spent decades investigating the brain activity behind these differences. In the early 2000s, for example, researchers at Yale reported that brain regions responsible for processing faces showed lower activity in autistic individuals than in people without autism.

James McPartland, PhD, Harris Professor in the Child Study Center and director of the Center for Brain and Mind Health, studies how autistic people process faces using electroencephalography (EEG), a technique that records the brain's electrical activity. Previous EEG studies, however, have typically examined signals from limited regions rather than analyzing activity across the entire scalp.

To gain a more complete picture, McPartland and his colleagues conducted a study involving approximately 400 autistic children, examining electrical signals recorded across the scalp. They discovered that brain activity associated with face recognition was less clearly distinguishable in autistic participants. They also identified a striking difference in development: In neurotypical children, the brain's response to faces became more specialized with age, while autistic children did not show the same pattern.

"This is the first time we've leveraged the full volume of very rich information provided by an EEG recording to understand face perception and autism," McPartland says.

How Scientists Discovered Differences in Autistic Brain Activity

McPartland compares traditional approaches to studying face recognition with attempting to understand traffic throughout an entire city by monitoring just one downtown street.

"That's the way most research on face processing so far has worked," McPartland says.

The new approach provides a much broader perspective, similar to observing traffic across an entire city using satellite imagery. To investigate these patterns, the researchers analyzed information collected through the Autism Biomarkers Consortium for Clinical Trials (ABC-CT). The dataset included autistic and neurotypical children across different age groups.

During the experiments, participants viewed pictures of faces and everyday objects while EEG equipment recorded their brain activity. Instead of concentrating on a small number of recording locations, the scientists examined electrical signals from all 128 electrodes positioned around each participant's scalp.

They then applied machine learning techniques to determine whether patterns in the recordings could reveal what each child was viewing.

The results showed that brain activity provided a clearer indication of face viewing in neurotypical children than in autistic children. In other words, the electrical signals associated with seeing faces were less distinguishable in the autistic group.

"The neural activity that we measure at the scalp is just not as distinct in autistic children," says first author Jason Griffin, PhD, assistant professor of psychology at University of Houston. Griffin previously worked as a postdoctoral researcher in McPartland's laboratory.

The researchers also found that the ability to identify what a child was viewing from brain activity improved with age among neurotypical participants. This age related improvement was not observed in autistic children, suggesting that their neural responses to faces develop along a different pattern.

"Their face-specific processing is not following the same trajectory," Griffin says.

Face Recognition Differences May Begin Earlier Than Previously Thought

Beyond identifying differences in brain activity, the study offers new insight into how early those differences emerge during face perception.

Recognizing someone involves multiple stages of neural processing. The sequence begins when visual information enters the eyes and continues as the brain interprets that information until a face is recognized.

Much of the earlier research on autism and face perception has concentrated on a brain signal called the N170. This measurable change in electrical activity typically occurs about 170 milliseconds after a person sees a face. Previous studies have shown that the N170 response tends to occur later in autistic individuals.

By analyzing brain activity over a wider period, the researchers found evidence that differences in face processing begin before this familiar signal appears. This suggests that autism related differences may arise at earlier stages of visual processing than researchers had previously established.

"But when you look at face processing through an approach that's more inclusive of the full temporal range of information, we're seeing that differences in autism occur even earlier," McPartland says.

What the Discovery Could Mean for Autism Diagnosis and Early Support

The findings could eventually influence how researchers approach autism diagnosis and the development of supportive interventions.

Historically, some clinicians attempted to address difficulties with face perception by requiring autistic individuals to maintain eye contact. Research has since shown that forcing eye contact is not an effective solution. A better understanding of how face processing develops in the brain could help identify approaches that are more useful and appropriate.

An ongoing challenge in autism research is separating neurological differences associated with autism itself from changes that may develop through the experience of living with the condition. According to the researchers, the patterns identified in this study suggest that differences in face processing are connected to underlying developmental characteristics of autism rather than simply reflecting accumulated life experiences.

"When we're seeing this expected refinement and specialization in non-autistic people, we're seeing a reduced developmental change in autistic people," McPartland explains.

Because the differences appear during development, the findings also raise the possibility that support aimed at helping autistic children interpret facial emotions could be particularly beneficial when introduced earlier in childhood.

However, the researchers emphasize that autism does not necessarily create difficulties that require intervention. Many neurodivergent people experience little or no impairment related to their autism and may neither need nor want additional support. For others, challenges with recognizing faces or understanding facial expressions can complicate everyday social interactions.

"Imagine navigating life when it's hard for you to perceive the information in another person's face, from navigating a playground to navigating a job interview," McPartland says. "This is one of the critical areas that causes difficulty for many autistic people."

New Brain Signals Could Help Improve Autism Diagnosis

Another potential application involves the search for more objective ways to assess autism. Currently, clinicians diagnose autism primarily by evaluating behavior because reliable biological tests are not yet available for routine diagnosis.

Scientists are investigating several possible biomarkers, which are measurable biological characteristics that could improve diagnostic assessments or help distinguish different groups within the autism spectrum.

The newly identified patterns of face related brain activity could contribute to that effort. By examining how these neural signals differ among individuals, researchers may eventually develop additional diagnostic tools or better identify autistic people who are likely to benefit from particular forms of support, including social skills therapy.

Although more research is necessary before these findings can be used in clinical practice, the study provides a new way to investigate how the autistic brain processes faces and how those responses change throughout childhood.

"This is a first step in introducing a new biomarker," McPartland says.

Materials provided by Yale School of Medicine. Original written by Isabella Backman. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#523. Beyond Bad Cholesterol: Scientists Uncover a Hidden Driver of Heart Disease

📅 Oct 28, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Protein patterns linked to TRL and Lp(a), two types of fat-carrying particles, suggest shared inflammatory pathways that could offer new targets for heart disease prevention.

Lowering “bad” cholesterol substantially reduces the risk of a heart attack, yet some people still develop coronary heart disease after treatment brings their LDL cholesterol down to a recommended level. Other particles that carry fats through the blood can contribute to the risk that remains. Understanding how they affect blood vessels could help researchers find more precise ways to prevent heart disease.

Coronary heart disease develops when cholesterol accumulates in the blood vessels supplying the heart and can lead to a heart attack if untreated. According to the World Health Organization (WHO), cardiovascular diseases kill nearly 17.9 million people worldwide each year, with heart attacks and strokes accounting for more than four out of five of those deaths.

Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have now identified blood protein patterns that offer clues to what happens beyond cholesterol buildup. Two types of fat-carrying particles, triglyceride-rich lipoproteins (TRL) and lipoprotein(a), or Lp(a), shared many protein patterns and signs of potentially harmful biological effects. The pattern associated with LDL, or low-density lipoprotein, was mainly linked to cholesterol accumulating in artery walls.

All three types of particles can deposit cholesterol in artery walls, and LDL is the most abundant. But abundance does not tell the whole story. A previous study by the same research group found that counting these harmful particles was the most effective way to predict future heart disease risk. It also showed that TRL and Lp(a) were more harmful per particle than LDL. Researchers believe these two types account for much of the remaining risk after LDL cholesterol reaches a healthy level.

“For this study, we wanted to understand what happens beyond the accumulation of cholesterol in artery walls, whether different fat-carrying particles can damage the blood vessels differently and why the remaining risk of heart disease still occurs,” says Jakub Morze, lead author and a postdoctoral fellow at Chalmers University of Technology.

The team used proteomics, the study of proteins, to examine how the three types of particles affect the body. Proteins carry out essential functions, and their levels can change with diet, stress, aging, or disease. Measuring them provides a snapshot of how the body is functioning at that moment. The researchers combined measurements of almost 3,000 blood proteins with genetic data and long-term health records to identify patterns that might help explain how the particles damage arteries.

The overlapping protein patterns associated with TRL and Lp(a) appeared to be linked to the later development of heart disease. These particles also contained proteins that attract immune cells, which could contribute to inflammation and changes in the structure of blood vessel walls. Although TRL and Lp(a) are different types of particles, the findings suggested that some of their harmful effects may involve shared biological pathways.

“The overlap for TRL and Lp(a) is one of the most interesting findings,” says Morze. “It suggests that these particles may affect some of the same inflammatory and vascular processes. This could help identify targets for more precisely directed prevention in the future.”

“Lowering LDL cholesterol remains a cornerstone of prevention,” says Clemens Wittenbecher, who led the study and is Assistant Professor of Precision Medicine and Diagnostics at Chalmers. “Our findings point to biological processes that may help explain some of the risk that remains beyond LDL. The next step is to establish whether measuring or targeting these processes can improve prevention alongside existing treatments.”

Reference: “Apolipoprotein B-containing lipoproteins: distinct roles in atherosclerosis revealed by plasma proteomic profiling” by Jakub Morze, Elias Björnson, Michael Y Mi, Martin Adiels, Giorgio E M Melloni, Gonçalo R da Silva, Andrzej Rynkiewicz, Nicholas A Marston, Marta Guasch-Ferré, Michael Y Tsai, Jerome I Rotter, Robert E Gerszten, Qi Sun, Frank B Hu, Rikard Landberg, Göran Bergström, Chris J Packard, Jan Borén and Clemens Wittenbecher, 31 August 2026, European Heart Journal. DOI: 10.1093/eurheartj/ehag718

This work was supported by the Royal Swedish Academy of Sciences (grant number ME2024-0009), the SciLifeLab & Wallenberg Data Driven Life Science Program (grant number KAW 2020.0239), the Swedish Research Council (grant number 2022-01529), and the Swedish Heart and Lung Foundation (grant number 20241115).

#532. Drinking Tea May Lower Your Risk of Vascular Disease – but There’s a Catch

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Not all tea-drinking habits were associated with the same health benefits. Researchers found that tea type, strength, and temperature were linked to notable differences in vascular disease risk.

People who regularly drank tea had a lower risk of diseases affecting the heart and blood vessels in a large study in China. However, the pattern varied with the type, strength, and temperature of the tea, with “burning hot” tea associated with elevated risk.

The study, published in the Journal of Internal Medicine, followed 485,934 adults for a median of 9.05 years. Researchers recorded 14,863 cases of cardiovascular disease, cerebrovascular disease, pulmonary heart disease, and peripheral artery disease. These conditions involve the heart and circulation, including blood vessels in the brain, heart problems related to lung disease, and reduced blood flow through arteries supplying the limbs. Regular tea consumption was associated with an 11% lower risk of these outcomes compared with not drinking tea.

Tea drinking was also considered alongside smoking and physical activity, providing context for how it fit into a broader pattern of habits.

“Joint analyses showed that tea consumption, smoking status, and physical were collectively associated with vascular risk, with the favorable combination of habitual tea consumption, non-current smoking and high physical activity associated with the lowest risk,” the authors wrote.

Among weekly tea drinkers, the lowest risk was estimated at approximately 6 to 8 cups per day. Black tea showed more consistent associations with lower vascular disease risk than green tea, while moderate-strength tea was associated with the lowest risk. These findings describe associations, rather than establishing that a particular amount or preparation of tea prevents disease.

The elevated risk associated with “burning hot” tea contrasts with the lower risk linked to regular tea consumption overall. Temperature therefore matters when interpreting the findings, but the reported results do not explain why very hot tea was associated with higher risk or establish that heat itself caused the increase.

Reference: “Habitual tea consumption is associated with lower cardiovascular disease risk” by Zichen Ye, Yawen Wang, Yimin Qu, Manman Chen, Jinming Fan, He Zhu, Keyu Han, Yuankai Zhao, Jiahui Wang, Qu Lu, Dianjianyi Sun, Canqing Yu, Jun Lv, Yu Guo, Yu Jiang, Liming Li and , 21 October 2026, Journal of Internal Medicine. DOI: 10.1111/joim.70172

#3896. When Suffering Is Confused for Strength

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

We’ve all heard the expression, “If you’re going through hell, keep going.” On the one hand, this phrase brilliantly encourages us to evolve through discomfort; to keep pushing through so we can come out stronger on the other side. From this perspective, we cannot grow if we don’t push ourselves out of our comfort zones and feel uncomfortable at times, mentally or physically.

On the other hand, this phrase can become an excuse to stay in a situation when conditions are poor. While we know pain equals gain at the gym, that is not always true in relationships, careers, friendships, finances, or other life circumstances. Discomfort doesn't always lead to evolution. Sometimes it's just needless suffering: pain that will not likely enlighten us just from the continued experience of it. Walking away from things, people, and places that could harm you or have harmed you doesn't make you a coward; it makes you the bravest version of yourself yet.

Letting go is not giving up. While these two experiences may have the same outcome, be it releasing a relationship, career, material possession, or otherwise, they have entirely different intentions.

Imagine still driving a broken-down car; the parts are rusted, the engine barely starts, the brakes are shot. You’ve put a lot of money and time into this vehicle, and while letting it go may even feel emotional, no one would judge you for it. In fact, you’ll be safer and go further in a different model. Giving up, in contrast, might look like trading in the car after you spill coffee on your seat.

When “going through hell” means ignoring safety risks and crossing your fingers, it might hurt you, or someone else. Likewise, choosing to stay in unhealthy life circumstances, which can range from irritating to life-threatening, will not help you evolve.

Suffering in what's familiar but unhealthy and calling it perseverance is an earnest but misguided perspective. One of the most difficult truths, here, is that all the time, money, and love we put into something or someone does not justify us holding on to it. The sunk cost fallacy does not deserve to run your life.

The strength you muster in your life will not come from the intensity of pain, but from your willpower to liberate yourself from it. Consider a time when you had no choice but to stay on your path, when circumstances were beyond your control. Here, resilience meant adapting to adversity and learning how to live in “hell.” Under these circumstances, you may have taken on a common adaptation to adversity, such as dissociation, people-pleasing, denial, self-blame, or toxic positivity.

Whether you’ve been used to denying the harsh realities of your life or blaming yourself for them, those adaptations can get in the way in situations where we do have a say. It is extremely important to acknowledge how adapting to adversity this way helped you at some point; maybe it even saved your life. Equally important is learning to trust that you, as an autonomous adult, have the power to save yourself now.

Releasing false justifications that once protected you from these harsh realities might be the kindest thing you ever do for yourself. This may feel uncomfortable, but it’s the sort of pain that leads to liberation, not stagnation.

Though hardship can foster perseverance, you don't deserve to live your whole life in survival mode. When we can change course, true resilience looks like accepting the reality of the painful situation we’re in so we can confidently step out of it.

Once we genuinely trust that we’re safe to do so, and that staying stuck is far scarier than changing course, resilience grows stronger with each step we take off the path that led us here. Then, we just might discover that bravely choosing a different path out of “hell” is the true roadmap to growth.

Ford, J. D. (2025). Dissociation and emotion dysregulation: New findings and nuances. Journal of Trauma & Dissociation, 26(2), 151–158. https://doi.org/10.1080/15299732.2025.2454865

Heinz, S. S., O’Brien, A. J., Walker, C., O’Sullivan, M., Rouse, P., Whitehead, J., Parsons, M., Cunningham, R., & Edmonds, M. (2025). Mediating pathways between resilience, Mental Health and Wellbeing: A scoping review of individual, social, and systemic factors. BMC Public Health, 25(1). https://doi.org/10.1186/s12889-025-24897-1

Romano, R. (2026). Trauma moralization in victimization fields: shame, self-blame, and victim blaming. Phenomena Journal - International Journal of Psychopathology, Neuroscience and Psychotherapy, 8(2), 49–55.

#541. A Routine Blood Test Could Predict Dementia Risk 15 Years in Advance

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A large study tracking more than 316,000 adults for up to 15 years suggests that a routine blood test may reveal important clues about future dementia risk.

A standard blood test taken during a routine health check could offer clues about dementia risk more than a decade later. In a study of more than 316,000 adults, researchers found that people with unusually low or high levels of hemoglobin were more likely to develop dementia, although the risks varied depending on the type of disease.

Hemoglobin is the oxygen-carrying protein in red blood cells. Low levels are commonly associated with anemia, while unusually high levels can result from smoking, dehydration, or certain heart and lung conditions. The findings suggest that this familiar blood measurement may also reflect broader aspects of physical health associated with brain aging.

Researchers analyzed health records from 316,542 Korean adults aged 40 and older who underwent national health screenings in 2004 and 2005. Participants were divided into five groups based on their hemoglobin levels, from the lowest 20% to the highest 20%. National health insurance records were then used to track new dementia diagnoses through the end of 2019.

Compared with the group that had the lowest dementia rate, participants in the lowest hemoglobin category had a 16% higher risk of developing dementia from any cause. Those in the highest category had a 7% greater risk.

The findings, published in the Journal of Alzheimer’s Disease and presented at the 39th ECNP Congress in Munich, also identified differences between Alzheimer’s disease and vascular dementia.

For Alzheimer’s disease, the increased risk was concentrated among people with the lowest hemoglobin levels. Participants in this group were 14% more likely to develop the disease than those in the reference group.

The relationship was different for vascular dementia (caused by reduced blood flow to the brain). People in the lowest hemoglobin category had a 19% higher risk, while those in the highest category had an 18% higher risk. However, the association did not follow a consistent pattern across all five hemoglobin groups. The researchers also observed clearer and more consistent relationships among women than men.

Lead researcher Dr. Hyun Soo Kim, an assistant professor at Dong-A University College of Medicine in Busan, South Korea, emphasized that the results do not establish a direct causal relationship.

“The findings do not show that hemoglobin itself causes dementia, but suggest that abnormal hemoglobin levels may be a visible marker of wider physical vulnerability relevant to brain health.”

Kim added, “Dementia is one of the fastest-growing health challenges in aging societies, and there are not many treatments and no cure. Identifying simple, accessible markers of future risk could help researchers and clinicians better understand how whole-body health relates to brain health over time.”

Hemoglobin is already measured in standard blood tests around the world, making it considerably more accessible than specialized brain imaging or newer dementia biomarkers. The researchers suggest that existing blood test results could potentially help scientists investigate connections between general physical health and long-term cognitive decline.

Kim explained, “A blood test most people already get at a routine check-up may say something about their brain health many years later. In this large Korean cohort, people with unusually low hemoglobin, and, to a smaller degree, those with unusually high hemoglobin, were more likely to develop dementia. This does not prove that hemoglobin causes dementia, but it supports the idea that general physical health, including the body’s ability to carry oxygen, matters for long-term brain health.”

Because the study was observational, it cannot determine whether abnormal hemoglobin levels contribute directly to dementia. Other medical conditions could influence both hemoglobin measurements and brain health. Early dementia processes might also affect nutrition, body weight, frailty, or anemia before the disease is formally diagnosed.

“We want to emphasize that the findings should not be interpreted as advice to take iron supplements, to raise hemoglobin levels, or to lower high hemoglobin levels in order to prevent dementia. An abnormal hemoglobin result should instead be discussed with a doctor, because it may point to underlying health issues that deserve attention,” Kim said.

The study drew on health records from South Korea, leaving questions about whether the same relationships apply to people from other ethnic, geographic, and medical backgrounds. Further research is also needed to establish whether identifying abnormal hemoglobin levels can meaningfully improve dementia risk assessment or prevention.

Dr. Lindsey Sinclair (University of Southampton), who was not involved in the research, commented:

“There is a growing body of evidence that physical health and brain health are intertwined. We are also increasingly aware that changes supporting better brain health, be they medical or lifestyle changes, are important in mid-life to reduce the risk of later dementia. While this study shows a possible link between hemoglobin levels and dementia risk, more work is required to understand if this link is present in people from a wider range of backgrounds; and what can best be done to reduce dementia risk in people with the lowest and highest levels of hemoglobin.”

#3926. Why Pregnancy Loss After Infertility Is More Than One Loss

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Kaja Perina

When you get a positive test after years of trying to get pregnant, the joy is usually accompanied by some fear. After so long, your efforts finally worked—and now there’s something that could be taken away.

If that pregnancy, in turn, ends in a loss, there’s a distinct type of grief along with the disappointment and sadness, as well as a new kind of fear that compounds on top of the initial fear. This compounded fear comes from knowing now how painful it is, both physically and emotionally, to lose a pregnancy. This loss is layered because you are now grieving more than one loss.

If you've been struggling with infertility, getting pregnant is a relief. It finally worked, and you know your body can actually get pregnant. Losing that pregnancy can feel like having no say over what happens, with no guarantee that next time will be different.

Beyond proof your body can get pregnant, it can feel like the hard part is over. It may be the first time in a long time that you've let yourself plan.

If you've been in treatment, there’s often a moment of graduating from the fertility clinic to regular pregnancy care. After a loss, you go back to the clinic, the waiting room and the protocols, and it can feel like starting over from zero.

You may have followed every instruction this time, and it still ended, which can make the loss of control feel even greater.

When we think of grief, it is basically a response to loss. A loss can be tangible, emotional, or even imagined.

A pregnancy loss after infertility is complex because it involves losses at all these levels.

There's the loss of that reproductive story we imagined. Clients usually describe this timeline loss as a disruption in the order of things, at least the order many of us expect.

Then we have losses along the way: the loss of control, identity, belonging, hope, innocence, and the loss of planning.

Infertility and pregnancy loss also involve grieving a future. What makes this grief different is that it’s for something that never happened, something you wanted but never had.

When pregnancy finally happens, especially after fertility treatments, we get to see the light at the end of the tunnel. Unfortunately, 1 in 4 pregnancies end in a loss, which can make that moment very brief (Koert et al., 2023).

A loss can also take away the joy of celebrating when a pregnancy happens again. In session, this is often described as losing the innocence of getting pregnant before knowing that it can be lost.

After one pregnancy loss, the brain learns that loss is very much possible. From then on, it holds both possibilities: a pregnancy that could continue and one that ends in loss. Since this loss can be significant and traumatic, it is now focused on the negative possibilities mostly to keep you safe from future suffering.

When the next pregnancy happens, there's no more of that carefree joy of planning the nursery, choosing baby names, and picking out cribs and newborn onesies. You are now experiencing the mature side of pregnancy after loss, and with the maturity comes the bracing for negative results. Couples who became pregnant again after a loss have described this as never being “pregnant in the same way again” (Koert et al., 2023). Clinicians call this anticipatory grief, when part of you starts grieving a loss before it happens.

Pregnancy after loss brings its own fears, especially after infertility and fertility treatments. Part of what makes it so hard is trying to hold on to hope that this time could end well. I wrote more about this in an earlier post, “The Trauma of Repeated Hope in Infertility.”

Having gone through fertility treatments brings on even more losses, such as loss of a cycle, the money spent, and sometimes the last embryo.

There’s also the time. Each cycle takes weeks of appointments and waiting, and after a loss there’s often another wait before you can try again, while you’re aware of every month that passes.

In a recent study, women going through fertility treatment described the process as an “extended present,” where time moves back and forth between hope and uncertainty (Karaca Biçakçı et al., 2026). They also talked about their bodies becoming the focus of medical attention, and about the pressure they felt from the people around them. The researchers described infertility as a “biographical disruption,” something that changes how we see ourselves and where we fit in with others.

This study looked at women in treatment, and I see the same thing happen after a loss. When a pregnancy ends after fertility treatment, it can pull you right back into that extended present, just when the end finally seemed in sight. And this time, hope can feel harder to reach.

Once you’ve lost a pregnancy after infertility, loss is no longer something that only happens to other people.

It’s understandable to want control over what happens next, especially after everything you’ve done to get here. A lot of the pain comes from the struggle itself, from trying to make sure it never happens again when so much of it was never in your hands.

In therapy, a big part of the work after a loss is letting go of that struggle to be in control. Of course, this is hard to do when so much of trying to conceive has already been about losing control.

When the struggle starts to ease, it often becomes a little easier to hope again, even with the fear still there.

Karaca Biçakçı, N., Çalmaz, A, & Altay, B. (2026). Temporal meaning-making of women’s emotional experiences during the infertility treatment process: A qualitative study. International Journal of Qualitative Studies on Health and Well-being. https://doi.org/10.1080/17482631.2026.2672214

Koert, E., Hartwig, T. S., Hviid Malling, G. M., Schmidt, L., & Nielsen, H. S. (2023). ‘You’re never pregnant in the same way again’: Prior early pregnancy loss influences need for health care and support in subsequent pregnancy. Human Reproduction Open, 2023(3), hoad032. https://doi.org/10.1093/hropen/hoad032
