#425. How Apple Built Watch Series 12 to Track Heart Rate 24/7—Without Nuking Battery Life

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The newest models take measurements every five seconds, thanks to brighter LEDs and redesigned sensors.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

When Apple announced its new lineup of smartwatches—Apple Watch 12 and Apple Watch Ultra 4—in September, one of the biggest highlights was that the devices are now capable of keeping tabs on your pulse roughly every five seconds around the clock, with even greater accuracy than before. This is a 60 times the improvement over the previous models. To do so, Apple had to create larger, brighter green LEDs on the back side of the watch. Those same LEDs also allow the device to check in on your heart rate variability (HRV), an indicator of stress and recovery, 24 times as often, throughout the day.

The biggest question we had following the announcement: How does this not nuke the battery on a device that’s already criticized for needing daily fill-ups?

We spoke with the team at Apple that was tasked with not only hardware modifications but also algorithmic advancements that allow the Watch to take your HR more often while being more power efficient.

Apple also claims that the new Health Sensing System results in the most accurate heart rate tracking of any wearable—and went so far as to distribute the findings of its own study on the matter.

So, does it work? And how well? Let’s dive in.

In a nutshell, all wrist-based optical heart rate monitors work in roughly the same fashion: LEDs shine light into your skin, your blood absorbs some of that light, and photodiodes read the amount of light that gets reflected back. From there, the watch can determine your heart rate and HRV.

There are some complications to overcome, however. The physical real estate on the backside of a watch is limited. And there’s a ton of movement that happens at your wrist, which makes accurate readings challenging—anything that requires a strong grip, like strength training, creates significant interference.

For the new watches, Apple looked to maximize the use of that sensor space. If you look at the backside of the older models of Apple Watch, you’ll see eight small dots in a circle at the center of the unit. Four of them shine a combination of green, red, and infrared light. The other four are sensors to capture the reflected light. Those are tiny in comparison to what’s been rolled out on AW12 and AWU4.

On the Series 12, Apple separated the LEDs—four exclusively emit green light, because that works best in challenging daytime conditions and workout scenarios. The other four combine red and infrared, and activate when you’re largely motionless or sleeping—times when those are adequate. They’ll be less bothersome while you sleep, and the red, in particular, is needed to measure blood oxygen levels.

Those larger and brighter LEDs, though, take up the space where the old photodiode receptors were set, so Apple replaced them with a ring of eight trapezoid-shaped photodiodes arranged in a ring surrounding the LEDs.

The larger sensors improve the readings, but only if the bright light shining out of the LEDs can be properly isolated. To prevent any light leakage, Apple had to re-engineer the crystal covering the sensors. That casing is not there just to keep out dirt and water, but it’s set up so light can be directed at your skin, yet shielded from the sensors.

“What's really different here, we took red and white zirconia and did a couple processes with a lot of heat and pressure,” said Eugene Kim, vice president of hardware engineering at Apple. “It's a very unique singular piece of zirconia that has specific opaque and clear areas—black and clear—which really helps us isolate the light source from the receiving source, because you only want to see the light off your blood, not just random stray light that might be emitting from the LEDs.”

Apple used a new process to create those black and clear sections of glass. Previously, the housing was made up of multiple pieces of crystal that were essentially glued together. Now, it’s created using a “double-shot” process where the red zirconia is molded first for the areas that will end up being dark. Then a second injection mold of the white ultimately becomes the clear sections. After it goes through the heat and pressure processes, it’s polished to give the single, shiny glass cover you see on the back of the Watch.

One knock on Apple Watch versus sport-specific GPS devices is its relatively short battery life. Compared to many other smartwatches, Apple Watches have historically needed to be charged every day. Devices from Garmin and Coros, on the other hand, can last days or weeks—you charge them so infrequently you might forget where you placed the charging cable. The battery life of an Apple Watch Ultra, say, has been extended, but you’re still putting it on the magnetic puck every other day at least. So, Apple knew it couldn’t reduce those run times, even though it wanted to improve the HR sensing capabilities.

“The name of the game there is just efficiency,” said Kim. “We really wanted to improve our performance by improving the efficiency of the system. And I mean that in very broad terms. It's physical packaging, it's energy usage, it's computation.”

Apple didn’t plan to just pack in a bigger battery, but to ensure that everything required less power to operate.

“We actually took our work on the AirPods heart rate,” Kim continued, “where that is also a very power-constrained product, and we took the [machine learning] approaches there and actually brought that to the Watch to increase efficiency of the algorithms, to not only improve performance but also use less power.”

Steve Waydo, Director of Health Sensing, explained that the biggest impact is the improved efficiency. “For workouts, for example, we expect similar heart rate performance for that five-second stream, but we're achieving that performance with a lot less power. So that can translate into longer workout durations or just a smaller overall impact on your battery life.”

The efficiency improvements also mean bigger changes to the rest of the day. Previously, HR features were based on infrared light largely because they required less power. But, with the more efficient LEDs and sensor, and the ability to provide the data with a lower power cost, Apple is able to fire up the green lights all day without overly taxing the battery.

I have been fairly impressed with the battery on the AWU4. One of my first runs with it was a 10-miler that included five fast intervals each one mile long. After 75 minutes of running, I only drained 6 percent of the battery. In the first mile, I realized that I messed up the transfer of my cellular data plan from my previous Apple Watch, so I couldn’t fire up music to power me through that workout—which would certainly have delivered a bigger drain on the battery. (I got it all sorted later that day.)

As a competitive distance runner who follows a structured training plan, I use my watch to keep tabs on my sleep, too. I want to know not only how well I slept but also how well I’ve recovered from the workouts of the previous day or two. Tracking overnight HRV stats can give me insight into that, but can also be factored into a Readiness score (1 to 10) that Apple surfaces in the Health app. The score is an easy way for me to know whether I’m recovered enough to tackle another speed session on the track, or if I should pull back and go for an easy jog—or take a rest day completely. (To be fair, most other watchmakers offer a similar metric.)

But even non-marathoners and athletes can benefit from the overnight data as well as the new all-day HRV readings. Dr. Asha Chesnutt, a member of Apple’s clinical team, explained why that matters.

“As a clinician, obviously, continuous heart rate monitoring is part of the hospital, the ICU where I come from,” she said. “For some users, they really just like to be able to connect the dots—like not drinking enough water corresponds to increasing your heart rate, or drinking that extra cup of coffee and making you jittery might increase your heart rate. For me as a clinician, I think that that's pretty obvious, but to a lot of people it's not. Even before you physically have a fever and get sick, you might see some subtle changes in your heart rate and HRV. I think it helps people become more in tune with their bodies.”

As I noted above, Apple shared its own findings that suggest the new Watch Series 12 and Watch Ultra 4, using the new Health Sensing System and the S11 chip, have the most accurate heart rate measurements of any wearable. It compared the new watches to the Garmin Forerunner 970, Google Pixel Watch 4, Huawei Watch 5, Samsung Galaxy Watch 8, Whoop 5, and Oura Ring 5.

I didn’t simply want to take the brand’s word for it, though. I’ve used the Watch Ultra 4 to track more than 200 miles of runs over the past month, and have been looking at how the data recorded compares to a Garmin Fenix 9 Pro Solar and Forerunner 970, as well as heart rate captured with a Coros armband monitor and Garmin HRM600 chest strap.

If I want the most accurate heart rate data, say for a session of fast intervals where my HR rises and falls quickly, I still reach for the chest strap. Nothing I’ve seen from any optical sensor comes close to its accuracy. AFF of athletes get chafing or irritation from a chest strap—and you obviously won’t wear one casually all day.

By taking a closer look at graphs of my heart rate from the various devices during workouts, I’ve seen the Apple Watch Ultra 4 mirrors the strap remarkably closely—with one quirk.

While Apple says that the Watch measures heart rate every five seconds, it’s not truly every five seconds exactly. In my data files, I see gaps—sometimes a minute or longer—where there is no recorded value. I asked Apple’s team why that happens, when other devices display measurements up to every second.“We took a strong stance, really from the early days of the first Watch, that if we're going to claim to be doing a measurement—if we're claiming to measure heart rate—we need to have the data coming from the sensor to back that up,” said Waydo. “Any optical measurement at the wrist—a hardware device interacting with physiology—the signals are not perfect all the time. If we don't have real data to support the measurement that we're going to present, we'll choose to withhold that measurement rather than present something that we aren't really sure is the right answer.”

When I asked what goes into that “confidence check” to determine whether or not to present a reading, he explained the following:

“The algorithms are a large machine learning–driven neural network that takes in the optical signals from the sensor as well as the motion data from the accelerometer. And inside the system, it outputs two things: It outputs, ‘here's what I think the heart rate is,’ and ‘here's how sure I am about that.’ And we train the, ‘here's how sure I am’ component based on data from thousands of people, where we're looking at the ability of the system to accurately estimate the heart rate on their signals.”

When that’s combined, the algorithm might have low confidence even if the reading is correct—so it leaves a blank gap. What that means is the data that is actually reported has a stronger likelihood of being the true measurement, compared to devices that just give you a number regardless.

The times I have found the watch to struggle the most were at the beginning of runs, or at the start and end of speed intervals. In the case of the beginning of runs, I’d see a flat line on my heart rate graph, but then the Watch’s data would snap up to match what my chest strap was reading nearly dead-on.

In the case of intervals, I think what happens is that I mark the beginning and end of my efforts by pushing the Action button on the side of the Watch to record a segment. In doing so, I may be jostling the Watch just enough to create a gap between the sensor and my skin, destroying the algorithm’s confidence in what the sensor is seeing.

In the end, the changes that Apple rolled out with these devices do move the needle to make wrist-based heart rate monitoring more useful and reliable. The accuracy, even if not as spot-on as a chest strap, proves to be good enough that most of my running is done without a chest strap—and I’m confident that I’m pushing at the right effort level.

And the efficiency improvements give you better access to your heart rate all day. The usefulness of that insight right now might not be apparent, but that data will continue to be rolled into features and apps for even more meaningful actions. It’s currently being used within the Heart Rate app, where you can see a graph of your last two minutes as well as your resting HR and even your walking HR average.

Best of all, Apple managed to make these improvements without any hit to the Watches’ battery life.

Jeff is Runner-in-Chief for Runner’s World and the director of product testing. He has tested and reviewed running shoes, GPS watches, headphones, apparel, and more for nearly two decades. He regularly tests more than 100 pairs of shoes each year, and once had a 257-day streak running in different models. Jeff can usually be found on the roads, racing anything from the mile to a marathon, but he also enjoys racing up mountains and on snowshoes. When he’s not running, you’ll probably find him hanging from a ladder making repairs and renovations to his house (he’s also director of product testing for Popular Mechanics).

#885. Boxers, Briefs, or Boxer Briefs? Let’s Settle This Once and for All

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

For many years, a great debate raged: boxers vs. briefs. Men were divided. Allegiances forged. Some preferred the boxer for its roomy comfort; others, the brief for its sleek profile and ability to gather and contain the important parts. Then came the dark times. What had once been a good-faith debate devolved into name-calling, mockery, and shoddy science. Both sides entrenched themselves, blinded by their own sense of righteousness.

Then, a savior arrived: the boxer brief. Offering the best of both worlds, it combined the coverage and comfort of a boxer with the containment and discretion of a brief, without any of the tightie-whitie stigma. At last, there was peace.

Nowadays, though, decades into the boxer-brief strife, men have begun to rediscover the pleasures of the old order. As ever, there’s a strong case for boxers, briefs, and boxer briefs, but in this age of unprecedented menswear latitude, they might not be what you think. Here’s why we think you should keep all three main underwear variants in your drawer, and when to reach for each.

According to data from a 2016 fertility study of American men, 39% wore boxer briefs during the day, 35% wore boxers, and 26% wore briefs. Boxers gained ground at night, however, with 43% of men wearing them to sleep, compared with 24% in boxer briefs and 13% in briefs. Another 20% wore nothing at all. The study’s real purpose, though, was to better understand whether underwear choice affected male fertility, and when researchers looked at semen quality and how long couples took to conceive, they found no real link between underwear type and fertility.

Dr. Michael Eisenberg, a urology professor at Stanford University, co-authored the 2016 study. He says the question of boxers versus briefs comes up when he counsels men about fertility, largely because the testicles need to remain cooler than the rest of the body to function properly. “When we’ve actually studied it, the particular type of underwear tends not to be predictive of semen quality,” he confirms. “It’s unlikely any single layer is going to make that big of a difference.”

In other words, from a health perspective, there’s really no reason to choose one over the other. “Just wear what’s comfortable,” says Eisenberg.

Like Calvin pulling on his lucky rocketship-print briefs before a big day of sledding with Hobbes, it’s less about what is right or wrong and more about what fits the occasion and outfit. That’s why the person with access to all three types of underpants in their top drawer will likely be the most comfortable and outfit-appropriate. “It really depends on the pant,” says Daniel Salmon, a Brooklyn-based men’s personal stylist and design studio instructor at Parsons School of Design. “If you’re wearing a slim or fitted pant, you don’t want a pair of baggy boxer shorts stuffing extra fabric in there,” he says. “It’s definitely going to show up.”

For slimmer trousers, particularly those made from lightweight fabrics like tropical wool, Salmon recommends briefs or boxer briefs. The latter help maintain a clean silhouette, since traditional briefs can show through fitted layers. But with trousers now wider and waistbands hanging lower, could old-school boxers be poised for a comeback? Salmon points to recent looks from Haider Ackermann at Tom Ford, where boxers peeked above the waistbands of relaxed trousers. “It just adds that little bit of a styling element,” he says. “Sometimes it can allude to what’s underneath, if you want to be a little provocative.”

Of course there are practical considerations, too. “If you’re wearing really short shorts, you need to be contained,” he says. “I would say no to a boxer short.” For bigger guys, he suggests avoiding the extra bulk of loose boxers while still paying attention to fit, regardless of style. Too tight and you’re spilling over the waistband. Too loose and you’re dealing with bunching and sagging.

Ultimately though, Salmon echoes Eisenberg’s advice about personal comfort. What fits depends on your body, your clothes, and even your mood. “It varies on how you’re feeling, what you’re wearing,” he says. In other words, why limit yourself to one when you can say yes to all three? What a time to be alive.

#391. NASA Invites Media to Crew Completion of Yearlong Mars Simulation

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The four volunteers who lived and worked in a simulated NASA Mars mission for more than a year will exit their habitat on Saturday, Oct. 31, after 378 days of isolation in a facility at the agency’s Johnson Space Center in Houston. Health and performance data collected from the simulation can help NASA develop methods to counter human spaceflight risks for future Artemis and Moon Base missions.

Beginning at 3 p.m. EDT, NASA will provide live coverage of the volunteers’ departure from the Crew Health and Performance Exploration Analog (CHAPEA, pronounced cha-pea’-ah) mission habitat. The exit ceremony will be streamed on NASA Johnson’s YouTube and social accounts.

Johnson Center Director Vanessa Wyche and NASA astronaut Jack Hathaway will provide remarks. The CHAPEA crew members Ross Elder, Ellen Ellis, James Spicer, and Matthew Montgomery also will provide remarks.

Media are invited to watch the CHAPEA Mission 2 ceremony live at NASA Johnson on a screen outside the hangar holding the CHAPEA facility, after which a subject matter expert will be available in person to answer questions. Media will have the opportunity to capture the moment the crew exits the building, stepping outside for the first time in more than a year.

U.S. media interested in attending the activities in person, or media interested in speaking virtually with the CHAPEA Mission 2 crew members in the days following completion of their mission, must contact the NASA Johnson newsroom no later than 2 p.m., Friday, Oct. 30, at jsc-newsroom@mail.nasa.gov.

The second CHAPEA mission began on Oct. 19, 2025. Four crew members have lived and worked inside a 3D-printed 1,700-square-foot facility, simulating Mars mission operations, conducting simulated spacewalks, exercising, performing habitat maintenance, growing plants, and more, while operating under stressors that future deep space crews will likely experience. These included communication delays, resource limitations, and isolation.

Data from the mission can aid preparation for NASA’s lunar missions by optimizing food system design, efficient use of crew time, bandwidth on NASA’s Deep Space Network, and other aspects of living in deep space. In addition, data can help flight doctors and psychologists strengthen spaceflight medical care and behavioral support practices to ensure crew readiness and performance.

To learn more about NASA’s CHAPEA mission, visit:

https://www.nasa.gov/humans-in-space/chapea

#557. The Ocean’s Mysterious “Snow” Could Be Hiding a Powerful Climate Secret

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A surprising property of the tiny particles sinking through the ocean could help determine how much carbon is stored in the deep sea. Scientists have discovered that the stickiness of marine snow affects how easily bacteria can break it down, potentially changing how much carbon reaches the ocean depths.

Researchers at UC San Diego’s Scripps Institution of Oceanography and the University of Lincoln found that particularly gooey marine snow resists bacterial degradation. Their findings, published October 8 in Science, suggest that highly viscous particles can transport more than six times as much carbon to the deep ocean as less viscous particles.

Marine snow consists of tiny clumps of dead plankton, waste, mucus, minerals, and other debris that continuously descend through the water column. As these particles sink, they carry carbon from the sunlit surface ocean toward deeper waters, where some of it can remain stored for hundreds or thousands of years.

Scientists estimate that marine snow transports more than 10 gigatons (11 billion U.S. tons) of carbon away from the surface each year. However, only about 2 gigatons (2.2 billion U.S. tons) reach depths where the carbon can remain stored for extended periods. Much of the sinking material is broken down or recycled before reaching those depths.

The new research identifies particle viscosity, a measure of resistance to flow, as an important factor in this process. Marine snow particles vary in composition and consistency, and those differences influence how bacteria colonize and consume them.

Understanding these interactions required a technique that had previously been unavailable: directly observing individual living bacteria as they navigate the microscopic structure of mucus.

“Observing individual bacteria interacting with the physical structure of mucus had never been possible,” said Bryce Inman, who began investigating the problem in 2021 while working as a postdoctoral scholar at Scripps Oceanography.

After four years of unsuccessful attempts, Inman and his colleagues, including Scripps microbiologist and professor emeritus Farooq Azam, developed a working approach in 2025. By combining 3D confocal microscopy with specialized fluorescent molecules, they could map the intricate physical structure of marine snow and track how bacteria interacted with it. The imaging technique is described in a companion study published in Nature Communications.

The images showed that bacteria colonize less viscous regions near the surface of marine snow particles more easily than the thicker, more resistant material inside. These internal regions present a physical barrier that bacteria must gradually break down before penetrating farther into the particle.

“Bacteria can latch on to less viscous portions of each particle, but cannot reach more viscous regions until they break it down,” Inman explained. “Think of it like a worm in an apple: the flesh is easy to penetrate, but the core is tough to chew through”

Laboratory experiments confirmed that more viscous marine snow takes longer for bacteria to degrade and fragment. Computer simulations further suggested that these resistant particles can sink to greater depths before breaking apart. Together, the findings indicate that viscosity can substantially influence how much carbon escapes recycling in shallower waters and reaches the deep ocean.

The newly developed imaging method could also help researchers investigate interactions between bacteria and mucus in other environments. Similar relationships occur in coral reefs, kelp forests, plant root systems, and the digestive tracts of humans and other animals.

“Mucus, slime, whatever you want to call it, has a surprising amount of structure,” Inman said. “There are bulges, crevasses, all this sort of structure to it that we really had no idea about at a scale smaller than bacteria. This is the first time we’re really examining this physical habitat.”

By making these microscopic environments visible, the technique could support research into how bacteria colonize mucus, interact with its physical structure, and influence processes ranging from ocean carbon storage to coral reef recovery and human health.

“Marine snow viscosity regulates microbial degradation and the ocean carbon sink” by Bryce G. Inman, Stuart Humphries and Farooq Azam, 8 October 2026, Science. DOI: 10.1126/science.adx4170

“Molecular rotors reveal the 3D viscous habitat of mucus-colonizing bacteria” by Bryce G. Inman, Nirav Patel, Gabriel Castro-Falcón, Taylor Hernandez, Ryan J. McGorty, Aki H. Ohdera, Logan M. Gonzalez, Kay D. Bidle, Farooq Azam and Stuart Humphries, 22 July 2026, Nature Communications. DOI: 10.1038/s41467-026-75892-y

#4537. TikToker FunBucketKustoms says he briefly died & “can’t walk anymore” after head-on moped crash

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

TikToker FunBucketKustoms has revealed that he “can’t walk anymore” after suffering multiple broken bones in a serious moped accident.

The content creator, known for his automotive videos and humorous personality, shared the news with followers after spending approximately two weeks in the hospital, explaining that he suffered extensive injuries to his left leg and pelvis.

The accident occurred on October 10, 2026, when FunBucket says another driver entered his lane and struck him head-on. He has since begun documenting his recovery, with hopes of eventually walking again.

In his initial video addressing the situation, FunBucketKustoms appeared in a wheelchair and explained that his injuries had left him unable to walk.

“I can’t walk, I can’t stand, and I can’t run away from you because my legs don’t work no more,” he said.

He explained that his left leg had been severely injured, with multiple fractures throughout his leg and pelvis.

“My left leg was completely destroyed. It was broken in like 15 places, and my pelvis was all broken in like 15 places,” he revealed.

The TikToker also claimed that he briefly died following the accident and had to be resuscitated by medical personnel.

“They had to shock me with the thingies and add blood to keep me alive,” he said. “I, I was not alive for a couple minutes.”

“I’m lucky to be alive right now. And I thank god that I am alive.”

FunBucketKustoms went on to explain that he had been discharged from the hospital after approximately two weeks, claiming that his lack of health insurance played a role in his release.

#3302. Runna’s Training Plan Felt Too Aggressive. Here’s How I Adjusted It—and Ran My Fastest Half Marathon

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

How to make a training app truly work for your fitness level, goals, and recovery needs so you avoid injury.

When I spotted an 800-meter repeat workout and progression run scheduled for the first week of my Runna training plan, I immediately tried to figure out a way to get out of it. I wanted to get faster, but gradually, and seeing those two sessions on my plan sent off alarm bells inside my head.

I signed up for Runna—a personalized training app—with a long list of to-dos. Nailing down a solid routine with a mix of speedwork, strength training, and mobility was top priority.

As a newly RRCA-certified run coach, I had an idea of how my next training plan would look: A steady progression of mileage and intensity—one that kicks off with easy runs in the first week, followed by short intervals or strides the next, and then getting into more structured workouts. The Runna plan didn’t mimic anything I was currently doing at the time, so I was skeptical it was aligned with my fitness level.

But I didn’t want to give up on following a plan in the app, so I went through the setup process several more times, until I finally got a program I felt comfortable with following.

Finding the right training plan that aligns with your fitness level and goals isn’t always easy. If you’re a runner who’s looking for a more tailored approach, apps like Runna can fill the gap, especially if training with a coach is out of your reach. The key, though? Knowing how to navigate the app for smarter training, so you avoid taking on too much too soon, while also building consistency and progress that allows you to perform your best on race day.

I successfully used Runna to train for the Copenhagen Half Marathon, cutting more than 10 minutes off my finish time. Here’s exactly what I did to make the plan work for me and my fitness level.

Before you start training with Runna, you’re asked a series of questions about your current fitness and mileage, injury history, and race history. This helps the app generate the best training plan for you based on your answers.

It’s important you respond to these questions honestly, and not based on what you think you can do, says Stephanie Kessell, elite marathoner and head coach at Runna. You can always tweak your training plan, dialing things up or down based on how your training is going afterward.

I told the app I would like to run three days a week, and I used my second half marathon time of 2:48:54 to estimate my finish time. (I completed that race about a month before I started training with Runna.)

Based on that information and a few other answers, the app outlined my 14-week training plan and predicted I could finish my next race anywhere between 2:25 and 2:35. But I took the questionnaire several more times, each time filling in slightly different answers, hoping I could push off the speedwork.

But because I wanted to get faster, I wasn’t able to avoid the initial speed workout. However, I adjusted the training volume of my plan from “progressive” to “steady” and the difficulty level from “balanced” to “comfortable,” which dropped my speed workouts from once a week to once every two weeks. This brought my predicted race time to between 2:30 and 2:40, and I was ready to work toward that target.

Making time for a warmup helps me better settle into a running pace, so I always try to do one (even when I’m not training with an app). When you skip the warmup, you’re forcing your muscles to go from not working at all to working really hard, and that increases your risk for injury, Kessell says. Doing a targeted full-body warmup can help you transition better.

If you don’t know where to start, Kessell recommends doing dynamic stretches and walking or light jogging.

Runna actually suggests warmups, which I always follow, that include moves to target tight areas, like the calves (via a standing calf stretch in which you step one foot back and drive heel to the floor) and hips (with a move like open gates, which requires driving one knee up to hip height, then out to the side and back down).

When I do a shorter warmup or if I skip one entirely, it usually takes a couple of miles for me to get comfortable with moving. But after a 10-minute warmup, I find my pace more easily.

You might find you need more time or to focus on different areas of your body to better prepare. Regardless, making the time to warm up can help you ease into a workout and feel better as you run it—especially if you have speed on the schedule.

Tracking your runs with rate of perceived exertion (RPE)—on a scale of one to 10 with 10 being an all-out effort—helps you tap into how your body is responding to different paces. This also lets you consider other factors that influence pace, such as sleep, nutrition, and weather. The rain, wind, heat, and humidity can make running feel harder, so you might want to go slower when in these conditions, for example.

If you opt for it, Runna even tweaks your pace targets based on heat and time of day. But it’s still important to pay attention to how you feel, especially midrun.

Paying attention to how you feel in between sessions is also important, considering fatigue and soreness can affect your performance. Feeling sore after a hard workout is normal; that’s your body adapting to the work, but if you make huge jumps in mileage or increase intensity too quickly, that can lead to aches and pains, which signal you need a break from running and to see a medical professional, Kessell says.

If you’re feeling fatigued with tired legs, week after week, that can also mean a plan isn’t working for you, she adds. In this case, you may need to adjust intensity or mileage or how many days a week you run.

On the flip side, when you’re recovering well (showing up to each of your runs feeling strong and ready to go) and are consistently ahead of your pace targets a couple of weeks in a row, it’s time to consider progressing your plan.

Runna will automatically recommend training adjustments when you’re under or over your pace targets for more than three consecutive runs, but it’s up to you to accept or reject the change. Keep these signs in mind—about how you’re feeling, not just the speed you’re running—to determine if a tweak to the plan is right for you.

Runna doesn’t have an advanced training program for those who use the run/walk method as a race-day strategy, but that doesn’t mean you can’t train with it. In the past, I’ve experimented with different run/walk intervals to figure out the ratio that works for me. For example, I prefer to run for three minutes and walk for one on my easy days, but on hard days, I run for 90 seconds and walk for 30. I used these ratios to get through all of my training sessions with Runna.

I also downloaded the Simple Interval Timer app to time my run and walk intervals. This helped me better track my walk breaks, so I didn’t miss them. (Runna doesn’t have a built-in timer for you to use.)

When training with Runna, you have the option to monitor your workout targets based on time or feel. I went with a time-based approach, so the app told me whenever I needed to slow down or speed up to keep me on pace.

If you have a time goal, Kessell recommends toggling between both options, so you learn exactly what your target pace feels like. (To do this, select “manage plan” in the Runna app, then change the units of measure.)

Every runner should take some time off after a race, whether it’s a few days or a few weeks. Runna makes this easier by offering postrace training plans to help you make a smoother transition and avoid ramping back up too quickly.

The postrace recovery plan I followed allowed me to start running again without the pressure of a goal. Almost all of the runs I did were 30 minutes or less, and that was just enough to keep me motivated. Running didn’t feel like a chore; it felt like maintenance—exactly what I needed after a big race.

Monique LeBrun is a health and fitness editor who is based in Easton, Pennsylvania. She covers a wide range of health and wellness topics, with a primary focus on running performance and nutrition. Monique is passionate about creating content that empowers runners to become the best versions of themselves. As an avid runner and parent, she loves spending time outdoors with her daughter, who often accompanies her on weekend runs as her personal mini run coach.

#583. Restoring rivers brings back wildlife, but their natural functions may not recover

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A global analysis of 281 streams found restoration helps species return, but key processes like nitrogen removal often look unchanged.

When a stream gets its curves back or loses a dam, the project is usually judged by what lives in the water afterward. More species often do come back.

The work that goes on in a healthy stream, such as algae growth and the removal of nitrogen from the water, doesn’t reliably return with them.

A new global analysis checked those processes. For most of them, restored streams looked about the same as damaged ones that were never repaired.

The finding matters in Europe, where the Nature Restoration Regulation lists those processes as a goal of river restoration.

The analysis was led by Ulrike Haase, a doctoral researcher at TU Dresden in Germany.

River ecologist Mario Brauns coordinated the study at the Helmholtz Centre for Environmental Research (UFZ), with scientists from 13 research institutions taking part.

The team looked at work that changes a stream’s shape and flow. Researchers call it hydromorphological restoration.

“Hydromorphological restoration can improve biological structure, but ecosystem functions do not automatically recover as a result,” Haase said.

“Our findings show that improvements in stream structure or biological recovery do not necessarily indicate that ecosystem functions have recovered.”

Many of Europe’s rivers and streams have been straightened, blocked by dams and weirs, and had their banks and beds reinforced.

According to Germany’s Federal Environment Agency, fewer than 10% of the country’s rivers and streams receive at least a good rating for ecological health.

The rating depends mainly on which species live there.

Reshaping and polluting rivers cuts the variety of life in them. It also weakens their natural ability to clean themselves.

Restoration projects try to undo the damage. Crews reshape channels, remove dams and other barriers, reconnect rivers to their floodplains, restore plants along the banks, and add large pieces of wood to the water.

Scientists measure a stream’s health partly through ecosystem functions – the natural processes that keep it working.

One is how fast algae and water plants grow using sunlight. Another is how fast fallen leaves and other dead material break down.

A third happens in the streambed, where microbes turn nitrate, a form of nitrogen common in polluted streams, into nitrogen gases.

That process, called denitrification, removes nitrate pollution from the water.

Haase’s team sorted through more than 5,900 publications and kept 66 studies that measured these processes in restored streams.

Together they covered 281 streams or sections of streams in North America, South America, Europe, Asia, and Oceania. The sites ranged from about 50 feet (16 meters) above sea level to nearly 9,800 feet (2,974 meters).

The team compared nine processes across three kinds of sites: damaged, restored, and near-natural. The near-natural sites showed what good health looks like.

Of the nine processes, only denitrification clearly differed between restored and damaged sites. The rates were lower at the restored ones.

“However, this does not automatically mean that the function has deteriorated,” Brauns said. “Lower denitrification rates may also indicate lower nutrient enrichment.”

The researchers also suggested more oxygen or less buildup of organic matter, such as dead leaves, as possible reasons.

But damaged and near-natural sites didn’t clearly differ on this process.

That made it difficult to determine whether lower denitrification rates reflected healthier conditions or other environmental changes.

For the other eight processes, restored and damaged sites showed no clear difference.

The type of restoration didn’t seem to matter either. The authors wrote that their four broad groups of methods may be too coarse to show a difference.

“This shows that the restoration measures commonly used to date are usually not sufficient on their own to ensure a measurable recovery of the ecosystem functions we studied,” said Brauns.

Plant and algae growth showed why a higher number isn’t always a better one. That growth was higher at damaged sites than at near-natural ones.

Extra nutrients from pollution can feed algae, so a polluted site can look more productive than a healthy one.

Restored sites also tended to have more growth than near-natural sites, but that gap was too uncertain to count.

Restored sites removed nitrate from the water more slowly than near-natural sites did, too. At older restored sites, algae growth and denitrification were lower than at damaged sites.

Even so, none of the gaps between restored and near-natural sites changed measurably as the projects aged.

“However, we can assess whether an ecosystem function is actually recovering only if we know its status under appropriate reference conditions,” Brauns said.

Some of the studies tracked both the processes and the living community, such as the number of species and how many living things there were.

In those studies, the living community generally did better at restored sites than at damaged ones. The processes changed less consistently.

The researchers wrote that this comparison rested on only three to eight studies per process. The data were too uncertain to say that life and function recover separately as a rule.

What the analysis does show is that a rising count of freshwater species isn’t enough to show that the processes have recovered.

Besides the Nature Restoration Regulation, the EU’s Biodiversity Strategy for 2030 calls for restoring natural river functions.

The Kunming–Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework lists restoring ecosystem functions as a goal too.

Scientists call plant and algae growth gross primary production, and the rate at which nutrients are removed from the water is called nutrient uptake.

“Quantifying ecosystem functions such as gross primary production or nutrient uptake often requires considerable effort,” said Brauns.

Unlike biodiversity, these processes still lack common standard methods and reference values. Natural rates also differ between regions and types of rivers, so one healthy number for every site is unlikely to exist.

“The main priority is to further develop and standardize functional indicators for practical use,” Brauns added. “We must incorporate these indicators into future monitoring and assessments of restoration success.”

Until those reference values exist, researchers can measure how fast a restored stream removes nitrogen. They just can’t say whether that rate means it has recovered.

The authors also called for studies that track sites before and after restoration, alongside damaged and near-natural sites.

That approach would help researchers distinguish the effects of restoration from natural changes in weather and water flow.

The full study was published in the journal Global Change Biology.

#3313. This Man Just Turned 50—and Ran an Astonishing 2:15 Marathon, Shattering the Age Group World Record by 4 Minutes

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Mark Kiptoo averaged 5:10 pace at the Frankfurt Marathon to reset a 35-year-old masters mark.

Mark Kiptoo has had some pretty memorable trips to Frankfurt. He won the city's marathon in 2014, returned four years later to set a then-world record for runners over 40, and on Sunday added another achievement to the list. This time, at 50 years old.

The Kenyan distance runner finished the Frankfurt Marathon on October 25 in 2:15:24, averaging roughly 5:10 per mile across 26.2 miles. He placed 13th overall, but his performance was particularly impressive for another reason: It was the fastest marathon ever recorded by a man in the 50–54 age group—and it wasn't especially close.

For 35 years, the M50 record belonged to South Africa's Titus Mamabolo, who ran 2:19:29 in Durban in July 1991. Kiptoo cut four minutes and five seconds off that mark on Sunday, a substantial improvement that now awaits official ratification by World Masters Athletics.

Kiptoo has barely had time to get used to being 50. He celebrated his birthday on June 21, less than two months after finishing 2:12:21 at the Geneva Marathon. That performance came too early to qualify for an M50 record, but it offered a pretty good preview of what he could do once he entered the age group.

Frankfurt wasn't even his first record-setting performance this month. On October 4, Kiptoo won the San Sebastián Half Marathon in Spain in 1:03:57, establishing a new M50 world-best time for the distance. Three weeks later, he was back on a starting line, this time with twice as many miles to cover.

Kiptoo didn't start competing until he was 28, entering his first race in 2004 while serving in the Kenyan Air Force. He went on to win the African 5,000-meter title in 2012, but didn't make his marathon debut until age 37. That first 26.2 was in Frankfurt, where he finished second in 2013, just one second behind the winner.

He also lost roughly a year of training to Achilles tendon trouble and the pandemic, according to the Frankfurt organizers. He returned to win the Zurich Marathon three years in a row from 2023 through 2025, clocking 2:09:16 in his last victory at age 48.

Before Sunday's race, Kiptoo told organizers he hoped his running would inspire people not to give up or stop taking care of their bodies as they got older. Running a 2:15 marathon at 50 certainly gives him a pretty persuasive example to point to.

Sean Abrams was the Senior Editor, Growth and Engagement at Men’s Health. He’s a former hip hop dancer who likes long walks on the beach and large glasses of tequila. You can find his previous work at Maxim, Elite Daily, and AskMen.

#3991. Shame Is a Repeating 6-Act Cycle: An IFS View of Self-Attack

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Abigail Fagan

IFS is an 'experience-near therapy,' which is to say, it follows subjective experience closely. As a result, IFS practitioners understand the mind as plural. It is a community of parts (as people say) of varying ages and roles. When something bad happens, some parts become protective and other parts (the vulnerable ones) get blamed and exiled.

But that’s not all. In addition to parts, the mind produces (or channels, depending on your perspective) a different kind of consciousness, which is characterized by cognitive clarity and centered emotional states like curiosity and compassion. IFS calls this (again, as people say) Self, with a capital S to signal a special use of the word: “This is my true Self.” When the Self is overshadowed by the strong feelings of parts, we lose balance. Shame is especially unbalancing.

Here is a description of shame as a long-term experience featuring distinct acts in relationship and in the theatre of the mind.

An authority figure (caretaker, relative, teacher, older child, sibling, or peer) shames a child. The bad thing that happens is interpersonal. The shamer is not me. The shamed child is a passive recipient of the shaming with no intent in this situation. If we flash forward to therapy in the present, the bad thing that happened has become history.

Act 2. Acceptance: The Shamed Part Believes I Am Bad (Defective, Worthless, Evil, Rotten, Monstrous, Too Much, Too Little)

The most vulnerable part of the shamed child accepts the judgment and concludes I am shameful. Acceptance, like the shaming event itself, sets the stage for the problems that might lead a person to therapy.

Act 3. Critics Go Large: That’s True, You Are Bad

Proactive manager parts hold the shamed part of the child responsible for being shamed. Hoping to improve or at least suppress its despair, they start shaming it. I call this instrumental shaming because, even though shaming will eventually lead to antisocial avoidance and reactive rage, this inner shamer has a positive, prosocial intent. By inhibiting the shamed part, who it views as a liability, it aims to take control of a dangerous situation. With the advent of inner shaming, "I" am not only the recipient of shaming, “I” am also the shamer.

Anticipatory proactive "scout" protectors do a lot of warning: Do be this! Don't be that! Be quiet! Apologize! Aiming to prevent more shaming, inside or out, they anticipate the complaints of a (real or imagined) critical audience. Because of them and other proactive critics, a problem that was once external is now self-­generated. Their intent is prosocial, but their actions reinforce both the antisocial existence of exiled young parts and the antisocial behavior of reactive protectors.

Reactive protectors cover a lot of territory. Some shame other people, diminishing them by comparison and rebooting the self-­esteem of a system that is collapsing under the weight of internal shaming. This external shaming is also instrumental. This reactive protector is rebellious, disinhibited, and ready to throw the hot potato. Though its behavior is antisocial and will have bad consequences, its internal intent is still prosocial. It aims to ward off the dangerous consequences of continual shaming.

Other reactive protectors get relief from shaming and shamefulness with some preferred way of self-soothing or distracting. A wide range of behaviors involving disinhibition can fit this bill. They drink too much, use drugs excessively, smoke cigarettes, gobble soothing foods, gamble compulsively, and so on. They take risks without concern for consequences. They also mean to be helpful, and they are essentially asocial—they just don’t care about other people—but their behavior generally has a negative effect on other people.

I’ve organized the shame cycle neatly into separate acts, but neither life nor therapy is neat. Protectors can all be in action at once—and that can be overwhelmingly confusing. Here’s the thing to remember: This confusion can be sorted by the motives and actions of parts. It is the story of shame. The parts who are caught in the shame cycle need your help to slow down and take turns telling their portion of the story. They need you to listen with kindness and compassion, and ask questions with genuine curiosity. When you do, the big story will gradually change from I am bad to A bad thing happened to me.

This post is adapted from the book Internal Family Systems Therapy for Shame and Guilt.

Sweezy, M. (2023). Internal family systems therapy for shame and guilt. New York, NY: Guilford Press.

#4015. College Provides Opportunities for Social Skills Building

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Margaret Foley

"Young adults are inundated with ideas that the college experience will be the best 4 years of their lives, but research has demonstrated that students often feel their actual experiences are different than what they expected, causing a lack of quality of their social connections" (Wodika et al., 2024).

In a new article in the journal Social and Emotional Learning, graduate student Mikaela Dahlman and I highlight the challenge faced by college students who are skilled and competent in their content expertise but lacking in their interpersonal skills. We argue that college is the last “polishing” or finishing ground for students to hone their intra- and interpersonal skills in preparation for thriving in the workforce.

We see new graduate hires undermining their workplace performance and job stability by engaging in behaviors that compromise their employment. These can include:

This reminds me of when I was hiring a research assistant to work in my lab. When I explained the hours, the student responded: "But I have yoga at 10 a.m." I replied that I suspected there were multiple classes offered at the yoga studio and suggested the student might take a different class. To this, the student replied, "I align with the 10 a.m. instructor. You want me to take care of my well-being, don't you?"

A recent piece in Forbes titled lays the blame on employee attitude. This is echoed in a recent post on Psychology Today reflecting on college graduates’ readiness for an ever-changing workforce, which argues that “Communication, professionalism, adaptability, critical thinking, and collaboration remain among the most sought-after competencies.” Extending the conversation further, a recent piece in Forbes argues for the advantage offered by “soft skills” in a context where everyone is technologically proficient. “… employees who excel in both technical skills and soft skills like empathy, communication, collaboration and problem-solving are more likely to climb the corporate ladder and earn a higher salary.”

The college context, through both formal (i.e., collaborative assignments in courses) and informal (e.g., joining clubs or associations) pathways, provides a rich training ground for students to hone their social and emotional competencies. Colleges must continue providing opportunities for students to polish their social skills, but students too have a responsibility to ensure they are developing their soft skills alongside their academic training and emerging content expertise.

Binfet, J. T., & Dahlman, M. L. (2026). Loneliness undermines college students’ opportunities to hone their social and emotional competencies. Social and Emotional Learning Journal: Research, Policy, and Practice. https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2773233926000422

Wodika, A., Lanier, J., Almeda, J., Richter, A., & Schalasky, G. (2024). Helping students make meaningful connections: A cross-sectional survey of college student loneliness. American Journal of Health Education, 56(4), 291-304. https://doi.org/10.1080/19325037.2024.2396586

#906. Luke Hemsworth’s Recovery Formula Involves a Wolverine Peptide Stack and PRP Injections

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

It’s not easy having Chris Hemsworth as your younger brother. “He has a superhero gene,” says Luke Hemsworth, 45, from his hotel room in Melbourne. “It’s not fair. We’re always, all of us, playing catch up with that guy."

But big bro is all smiles, buoyant even, as we catch up about his role as the “fundamentally terrible” Landry in The Terminal List: Dark Wolf, the second series of the Navy SEAL thriller starring Chris Pratt. Top of the agenda is how he got in such good shape for season two. “The idea was that actually, as he got fitter and bigger and more intimidating, that was a sign that he was unraveling as a male,” Hemsworth says. So any special personal trainer sent by the studio? A rigid diet to follow? “I mean, I’m incredibly active,” he says, explaining that it wasn’t all that hard for him to bulk when he already does multiple hours of physical activity a day, between weightlifting, surfing, and cardio.

Usually, you’ll find him working out alongside his brothers Chris and Liam, and his best friend Hutch (Craig Hutchison) in Byron Bay, where they all live in nearby houses. “The group-style training that we do is very competitive, where we pit against each other, and then the Muay [Thai] sessions have been amazing.”

Again, when Thor is your brother, it can feel like the fight is stacked against you. But, “we’re connecting, and we’re learning how to take a punch and take a kick,” says the elder Hemsworth. “It creates camaraderie."

Below, Luke Hemsworth told GQ about a few of his day-to-day rituals, from morning surf to hid preferred peptide stack.

“Coffee I allow myself. It’s more of a mental health ritual. I’ll whip cream with a little hand buzzer, make it all fluffy. And then the long black with the Americano over the top of that. I’ll do two or three a day.”

“The dream growing up was to live close enough to the beach that I could walk to the surf... when the surf is good, I’m surfing most days. For me, the benefit is mental. When I’m not surfing, I really feel it. I just really get lost without it. Usually in the morning, there’s a window from sun-up till about 10, 11.”

“My garage has been completely converted [into a gym], and we have a little outside area as well. Between all of us [brothers and Hutch] there’s a gym in everyone’s house, and invariably every single day we’ll be doing something. Hutch has Monday sessions up at his house, which is like a big circuit session. We’ve started doing a Muay Thai session there on a Friday. But it’s more like a men’s health group.”

“I had a trainer, Brad Whitehead, who’s an ex-bodybuilder. I was very specific about [how] I just want to look like an old-school bodybuilder. So heavy weights, lots of low reps, classic big compound movements. I started deadlifting like 140 kilos. Three sets of 10 at 140, five days a week. The heavyweight stuff is not long; 30 to 40 minutes.”

“I’ll walk the dogs. I’ll sit on the treadmill for a while, I’ll sit on the Peloton for a while. I’ll just mix it all in. I’ll sit on the treadmill or walk, usually with a vest on, 20 pounds.”

“I was playing around with peptides—BPC-157 and TB-500. It was more about recovery, because I’m becoming an old man. It’s crazy how much it helps.”

[Editor's Note: BPC-157 and TB-500 have not been approved by the FDA for human use.]

“It’s pretty much a regular diet. The thing that I’ll do is just lower my carbs... focus on protein. Lots of salad to keep the fiber, keep you feeling full. It’ll be steak and salad, and my wife and I sort of cook together, I’ll do the meat and she’ll do the salads, baked cauliflower and stuff.”

“I’ve done some PRP a couple of times into the [knee] joint. On my right in particular, which gives me a couple of months of alleviation because there’s some osteoarthritis, some meniscus stuff.”

“If I do drink, then I’ll have a proper night, but I’m not an everyday sort of guy. Beer is definitely a weakness here.”

“If I’m awake after 10 p.m., I’m in all sorts. I will be asleep. I’ll be in bed, I reckon, quarter past 9 with my book, 10 p.m. done. I’m usually awake at 5:30. I’ll get up, and I’ll go to the beach. I’ll take the dogs to the beach, walk the dogs, and get all that sunlight in the morning.”

#919. Jay-Z Bets on Himself for the Super Bowl Halftime Show

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

This is an edition of the weekly newsletter Tap In, GQ senior associate editor Frazier Tharpe’s final word on the most heated online discourse about music, movies, and TV. Sign up here to get it free.

In 2008, when Washington Wizards guard Deshawn Stevenson called LeBron James overrated, Jay-Z intervened on LeBron’s behalf with “Blow the Whistle,” a loose, low-stakes freestyle over Bay Area legend Too Short’s instant classic of the same name. The track is all about being too big to respond to petty swipes. The most enduring line goes: “We let the money do the talkin—as you can see, we be talkin rather often.”

Fitting, then, that Jay opened his concert on Saturday night—the second of back-to-back shows at Los Angeles’ SoFi Stadium—with “Blow the Whistle.” In the midst of similar noise, he let the music do the talking.

The pair of shows marked the finale of Jay’s year-long Jay-Z30 project, celebrating the 30th anniversary of his debut album with a trio of sold-out concerts at Yankee Stadium this past summer and subsequent dates across London and Paris. Incidentally, the grand finale arrived at a moment when Jay is at the center of not one but two divisive debates. The first, currently raging on Rap Twitter and recently aggravated by 50 Cent, concerns his place in the rap canon (was he ever truly the undisputed “dominant” rapper in the game?) And then, with the announcement that he’s chosen himself to be the 2027 Super Bowl Halftime Show headliner, some are calling his relevance into question, or snidely remarking that he’ll need to lean heavily on his Rolodex to actually pull it off.

Both debates are ridiculous and reductive. So Jay used the occasion of two (more) sold-out stadium shows to prove why, running through a roughly 30-song setlist deftly curated with crowd-pleasers, B-Side gems(“The Watcher 2”!!), and hit singles—just him, a 10-piece band, and the occasional light show. Across both shows, there were only four guest moments: the aforementioned Short Dawg, John Legend & DJ Khaled for “God Did,” Scarface for what may have been the first-ever performance of their heralded collab “This Can’t Be Life,” and an extended medley from Dr. Dre and Snoop Dogg. All were sparing, intentional and worked in service of the overarching theme celebrating Jay’s lore. (After all, he did write “Still D.R.E.” Imagine if he came out with them to essentially perform his reference track.)

Jay’s fixation on his naysayers felt especially pointed, sometimes to a fault. While “God Did” is great, it made for an awkward encore on night one, though the stamina flex to do a three-minute verse right after doing a two-hour set was admirable. The obsession with tour cameos in general has gotten a little out of hand. Sure, it’s fun as hell when performers show off how deep their collaborative bag is and how influential their reach is—like, say, night two of Kanye’s Chicago homecoming or night three of Jay in Yankee Stadium. But reinforcing that you can move the crowd by yourself—without even the help of a backing track—is a flex of equal, if not greater, import. Having hits is paramount, but at what point is the effect diluted if you’re just having the track play and letting the audience do most of the work? Exhibiting athletic breath control, Jay tore through one anthem after another, from the lyrical gymnastics of “Hovi Baby” to a track that invites audience participation like “Beach Is Better”—or a song that requires both, like “N-gga What, N-gga Who,” breaking occasionally just to change fits.

On one hand it would’ve been even better if Jay was back on home turf instead of playing to an audience that inevitably consisted of some casuals. But in some regards, winning those folks over is an even more impressive display of endurance and reach. The “was Jay ever the guy?” discourse is amusing because even some of the people coming to his defense kind of get it wrong, relying too heavily on the idea that longevity trounces a few years of white-hot flash. That’s true, but it doesn’t tell the full story. I saw tweets that compared him to the turtle that beat the hare, or a reliable Honda Accord, both misguided, backhanded compliments that don’t really account for the magnitude of Hov’s imperial phase, with “Hard Knock Life,” “Jigga My N-gga,” “Big Pimpin,” “I Just Wanna Love You,” “Izzo,” “Excuse Me Miss,” “Dirt Off Your Shoulder,” and “99 Problems” being released in successive summers.

The Super Bowl thing is especially weird. Jay could’ve anointed himself the first rapper to do the Halftime Show, but instead he happily ceded that honor to Kendrick Lamar. In fact, he could’ve pegged himself for the gig at any time in the eight years he’s presided over the show. If Jay’s music just isn’t your thing, that’s your prerogative (and a skill issue). But the narratives feel forced. The notion that he has no appeal beyond New Yorkers and hip-hop fans of a certain age is a bizarre stance to take on the guy who headlined Coachella and Glastonbury, becoming the first rapper to do both. Moreover, the notion that he isn’t relevant or that his hits are “old” is coming from the same people who were excited to see Usher perform classics from 20-plus-year-old albums. Picking an artist who appeals to Gen-Z or Gen-Alpha has hardly been a major concern in years past. On their podcast, Joe Budden and his co-host Ice said they couldn’t get excited at the prospect of Jay having to perform some of his most pop and broadly appealing hits. But didn’t they chastise Lamar for a Halftime Show setlist mostly comprised of then-three-month-old GNX tracks? Is it “Oochie-wally” or is it “One Mic?”

To that last point, I’d argue that Jay’s recent shows—even going back to his Roots Picnic one-off in May—have been more thoughtfully and intentionally sequenced (backed by his longtime engineer, Young Guru, and longtime band, 1500 or Nothin) than even his classic tours of yore, weaving in a little something for everyone (like the way he mixes “N-ggas in Paris” with “Big Pimpin,” serving two different generations of fan at once). He didn’t just play the hits; he played the cool hits. Quieter songs got beefed up with string quartets, or an assist from Jay’s daughter on the keys. He used Watch the Throne beats to get off classic radio freestyle verses. Each tour stop had a moment designed to amp up the locals, like rhyming over a Giggs staple in “London” or mixing in “Last Time That I Checc’d” in L.A. He has a showman’s knack for routine and on-the-fly innovation.

There’s no way he makes a Halftime Show full of concessions to the masses and zero nods to the fans. I’d argue he’s brazen enough to do something like kicking things off with “Young, Gifted and Black”—“I’m America’s worst nightmare/I’m young, Black and holding my nuts like chea”—which would be a fair trade-off should he include something poppier, like “Young Forever.” As for the staples: does anybody truly think “Hard Knock Life” or “I Just Wanna Love You” are too old to have crossover appeal with a Super Bowl audience? Do you really have to be able to go bar-for-bar on “U Don’t Know” to appreciate Adam Blackstone absolutely shredding that bass outro? I don’t know how anyone looks at the list of tracks from either LA show and doesn’t think Jay’s only problem will be whittling his Super Bowl set down to a tight 13 minutes. (For what it’s worth, he described Kendrick’s decision to highlight mostly new songs as “brave” when I interviewed him earlier this year.)

Jay indirectly addressed some of the narratives in his closing remarks on Saturday night, alluding to bot accounts and smear campaigns that alleged half-empty crowds, lackluster audience engagement, and middling reception. He urged the audience, instead, to look around, use their own eyes, and preach the truth. A fine sentiment, but it was the last line that got me: “They’ll tell you Jigga ain’t Jigga… Hov is motherfucking Hov!”

All this talk on his legacy has Jay referring to himself in the third person, and once again sporting an offensively ostentatious new chain. It makes me wonder if the smoke, doubt, and disparagement has woken up the shit-talking flexer who made “So Ghetto” or “It’s Hot”—Jigga Man, as it were. As everyone anticipates all of this activity building to a new solo album, most (including the man himself) have wondered what he might have to say, what themes could span 10 or so tracks the same way he did on 4:44. Despite being outside this year, he hasn’t rapped much—the only official new verse we got was a remix he did with his wife that sounded more like someone playing around in the studio and clearing the cobwebs.

Before that though, there was Roots Picnic, the comeback show that set the tone for this year, where he paused the celebration to heat check all of his enemies and detractors with a lacerating new freestyle. He ended that verse with a warning: “Not too much on my name/Don’t forget who I am.” At the Halftime Show in February, he’s got a chance to make them remember.

GQ’s AOC to TMZ Guide to the 2026 Midterm Elections

Rolex Is Breaking All Its Own Rules With These One-of-a-Kind Daytonas

#2463. Why Do I Get Dizzy When I Stand Up? Experts Say No, This Isn't Normal

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

“If something is repeatedly interfering with how you feel or function, you deserve a thoughtful evaluation.”

It happens when you get up from your desk at work, from playing on the floor with your kids, even when you get out of bed first thing in the morning. Lately, whenever you go from lying or sitting to standing, you get a little dizzy. You have to pause and close your eyes, maybe grab onto something solid, and wait a few seconds before taking off. So what gives? Why do you get dizzy when you stand up now, and is it normal?

Well, medical experts agree that getting dizzy every time you stand up is categorically not normal, and it’s worth making an appointment with your doctor to investigate why it happens to you. They say the answer is likely something easy to fix, and anything impacting your daily functioning is worth the investigation.

Adjusting your blood flow to your current activity level is one of those things our brain does all on its own that we don’t really appreciate fully until it goes wrong.

“When someone stands up, gravity pulls blood toward the lower half of the body, and the nervous system has to respond quickly to maintain blood flow to the brain. If that adjustment doesn’t happen efficiently, you can feel lightheaded, see spots, or feel as though you’re about to faint,” says Dr. Motisola Lewis, MD, internal medicine physician and founder of Lewis Medical NY.

For women in their 30s, 40s, and 50s, dizziness upon standing can be a symptom of many different things.

“Some of the most common causes are dehydration, not eating or drinking enough, low blood pressure, anemia, blood loss, and medication side effects. In women in their 30s through 50s, I also think about heavy menstrual bleeding, hormonal transitions, and medications that can affect blood pressure or heart rate. Sometimes there is more than one contributing factor,” Lewis explains.

It’s also important to distinguish between dizziness and vertigo so you can accurately describe your experience to your doctor.

“If they feel lightheaded, faint, or like they might pass out, I would think about blood pressure issues, especially orthostatic hypotension. Orthostatic hypotension is a drop in blood pressure with standing. Dehydration, medication side effects or interactions, blood sugar fluctuations, cardiovascular issues, and other medical conditions can also cause lightheadedness,” says Dr. Kim Bell, a doctor of physical therapy who specializes in dizziness, vertigo, balance problems, and fall prevention.

“If they describe more of a brief spinning sensation when they get up, I would also want to rule out benign paroxysmal positional vertigo, or BPPV, which is the most common inner ear cause of vertigo. BPPV can cause momentary dizziness or spinning with position changes such as sitting up in bed or getting out of bed.”

If you’ve made an appointment with your doctor to discuss your dizziness, ask yourself a few questions and log the answers for a week or two before you go. Pay attention to what the dizziness feels like, what movement or position triggers it, and how long it lasts, Bell says. “Those details can provide important clues about the cause.”

If it happens once, OK, maybe you were just dehydrated. But if it happens frequently, no, that’s not normal at all, Lewis and Bell agree.

“I would not want someone to assume that their height, age, stress level, or tendency to ‘stand up too fast’ explains recurring dizziness without actually investigating the cause,” says Bell.

If you’ve ever had a doctor chalk up your dizziness to standing up too fast, anxiety, or literally just being too tall (this really happens), look for a second opinion.

“Women deserve to be heard before their symptoms are explained away. Many women are exceptionally good at pushing through discomfort. They get the children ready, go to work, care for their families, and carry on even when they don’t feel well. Sometimes they normalize their symptoms because they’ve been told that what they’re experiencing is just stress, aging, or something they have to live with. But if you're consistently dizzy when you stand, exhausted despite sleeping, experiencing a racing heart, or struggling to function the way you used to, it’s worth asking why,” says Lewis.

This doesn’t mean your dizziness is pointing to a rare or serious condition; usually the cause is something simple and totally treatable, Lewis adds.

If you’re experiencing certain symptoms along with dizziness, it warrants a more urgent assessment, Lewis says. Here are the symptoms that Bell and Lewis say would make them want to see you ASAP:

If you experience new neurological symptoms, including sudden weakness or numbness on one side, confusion, double vision, difficulty speaking, or a sudden severe headache, that’s your sign to head to your nearest emergency room, Lewis notes.

If you’ve heard about POTS lately, well, that’s because it’s becoming a lot more common.

All around the world, studies show health experts are diagnosing more people than ever before with postural orthostatic tachycardia syndrome, or POTS: an autonomic nervous system disorder that causes your heart to beat faster than normal when you go from sitting or lying down to standing up. Unsurprisingly, dizziness upon standing is one of its hallmark symptoms. The condition can be caused by severe viral infections; public health experts believe the COVID-19 pandemic caused a massive number of previously healthy people to develop POTS.

While it might be in the zeitgeist right now, Lewis says POTS is not the most common explanation for dizziness upon standing. Women in their 30s, 40s, and 50s can have so much happening in their bodies that the dizziness may be the result of more than one factor as well — think sleep disruptions, hormonal changes, and iron deficiency.

If anything in your life, like getting dizzy when you stand, is interfering with how you feel or function, “you deserve a thoughtful evaluation” from your healthcare provider, Lewis says.

#4023. Mental Illness During War Is Another Battle to Fight

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Davia Sills

By Sophie Wagner and Robert T. Muller, Ph.D.

According to the Geneva Academy, there are more than 114 ongoing armed conflicts across the globe. Many associate armed conflict with physical danger and death, but the impact on civilians’ mental health is an overlooked crisis.

This involves more than the traumas they are already facing, such as bombings and malnutrition, but also anxieties about things they might face—like family death, injury, or becoming disabled. These anxieties are especially dangerous, as they can persist across one’s lifetime.

The experience of war can result in a multitude of mental health diagnoses, the most common being post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), anxiety, and depression. PTSD can develop after witnessing or experiencing a trauma related to a threat of death or injury.

Those with PTSD often relive the traumatic event through nightmares or flashbacks, constantly feel like they are in danger, and engage in destructive behavior. Although exposure to trauma does not guarantee the development of PTSD, citizens in war zones consistently develop it.

A 2025 study looked at PTSD rates among Palestinian civilians one year into the conflict with Israel. They found that of the 405 people surveyed, 85 percent met the criteria for a PTSD diagnosis. Additionally, 72.7 percent met the criteria for depression and 65 percent for severe anxiety. Similarly, a 2024 study found that 1 out of 6 civilians in Ukraine met the criteria for PTSD, and 88 percent reported poor sleep quality. The latter is attributed to frequent drone and rocket attacks that occur at night, forcing civilians to retreat into bomb shelters. PTSD and sleep disturbances are closely connected. Nightmares and PTSD symptoms can interfere with restful sleep, impacting mood stability and the ability to recover the next day.

Children experiencing armed conflict can develop mental health and developmental issues. They are more likely to develop behavioral issues such as increased anger, irritability, and difficulty maintaining social relationships. In Syria, years of ongoing conflict have had significant effects on children’s psychological well-being. Research involving 492 Syrian children found that 60 percent showed symptoms of at least one probable psychological disorder, with post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD) being the most common, followed by depression and anxiety.

Genocide can lead to intergenerational trauma, where the effects of trauma and ways survivors cope are passed down to future generations. This has been observed in families who survived Holodomor, the Holocaust, and in many other populations. Therefore, we must consider how future generations in places that are currently declared to be in the midst of a genocide, such as Palestine, will be affected.

Gazan doctors, in particular, struggle with mental illness as they often have to choose between mourning their own losses or caring for the wounded. Suppressing one’s personal suffering while treating others is a unique psychological burden that doctors report having.

In times of war, communities find new coping mechanisms, highlighting their resilience. Palestinians in the Gaza Strip gather over a shared meal, splitting their resources and telling stories, instilling hope. When a family loses their home, each neighbor offers whatever they can to the family in need: childcare, food, and household items.

Similarly, as schools have converted into overcrowded shelters, volunteer teachers will teach children math and writing skills. Civilians have also found strength in spiritual practices, using them to reframe their suffering as meaningful within the divine plan or as part of a divine test.

While these coping mechanisms are helpful, many do not address the long-term mental health effects that survivors experience. Currently, the focus of international aid is on food and medical care. However, it is crucial that we also address the mental health crisis faced by civilians to ensure that not only their futures are bright, but also those of their future families.

Sophie Wagner is a research student at York University.
