#1159. 4 Workout Strategies This Over-50 Trainer Uses to Keep Building Muscle

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Use his guidance so you can train hard and hurt less.

THE MUSCLE-BUILDING NARRATIVE after age 50 isn’t pretty. According to doomers online, your testosterone levels are falling, your energy levels are in massive decline, and everything seems to throw your back out.

But contrary to popular belief, you can still muscle up. And Bryan Krahn, 53 years old and as strong as he’s ever been, is proof. The trainer, who created MH’s Max Muscle at 50 workout program, still works out four or five days a week and has maintained a muscular 210-pound physique..

That doesn’t mean he trains exactly like he did when he was in his 20s. Instead, over the last three decades, he’s made a series of adjustments, shifting his fitness priorities (much less bench pressing) and experimenting with new gym ideas. All that trial-and-error helped Krahn develop these four critical post-50 training principles, which will allow you to push your muscle-building limits for decades to come without crushing your body. He discussed these ideas (and much more) on MH’s Strong Talk podcast.

LIKE MOST GUYS, Krahn relied on dumbbells and barbells for years. But about a decade ago, those exercises started to hurt his joints. He has since shifted his focus to training that minimizes his risk of getting hurt. He is quick to note this isn’t about being overly cautious—avoiding injury is not the same as training scared.

Part of the fix for Krahn involved taking a little bit of time off. The other part: He headed to a section of the gym that guys often forget about: the machine area. Because yes, dumbbells and barbells and kettlebells seem to build more “functional” strength (so the narrative goes). But as you get older, those tools don’t always work as well with your joints. “Within four or five months, I have no pain, and I’m strong on these machines,” Krahn says. “Cables are indispensable as you get older.”

Machines allow you to focus on doing movements along very fixed tracks, and that lets you concentrate on generating the maximum amount of force with your muscles. That’s a key mechanic for growth. “And if you’re a beginner, generating that force isn’t always easy with free weights,” says MH fitness director Ebenezer Samuel, C.S.C.S. “Inserting one machine exercise in your routines near the end can insure you get a chance to push your limits just a bit.”

Two machines to consider: A cable row machine, which blasts your back, and the leg press machine, which ensures you can challenge hamstrings, glutes, and quads with relatively heavy load.

A bonus: Experimenting with machines just may reignite your passion for the gym, too. Krahn says the machines allow him to enjoy his workouts—much like he did in his 20s. “I love going to the gym again,” he says.

FROM BARBELL BENCH presses to back squats to barbell deadlifts, some lifts in the gym are so popular and widespread, they might feel mandatory. But no—Krahn says you don't have to commit to any of them.

Krahn learned that when training arms. Yes, he loved skull crushers, a triceps exercise that can be notoriously tough on the elbows. But he swapped in triceps pressdowns and still built the muscle he was chasing. He did the same with his biceps work, swapping out supinated dumbbell biceps curls for hammer curls. "As soon as something feels off," he says, "let's try a variation."

Exercise physiologist and strength coach Dr. Pat Davidson agrees. Most lifts that guys think are mandatory can easily be replaced. "So many people just get emotionally attached to an exercise," Davidson says. "Don't marry an exercise."

Krahn says machines are especially valuable here, too. "It's so difficult to get hurt (on machines)," he says. "You can kind of unleash that side of yourself and really push into failure."

Major lesson: The moment you feel pain in your joints, consider finding a substitute lift for the exercise that's caused you pain. Don't wait.

THESE DAYS, KRAHN thinks about building more than muscle. He also wants to maintain his body’s ability to be powerful. What does it mean to be powerful? Muscles produce force—but they also need the ability to produce that force very quickly, which happens whenever you jump, hop, or begin to run.

And sure, plyometric exercises can train power. But once you’re in your 50s, your body can’t always absorb that pounding. That’s why Krahn loves sled pushes. He uses the move as a warmup, especially on upper body days when he’s focused on a variety of push activities. Pushing a weighted sled is a move that can build glutes, quads, and hamstrings, and light up your core. It also quietly trains something called triple-extension, the act of straightening your knee, hip, and ankle joints all at once—which is a key to running and jumping.

The best part of a sled push, Davidson says: You get to build power without much injury risk. “Anytime you’re in a forward lean acceleration posture, it's much safer,” Davidson says. “It’s almost a Smith machine of running.” Consider doing two to three sets of 30-second sled pushes to start your next workout.

SHORT ON TIME? If Krahn has only 15 minutes in a day, he’ll walk. It’s a non-negotiable in his life. “I get a lot done in that 15 minutes,” he says. “I don’t lift weights every day, but I walk every day.”

There are a lot of reasons to add walking into your daily routine. For Krahn, walking gives him the mental reprieve to step away from his responsibilities and keep his mind refreshed. Walking also keeps your body moving, limiting your sedentary time.

At 50, it’s not always about going hard or pushing or limits. Walking keeps your body moving in a joint-friendly manner, even when you're fatigued (while sneaking in some light calorie-burn, too). Daily walking also helps to lower the risk of heart disease, a concern at any age but especially for older adults.

“Right now, that’s the number one killer in the US,” Mario Mejia, PT, DPT, physical therapist at Next Play Physical Therapy & Performance, previously told MH. “Heart disease can happen anytime but as we age, physiologically there are differences in the heart, lungs, and bone structure.

Above all, Krahn's tips provide a template for how to keep building muscle and lose fat into your 50s and beyond. And Krahn's full Max Muscle at 50 program delivers even more guidance. Check it out here.

#339. JetBlue Posts Larger Loss, Higher Revenues in Q3

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

JetBlue on Monday posted a larger net loss for the third quarter but also higher revenues as consumer demand remains strong.

The carrier said it generated higher yields year-over-year and recaptured more of the increase in jet fuel costs than it expected.

Operating revenues for the quarter were $2.8 billion, up from $2.3 billion in the third quarter of 2025. The carrier’s net loss was $257 million, larger than the $143 million loss it reported in last year’s Q3.

JetBlue officials said they expect further margin expansion in the fourth quarter despite an 85% climb in fuel costs.

“I’m very encouraged by the progress we made in the third quarter,” JetBlue CEO Joanna Geraghty said in a news release. “Resilient demand and strong yields drove revenue well ahead of our initial expectations, allowing us to recapture more of the increase in fuel costs than we initially expected. That momentum has carried into the fourth quarter, and Fort Lauderdale continues to deliver exceptional results even as we significantly grow capacity.”

Earlier this week, The Wall Street Journal reported that a group of JetBlue creditors hired advisors amid concerns about the carrier’s long-term financial health. The creditors are now working with financial advisor PJT Partners and law firm Akin, the newspaper said.

JetBlue’s share price has dropped significantly since August but rebounded somewhat after Monday’s earnings call.

The airline is implementing a turnaround plan, known as JetForward, centered on cutting money-losing routes and introducing new premium products. The strategy is expected to return JetBlue to full-year profitability, which it has not achieved since 2019.

#2159. Gainesville Woman Convicted in $8.6M Medicare Fraud Scheme

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

An official website of the United States government

Official websites use .gov A .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPS A lock ( Lock Locked padlock ) or https:// means you’ve safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

A federal jury in the Middle District of Florida convicted a Florida woman who owned a durable medical equipment (DME) company and managed several other DME companies for her role in a fraud scheme that took advantage of the elderly and disabled and caused the submission of over $8.6 million in fraudulent claims to Medicare.

According to court documents and evidence presented at trial, Sophie Dufort, 57, of Gainesville, Florida, conspired with Paula Pirone, 70, of Ocala, Florida, and others to submit fraudulent claims to Medicare based on fake doctors’ prescriptions for orthotic braces.

Dufort and her co-conspirators illegally purchased these prescriptions from purported marketing companies that engaged in high pressure and manipulative telemarketing calls to get Medicare beneficiaries’ information. When confronted with complaints that the voice on purported call recordings did not match the beneficiaries’ voice, one co-conspirator explained that “some of the calls from overseas would be faked” and used to justify a bogus prescription. The marketing companies then paid medical practitioners to sign prescriptions for orthotic braces without contacting or evaluating the patients, or stole doctor’s credentials to create forged prescriptions, and then sold those prescriptions to Dufort’s and her co-conspirators’ DME companies.

Dufort and her co-conspirators then shipped braces to the Medicare beneficiaries and billed Medicare for millions of dollars in claims, despite the fact that the beneficiaries did not want or need the braces and no doctor had examined the beneficiary to determine if they needed the braces. And, as one medical expert testified, use of these combinations of braces could be “very dangerous” for the elderly Medicare population. When people complained and wanted to return the braces, “they would just tell [them] to just get rid of them or . . . just pass them down” so Dufort and her co-conspirators would not have to pay Medicare back.

The defendant was convicted of conspiracy to commit health care fraud and wire fraud, two counts of health care fraud, one count of conspiracy to defraud the United States and to offer and pay illegal kickbacks, and one count of offering and paying illegal kickbacks.  Dufort faces a maximum penalty of 20 years in prison for conspiracy to commit health care fraud and wire fraud, 10 years in prison for each health care fraud count and kickback count, and 5 years in prison for conspiracy to defraud the United States and to offer and pay illegal kickbacks.  A sentencing hearing is scheduled in Jan. 2027.  A federal district court judge will determine any sentence after considering the U.S. Sentencing Guidelines and other statutory factors.

Assistant Attorney General Colin M. McDonald of the Justice Department’s National Fraud Enforcement Division and Middle District of Florida U.S. Attorney Gregory W. Kehoe made the announcement. The case was investigated by the Department of Health and Human Services, Office of Inspector General (HHS-OIG) and the Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI).

Trial Attorney Jody King of the Fraud Division’s Health Care Fraud Section and Assistant U.S. Attorney William Hamilton for the Middle District of Florida are prosecuting the case.

On April 7, the Department of Justice announced the creation of the Fraud Division. The Fraud Division is laser-focused on investigating and prosecuting those who commit fraud against the American people. The Department’s work to combat fraud supports President Trump’s Task Force to Eliminate Fraud, a whole-of-government effort chaired by Vice President J.D. Vance to eliminate fraud, waste, and abuse within Federal benefit programs.

The Department of Justice’s Health Care Fraud Strike Force Program, currently comprised of nine strike forces operating in federal districts across the country, has charged more than 6,200 defendants who collectively billed federal health care programs and private insurers more than $45 billion since 2007. In addition, the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services, working in conjunction with the Office of the Inspector General for the Department of Health and Human Services, are taking steps to hold providers accountable for their involvement in health care fraud schemes. More information can be found at www.justice.gov/criminal-fraud/health-care-fraud-unit.

#1094. Are Young People Overusing Retinol? Dermatologists Weigh In

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Experts explain when the ingredient helps, when it hurts, and why a simple routine may be enough.

Retinol is undoubtedly one of the greatest ingredient breakthroughs in skin care. The vitamin A derivative has powerful wrinkle- and texture-smoothing benefits, but too much of it or improper use can have pretty damaging effects on the skin barrier and overall skin health.

Alongside sunscreen, some form of retinol is often recommended by dermatologists, particularly for more mature skin. Milder doses also work well as a preventative ingredient in younger skin; its popularity across TikTok and other social platforms has raised the question of whether younger skin needs such a strong ingredient, and whether retinol is being overused. Derms have weighed in and offer alternatives that may be less damaging and more appropriate for an early skin care journey.

Dr. Kseniya Kobets, director of cosmetic dermatology at Montefiore Einstein Advanced Care, says some are. “Especially when social media makes them feel they should already be preventing wrinkles. Then they combine retinol, exfoliating toner, and acne treatments and wonder why everything burns. But retinoids can help acne, clogged pores, texture, and pigmentation, so for me it’s less about the birthday and more about the skin’s needs and appropriate use,” Kobets tells ELLE.

There’s no magic age as to when it’s safe to introduce retinol into a skin care routine. What’s most important is understanding its purpose and when your skin may need it.

Board-certified dermatologist and founder of Weiser Skin MD, Dr. Jessica Weiser, says retinol can benefit teens with acne and can be a useful prejuvenation product for people in their 20s and 30s—but it’s best to use it under professional guidance. Weiser notes that in general, retinol shouldn’t be used for antiaging purposes until your 20s or even early 30s. Age isn’t the only consideration either: “For patients with sensitive skin, retinol can be irritating and cause inflammation or dermatitis,” Weiser notes.

Kobets further explains that while retinol is part of the vitamin A family called retinoids, cosmetic retinol differs from acne medications. “OTC adapalene 0.1% is labeled for acne at 12 and older; younger children need clinician guidance. That doesn’t mean every 12-year-old needs it,” says Kobets.

Without a specific concern, Kobets recommends prioritizing cleanser, moisturizer, and sunscreen in your routine. “Adults can consider retinol for texture, pigmentation, or fine lines; you haven’t missed a deadline by not starting at 20,” Kobets adds.

Weiser and Kobets both say retinol has significant skin benefits, depending on what you’re looking to treat. “Retinol is very useful for a few purposes: collagen stimulation to prevent or soften fine lines and wrinkles and also for skin turnover and oil regulation,” Weiser describes. “In this sense, retinol can help to prevent wrinkles from worsening but cannot impact muscle movement and therefore cannot fully prevent lines and wrinkles or aging,” she clarifies.

The ingredient has been shown to support collagen and improve photodamage, although Kobets notes that prescription tretinoin has stronger evidence and OTC formulas vary in efficacy. “Acne-directed retinoids also help prevent clogged pores and breakouts,” says Kobets, adding that for sun-related changes, sunscreen, hats, and shade come first. “We don’t have evidence that children need retinol to prevent wrinkles.”

Perhaps it’s no coincidence that as retinol product options increase, so have solutions for skin barrier support and restoration. “Retinol can cause dryness, flaking, irritation, and skin redness; it can weaken the skin barrier,” says Weiser. Barrier disruption and inflammation can leave dark marks.

Additionally, it can cause irritant dermatitis: redness, dryness, peeling, burning, and stinging from normally tolerated products. “Appropriate use doesn’t permanently thin skin, but pushing through irritation makes things worse. Burning, rawness, or an itchy rash shouldn’t be dismissed as ‘purging,’” Kobets cautions.

Weiser also notes that the effects of retinol are cumulative and can take months or even years to be appreciated. “Retinol can exacerbate redness, rosacea, dermatitis/eczema, and more, so it should be used with caution in sensitive and redness-prone skin. Finally, retinol causes thinning of the epidermis (top skin layer), so it should be stopped before waxing and some types of laser treatment,” Weiser advises.

Key advice from Kobets and Weiser is to start slow. They both recommend using retinol twice a week at the beginning and titrating up as tolerated. Kobets specifies starting with a pea-sized amount for the face and avoiding eyelids, nose, and mouth corners unless the product is intended there.

You’ll also want to take extra caution on the neck and chest where, as Weiser points out, there are significantly fewer sebaceous glands, so skin can become more easily irritated and dry.

How you layer in a retinol can make a difference in lowering irritation, too. “Moisturizer can go before, after, or both; initially separate exfoliating acids onto other nights. Results take weeks to months, and collagen-related changes take longer,” says Kobets.

Additionally, for sensitive skin, a sandwich technique can be used where clean skin is treated first with moisturizer, then with retinol, and then with another layer of moisturizer to sandwich the retinol between two layers of emollients.

The good news is that irritation usually improves not long after you stop using a retinol product. If you do feel like your barrier is compromised and skin is more irritated or worse damage has set in, Kobets says to pause retinol, acids, and scrubs and replace them with a gentle cleanser, fragrance-free moisturizer with glycerin or ceramides, and sunscreen. A little petrolatum can help dry areas. Wait until comfortable before restarting more slowly, possibly with a different product. Swelling, blistering, crusting, or persistent irritation should be evaluated by a dermatologist. Also keep in mind that residual dark marks can take longer to fade. Weiser adds that barrier repair creams or lipid-replenishing creams can be useful.

As with any ingredient, choose based on the concern you want to address. “Healthy younger skin may only need cleansing, moisturizer, and sunscreen. Salicylic acid or benzoyl peroxide can help acne, and azelaic acid can address breakouts and pigmentation, but all can irritate,” says Kobets. So, just like retinol, start slow and see how your skin responds.

If you’ve already started using retinol and want a gentler alternative, Kobets also recommends bakuchiol, a plant-derived active with some retinol-like effects. It’s not actual retinol. However, she adds a caveat: the testing of bakuchiol to treat pigment issues has been rather limited, and broader analyses haven’t established clear pigment-reducing benefit. “It’s an option for some adults who don’t tolerate retinol, not an equally proven replacement, especially for pigmentation, or something children need. Plant-derived doesn’t mean allergy- or irritation-free,” Kobets clarifies.

#3331. We Tested the lululemon Align and LSKD Fusion Leggings—Here’s Why You Need Both Pairs

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

One is good for recovery runs, and the other is better for hard runs, strength training and more.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

Tights are a dime a dozen, but that doesn’t mean you should just grab any pair for running, or all the other workouts you do to support your habit. You need a few pieces that can flow with you on easy runs, and others that fit like a glove without limiting your movement during harder sessions. That’s where the lululemon Align High Rise Pant and LSKD Fusion Ultra High-Rise Legging fit in.

Both of these tights are good for runners, but for very different reasons. I tested each style for a month to see exactly how these popular pieces could fit into a runner’s everyday life. I switched between the two pairs while training for the Copenhagen Half Marathon. I used them on easy, hard, and long runs, storing my phone, gels, keys, and sometimes even a 16-ounce water bottle in their pockets. I also wore them on non-training days to see how they feel when I’m not exercising.

Three other Runner’s World testers gave them a try also. We rated them based on fit, comfort, breathability, sizing, and overall wear. What we found isn’t that one pair is necessarily better than the other, but that each tight works for a specific purpose like easy runs, yoga, strength training, and just hanging out.

When it comes to fit, fabric, and feel, the lululemon Align High-Rise Pant and the LSKD Fusion Ultra High Rise Legging have a few things in common. Both tights are very comfortable. All of our testers compared them to feeling soft as butter, even though they’re made out of completely different fabrics.

The lululemon Align pants are made of mostly nylon blended with Lycra elastane. I was skeptical of this combination because I think of stiff and rubbery swimwear when I hear the word nylon. These lululemon pants are far from stiff. They’re plush and lightweight with four-way stretch, so you can move in them freely.

Amanda Furrer, running reviews editor, loves how the fabric doesn’t feel like a bathing suit, too. She said, “[The lululemon pants are] smooth and soft, and I feel like I could wear them all day because of their comfort.” The only issue is they’re more generously sized than other tights made by lululemon. I have the Fast and Free Tight and the Wunder Train Tight, both in a size 8. They fit me well. I have the Align in the same size, but the Aligns tend to slip down if I put anything in the pockets, and they’re loose around the ankles.

The LSKD Fusion Legging is similarly soft and comfortable, but these leggings are tighter and hug your body a bit more. They’re super flattering, and our testers thought it had a lifting effect on their butts, which was much appreciated. The blend of recycled polyester and spandex feels good on my skin, and they didn’t irritate our testers much either. They fit snugly, especially around the ankle, and that makes them harder to pull up. But that’s by design. They’re compressive—not restrictive—so you can still stretch and run in them. I also noticed they get softer and the fabric has more give after washing them.

Having a good pair of leggings that won’t slip down while running or fold over when sitting is a non-negotiable for me. I want to stay focused on running, not distracted because I’m constantly adjusting gear. I also want pieces I can wear for multiple activities, not just running since I spend a good chunk of time stretching and strength training. That’s why I like both the LSKD legging and the lululemon Align tight.

The LSKD leggings are multifunctional, designed for running, walking, Pilates, functional training, and HIIT. The compressive tight is stretchy and non-restrictive, and that’s what I like most about them. I have an older pair of these that I refuse to get rid of because they fit me so well. I wear them for outdoor runs in the fall and winter. The fabric is thick and warm. Ashley Tysiac, content hype editor, likes these no-fuss pants for runs and strength training sessions, too. “Not once did I have to pull up the leggings back to my waist midrun, nor did I notice slipping or movement of material while running and strength training,” she said.

You can wear the lululemon Align tights for yoga, and they feel good on easy runs when you’re not carrying much. The four-way stretch fabric feels weightless, so you can get a good stretch in them before a run. I wear these on easy runs and recovery days when my muscles feel tight and I can’t imagine wearing jeans. The plush fabric makes me feel cozy. Furrer likes them because the waistband doesn’t dig into your stomach, or make you feel like you’re wearing a corset. Both tights dry fairly quickly after a workout; however, I like that I can easily slip out of the Align tights with no problem, whereas the LSKD legging requires significantly more wiggling to remove.

For runners, both of these pieces will earn their keep in any closet. If you’re shopping for tights, it’s better to stock up on pairs that you can make work for specific workouts. You can grab the LSKD legging for long runs in the cold and lululemon for your yoga session or recovery run the next day. The LSKD leggings are also good for high-intensity workouts, so your hard runs and that random HIIT class you signed up for are kitted out, while the Align is good for those easy miles and slower days. But we do recommend you grab one of each.

#4081. Yes, You Can Help Prevent Dementia

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We’re all holding onto something we’re sure we can’t give up on, but research shows that letting go can boost our happiness a lot more than we might think.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted October 27, 2026 | Reviewed by Devon Frye

More than 57 million people worldwide live with dementia, and nearly 10 million are diagnosed each year, according to the World Health Organization (WHO). Alzheimer's disease accounts for an estimated 60 to 70 percent of these cases. Although there is no cure for dementia or Alzheimer’s disease, a growing body of research suggests that a there are steps everyone can take to help with prevention.

In July, WHO released the second edition of its guidelines on reducing the risk of cognitive decline and dementia. The main finding: up to 45 percent of dementia risk can be attributed to factors that we can control.

It’s also important to note there are some preventative interventions WHO does not recommend the use of vitamin B and E supplements, omega-3 fatty acids, or multivitamins for dementia prevention in people without a diagnosed deficiency.

In addition to the new recommendations from WHO, the U.S. National Institutes of Health recently published a 2026 progress report on Alzheimer's and related forms of dementia. The report highlights several studies that focus on prevention including:

The new findings echo the advice of psychologist and neuroscientist Marlen Z. Gonzalez, executive director of the Community Neuroscience Initiative (CNI), a project at Cornell’s Bronfenbrenner Center for Translational Research that seeks to make neuroscience more accessible and helpful.

Gonzalez offers four evidence-based steps that anyone can take to promote better brain health. “We’ve been calling these ‘grandma’s rules,’” Gonzalez said. “That’s because your grandma probably told you to do these things first. But now neuroscience supports it.”

The take-home message: The body of evidence continues to demonstrate that lifestyle choices that include exercise, sleep, keeping your brain active and controlling high blood pressure, influence dementia risk.

#1105. 9 Death-Inspired Runway Collections to Get You in the Halloween Spirit

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Fashion can be a lot of things—glamorous, elegant, cool, fun, sexy—but can it also be…scary? An interesting thought to consider as Halloween approaches. And the answer is, well, of course. As one of life’s great mysteries, it’s no big surprise that fashion does grapple, from time to time, with ideas of death and the afterlife.

“I have always found it interesting that death often reveals something profoundly human. It can bring people closer to their most authentic selves,” says French creative director, costume designer, and hair and makeup artist Charlie Le Mindu. “The prospect of losing everything changes our understanding of what is important. I sometimes wonder why we need to approach the end of life to begin living with that level of honesty.”

But Le Mindu is hardly the only designer to have confronted their own fears of death through fashion—designers have long used this medium to wrestle with what is paradoxically both life’s only guarantee and one of its greatest mysteries. Across cultures and eras, fashion has served as a means of reflecting on this universal human experience, giving tangible form to the otherwise incomprehensible. From Ann Demeulemeester’s spring 2019 gothic veils inspired by story of L’Inconnue de la Seine to Alexander McQueen’s spring 2008 tribute to his late friend and champion, Isabella Blow, some have drawn on the visual language of funerary dress, while others have transformed the runway into meditations on grief. Here, we revisit nine collections that explore the many ways fashion has confronted death—and, in turn, what it means to be alive.

“It is important to look at death because it is a part of life,” Alexander McQueen told Nick Knight in a 2009 SHOWstudio interview. Few designers embraced that fascination more fervently than McQueen, whose work repeatedly grappled with mortality, violence, religion, and the afterlife, particularly in his eighth runway collection, Dante, which propelled him to international acclaim. Named after the Italian poet whose Divine Comedy journeys through hell, purgatory, and Paradise, the fall 1996 collection explored the intersection of religion, warfare, and historical mourning traditions. Dedicated to his close friend and muse, Isabella Blow, the show unfolded on a crucifix-shaped runway inside London’s candlelit Christ Church Spitalfields, where a skeleton watched from the front row as the spectacle unfolded to a haunting soundtrack punctuated by gunfire. McQueen juxtaposed religious imagery with the brutality of war, incorporating Don McCullin’s photographs of the Vietnam War into garments alongside ecclesiastical tailoring, bumster trousers, and thornlike headpieces. Among the most striking pieces was a lilac silk faille corset embellished with black lace and jet beading, drawing directly on the colors and materials of Victorian mourning dress.

A decade later, McQueen’s fall 2006 collection, The Widows of Culloden, painted historical loss through an elegiac runway that he described as “romantic but melancholic and austere at the same time.” A tribute to the women widowed by the 1746 Battle of Culloden, which devastated the Scottish Highlands, the deeply personal collection drew on McQueen’s Scottish ancestry through family tartans, black lace, feathers, and mourning-inspired silhouettes. Its haunting finale featured a spectral Kate Moss floating inside a glass pyramid, conjured through a 19th-century illusion known as Pepper’s Ghost. The collection exemplified what curator Andrew Bolton has described as McQueen’s gift for “finding poetry and beauty in death,” a sensibility that permeated his work throughout his career.

Charlie Le Mindu’s fall 2010 collection, Sex Religion Bullshit Lucifer, grappled with his own mortality during a period of deteriorating health. “Rather than approaching death as something tragic, I became interested in the strange freedom that comes with accepting its inevitability,” recalls the French hairstylist and designer, who has also collaborated with brands including Balmain, Mugler, and Vivienne Westwood, and styled artists such as Lady Gaga, Cindy Sherman, Julia Fox, and more. “I wanted to celebrate it, not because I wished to die, but because I had never felt such an urgency to live.” Questioning the religious and moral structures that teach us to fear death, Le Mindu embraced Lucifer as a symbol of society’s anxieties surrounding desire and disobedience through his signature haute coiffure, in a collection of hair constructions, towering jeweled crosses, and mourning veils. Among its most striking creations was an oversized black lacquered rose fascinator, which Gaga wore later in the year. “Something that began with the possibility of dying ultimately changed the way I approached being alive,” he reflects. “The collection was never really about death itself, but about the extraordinary value we give to life once we understand that it is finite. I wonder why we need to approach the end of life to begin living with that level of honesty.”

For her fall 2011 haute couture collection, Capriole, Dutch designer Iris van Herpen found inspiration in the sensation of free-falling during a parachute jump. Exploring the tension between freedom and fear in a moment suspended between life and death, the 19-look collection featured a striking white 3D-printed skeleton dress, developed in collaboration with architect Isaïe Bloch and Belgian 3D-printing company Materialise. Constructed from laser-sintered polyamide, its bonelike structure enveloped the body like an exoskeleton. “This dress visualizes the inside of the body outside,” van Herpen explained to Vogue. “To me it represents freedom and imperfection, and between those two is where beauty can be found.” Later acquired by the Metropolitan Museum of Art and featured in its “Manus x Machina” exhibition, and now on view at “Iris van Herpen: Sculpting the Senses” at the Brooklyn Museum, the piece transformed a traditional symbol of mortality into an expression of liberation.

For fall 2012, Thom Browne took the expression “dying for fashion” literally, staging an elaborate funeral tableau complete with flickering candles and 10 coffins containing, as an officiant announced, “10 beautiful girls who died for fashion.” The models subsequently emerged from their coffins, resurrected by their love of clothing, in extravagant ensembles featuring exaggerated hourglass silhouettes, embroidery, and gilded embellishments. Juxtaposing the solemnity of Victorian mourning with his characteristically surreal theatricality, Browne transformed the runway into a narrative about fashion’s power to bring its devotees back to life.

For Comme des Garçons’ fall 2015 collection, The Ceremony of Separation, Rei Kawakubo explored an abstract interpretation of loss through 18 sculptural looks in black, white, and gold, colors associated with burial across different traditions. Models approached, passed, and separated from one another as though enacting a final parting between the living and the dead in voluminous constructions of intricate lace, oversized bows, and densely layered fabrics that enveloped the body. Among the standout looks were a white dress resembling bundles of bedsheets tied with bows, a tiered gold lace structure recalling religious vestments, and a black ensemble adorned with miniature Victorian-style children’s dresses and bonnets. The show culminated in a voluminous black tulle cocoon, its shroudlike silhouette reinforcing the collection’s meditation on mortality.

For Ann Demeulemeester’s spring 2019 collection, creative director Sébastien Meunier found inspiration in the story of L’Inconnue de la Seine, a mysterious young woman allegedly pulled from the Seine in the late 19th century, whose death mask has fascinated artists for generations. Meunier, who keeps a replica of the mask in his apartment, translated his fascination with her into a hauntingly romantic collection of diaphanous, nightdress-like gowns, fluid tailoring, and trailing silhouettes. Models walked with their faces obscured by veils in billowing black-and-white dresses, gauzy blouses, and long coats with an unfinished, waterlogged finish. Some of the most recognized looks were a sheer white dress layered beneath leather straps and chains, pale pink gowns cinched with harnesses, and tailored jackets adorned with clusters of flowers, evoking the spectral beauty of the drowned woman. “We don’t know a lot about her, so you can dream up lots of things and transpose your own world on it,” Meunier told Vogue.

Alessandro Michele staged Gucci’s cruise 2019 show, which explored what he described as “the idea of death as fascination,” after dark in the Alyscamps, an ancient Roman necropolis in Arles, France. The burial ground was transformed into what the house called a “party in a cemetery” against a backdrop of flames, tolling bells, and stone sarcophagi, and models appeared as mourning widows in velvet gowns and capes alongside skeleton-embroidered garments and leggings bearing the word memento mori. Among the most memorable ensembles were a black gown with a skeletal rib cage embroidered across the torso and a floral cape paired with a cathedral-like headdress, injecting funerary imagery into Michele’s signature maximalism.

Named after a river of the Greek underworld depicted in Dante’s Inferno as a torrent of boiling blood, Rick Owens’s Phlegethon visualized the anxieties of the pandemic and environmental crisis. Presented amid fog and flashing lights outside Venice’s Lido Casino, the dystopian collection featured towering thigh-high platform boots resembling waders, an allusion to both the mythical river and the city’s rising waters. Armor-like shoulders, oversized sleeveless jackets, and leather micro-shorts contrasted with sheer silk chiffon, fishnet dresses, and sculptural tulle ruffles. Against a largely black palette, flashes of alarm red and bubblegum pink heightened the collection’s apocalyptic ethos. Owens described the imposing shoulders as an “exaggerated middle finger to doom,” framing the collection as an act of defiance. “In the face of adversity…we have to pull ourselves up,” he said.

Drawing on the Greek myth of Orpheus and Eurydice, Jackson Wiederhoeft’s fall 2023 collection imagined a subversive afterlife in which the heroine refuses to be rescued from the underworld. “In our version of the story, Eurydice chooses hell,” said the designer. “We explore a post-Orpheus, post-gender underworld where interpretations of ancient Greek and Catholic mythologies meet at the intersection of Virgil’s Aeneid. A Venn diagram of fanaticism, fate, fear. Afterparty versus afterlife, prey versus predator, friend versus foe, fate versus free will. Fate as a consequence of free will.” Staged as an operatic spectacle, the collection opened with a voluminous white gown fashioned from plasticized taffeta, its sheer bodice revealing a tightly cinched corset beneath. Shimmering gold-sequined dresses, corseted denim, and a coat embroidered with stained-glass-like sequins further blurred the boundaries between gothic romance and extravagance, while sheer slip dresses featured hand-embroidered, tattoo-like imagery referencing the myth of Orpheus and other Catholic iconography.

#954. Why These Athletes Don’t Stress Over Sleep Scores

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

We can track almost everything about our health today—our pace on a run, our heart rate, our heart rate variability (HRV) and, of course, our sleep (or lack thereof). But what are fitness trackers actually calculating about shut-eye?

Most, including Whoop, don’t directly measure sleep (wearables don’t have the same capabilities as a kitted-out, in-hospital sleep lab). Instead, they use readings of things like movement, heart rate and HRV to estimate when we’re asleep and awake, then turn all of that information into a so-called sleep score.

That reading can help estimate how long we slept, how consistent our sleep has been, how much time we spent in different sleep stages, and how the night compares with what our body typically needs. Having all that data at your fingertips can be incredibly useful. But can obsessing over the number (especially before a big morning) sometimes do more harm than good?

We spoke to some of the world’s leading Hyrox and CrossFit athletes about their relationship with sleep tracking, and why they don’t always let the numbers have the final say.

According to the American Academy of Sleep Medicine 48% of adults in the US have used a sleep tracking device to monitor their sleep quality and duration. (And in fact men are more likely to do so than women.) But also: 76% of adults have been kept up at night worried about not getting enough sleep.

A poor sleep score doesn’t necessarily mean you’re going to have a poor day. So while sleep is extremely important, especially for high-performers, the pressure to achieve a “perfect” night can be counterproductive. There’s even a name for it: orthosomnia, which refers to an obsession with achieving a high rating on a smart device. You might wake up feeling fine, check your score, see that it’s not great, and suddenly start worrying that you actually slept poorly.

A 2024 study of 523 people found that people showing signs of orthosomnia also reported more severe symptoms of insomnia. That's a problem. If one bad score is enough to change your mood, knock your confidence, or make you rethink your training, the number is starting to have too much influence on your routine. For athletes used to training and competing under fatigue, and traveling across time zones, sleep is only one part of the recovery picture.

That doesn’t mean you should throw your Whoop in the bin. The number can tell you something about your sleep, but it shouldn’t be the deciding factor in how you start your day.

All this raises an obvious question: What if the best thing you can do with your sleep score is occasionally ignore it? Some athletes, it seems, have arrived at that solution.

We asked Aniol Ekai, the top men's CrossFit athlete in Spain this year, what he would do if he woke up to a poor sleep score. His answer was surprisingly simple: he wouldn't believe it. “I don’t want a number telling me how I’m going to feel or how I’m going to perform that day,” he says. “Whether it [is accurate] or not almost doesn’t matter.”

By refusing to let a sleep score dictate how he feels, Ekai creates a useful bit of distance between what his tracker says and how he actually performs. “I don't check my scores a week before a big competition, because I don't want another thing to think about, including any kind of stress, unless it's life or death," he says.

Charlie Botterill, currently ranked fourth in the world in Hyrox men’s singles, says sleep has become a much bigger focus for him this year, with a few simple habits making the difference: keeping his phone out of the bedroom for at least two hours before bed, waking up at the same time each day, and making his bedroom a better place to sleep. “Based on how much I train throughout the day, my cortisol will naturally be more elevated in the evenings, which is why keeping my phone away from the bedroom can help me sleep,” he says.

But even with those practices in place, staying asleep isn’t always easy. And while a disrupted night can affect his sleep score, Botterill doesn’t allow the number to determine how he approaches the day. He checks his score, but not every morning, and takes a deliberately relaxed approach to what it tells him.

People over 40 are now the fastest-growing group at Hyrox fitness competitions, reshaping what middle age looks like for the wellness-obsessed gym rat. One Midwestern regional sales director leads us through this year’s event.

Manuela García Caparrós, a Hyrox Pro athlete and former Elite 15 competitor, takes a similarly measured approach. She tracks her sleep with Garmin and Luna, but doesn’t let the data dictate her training. “If I get a bad sleep score, I always compare it with how I actually feel,” she says. Sometimes the data backs up her perception; sometimes it doesn’t. If she feels tired and the stats agree, she might reduce her training volume or prioritize recovery. But one bad night isn’t enough to change the plan. “I pay much more attention to trends over time than to a single bad night of sleep," she says. “I never make decisions based on the numbers alone.”

So yes, wearables can be hugely powerful—being able to track your recovery, strain, and sleep can give you a pretty good idea of what’s going on with your body, especially if you know what to do with the numbers. But after speaking to some of the world's top-ranked fitness athletes about how much they actually rely on their sleep scores, something else became clear: The data should never be the final word.

Instead, it’s best to look at the bigger picture. How do you feel? Are you focused? Do you have energy? And, perhaps most importantly, how does training actually feel? Tracking our sleep is useful, but it’s still just one piece of the puzzle.

A version of this story originally appeared in British GQ.

#3351. On Wants You to Feel Your Softest Ride Yet

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The Cloudsurfer 3 is built to make everyday runs feel smooth and effortless, even as the miles add up.

Just because you run doesn’t necessarily mean that you’re up on running shoe innovation. In recent years, there have been notable improvements in propulsion and efficiency, thanks to additions like carbon-fiber plates, smart sensors, and midsoles that promise high energy return. What’s more important than these buzzy words is: How do the shoes feel?

After all, you can have footwear designed by rocket scientists, but if you’re uncomfortable with every step, your run will be a slog, no matter the distance. You want your shoe to make the ride feel soft, which is why the On Cloudsurfer 3 stands out over so many others.

On running shoes have always been distinguished by their trademark hollow pods— the “clouds” that are specifically engineered to help create a softer underfoot experience and give the brand its unmistakable look. In the Cloudsurfer 3, On’s CloudTec Phase™ allows those pods to compress in sequence, helping absorb impact and create a smoother transition from heel to toe. This isn’t just another update to On’s running shoe line. With breakthrough Swiss engineering working beneath every step, the Cloudsurfer 3 delivers the brand’s softest ride yet.

An option for everyday and recovery runs as well as longer training sessions, the Cloudsurfer 3 combines CloudTec® Phase with On’s brand-new SurrealTM Superfoam. According to the brand’s internal testing, the pairing provides 15.5 percent deeper compression—and therefore more impact absorption—than the superfoam alone. The result is a softer landing that can help daily miles feel easier on the legs, particularly over long distances.

According to the same internal testing, the Cloudsurfer 3 is also predicted to deliver a 9.9 percent improvement in smooth-ride sensation. That figure reflects the way the reimagined CloudTec® Phase orientation enhances the rolling motion of the foot, helping each step flow more naturally into the next from landing to toe-off.

You’re also getting a newly engineered mesh upper in the Cloudsurfer 3, which provides a secure fit that adapts to your foot while still offering breathability and support. That breathability helps keep heat and moisture from building up, reducing the conditions that can lead to chafing, blisters, and hot spots.

Even the cushioning around the collar and tongue has been updated, so you get a cloud-like feel as soon as you slip them on. It doesn’t hurt that the Cloudsurfer 3 looks cool, too.

Together, these innovations give runners what they want from an everyday shoe: a natural feel, reliable performance, and lasting comfort. The result is a smoother ride, whether you’re going long or heading out for a quick training run.

That softer feel is a big deal, considering a 2025 study found that runners who perceived their shoes as more cushioned had a lower risk of running-related injury. When a shoe feels right, you’re less likely to think about every step—or adjust the way you’re moving to compensate for discomfort. That allows you to settle into your natural stride and focus on the run itself.

Enjoyment is a major factor, too. Wearing a shoe like the Cloudsurfer 3, with its focus on cushioning, responsiveness, and comfort, can be key to unlocking your best run yet—and enjoying yourself along the way. When that happens, even a hard run can feel softer, mile after mile.

Elizabeth Millard is a freelance writer focusing on health, wellness, fitness, and food.

#1118. How to Get Better REM Sleep, According to a Sleep Specialist

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

There’s no magic biohack, but a few expert-backed habits can help you get more restorative sleep.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

On the pyramid of wellness and overall health priorities, sleep very often gets bumped to the bottom. Despite constant reminders like dark circles, puffiness, fatigue, and low energy, many have difficulty sticking to a consistent sleep schedule. Or, if they do manage to get to bed at a decent hour, sleep quality is compromised. Much like other daily routines, a person’s sleep patterns are personal, but recent studies show that consistently getting to a REM snooze state has significant benefits beyond the bed. Dr. W. Christopher Winter, a board-certified neurologist and sleep medicine specialist, explains why REM sleep is crucial to long-term health and how to get the most of each night.

Winter explains that sleep happens in three stages: light sleep, deep sleep, and dream sleep. rapid eye movement (REM) is dream sleep. “Deep sleep and REM often get lumped together, but they’re almost opposite states,” he says. “Deep sleep is quiet body, quiet brain. REM is quiet body, active brain.” In this state, he describes, your brain is nearly as active as when you’re awake, but your muscles are essentially paralyzed so you don’t act out your dreams.

We cycle through the three stages several times a night, roughly every 90 minutes, notes Winter. Deep sleep dominates the first part of the night, and REM gets longer and more concentrated toward the morning. Regarding how sleep habits and this level of insight sleep specialists have been able to home in on, Winter says, “We have come a very long way since Eugene Aserinsky figured out REM sleep from the basement of the University of Chicago, messing around with an old brain wave machine on his 8-year-old son.”

The term “experience” is subjective in the sleep field. “Most people move in and out of REM sleep without perceiving or experiencing it,” says Winter. “That said, even in those who 'don't dream...or remember it,' they are experiencing REM sleep, probably four to five times each night.” If you're sleeping a normal amount, meaning more than a couple of hours, Winter says you're getting REM. If your marker for REM is remembering a dream or whether or not you had one at all, Winter explains that there's a difference between not having dreams and not remembering them. “For people who truly don't seem to dream or experience REM, it's likely because something is disrupting their REM sleep. That could be alcohol, certain medications, or a sleep disorder like sleep apnea.” According to Winter, sleep apnea patients often have very disturbed REM sleep, which can lead to memory and cognitive issues, more pain, and brain fog.

Winter says., “We spend about half the night in light sleep. The other half is some mix of deep sleep and dream sleep, about 50/50, so REM ends up being roughly a quarter of the night. “The first REM period is short, maybe 10 minutes, and later ones can stretch close to an hour. That’s why cutting your night short, with an early alarm, for example, cuts disproportionately into REM. Unlike deep sleep, which steadily shrinks as we age, REM tends to hold steadier across adulthood,” Winter says

REM is more about concentration, focus, and memory. “It’s a little more opaque than deep sleep. If you don't get enough REM, you’ll get through your day okay. You're just going to put your car keys in your refrigerator. That little injury you got at the gym is going to bother you more. You might have more depressive thoughts or lack the motivation to do something,” Winter shares. He describes sleep as similar to a balanced diet: Protein isn’t necessarily more important than carbohydrates. It just does something different in the body. The goal, he says, is properly balanced sleep at night. “For the athletes I work with, REM is sneaky important...it's the difference between a base hit and a foul ball...or a perfect bound pass and one that goes out of bounds just out of reach from a teammate,” he adds.

Overall, poor sleep has many negative implications. Winter warns that inconsistent REM can affect the cardiovascular system, mood, hormones, blood sugar, and weight, and it can cause inflammation. “The list is almost infinite, so it's not surprising to see a study like this. Researchers followed more than 95,000 people for about nine years. Those who got more REM had a lower risk of 83 conditions, including heart failure, dementia, and Parkinson’s disease,” Winter summarizes.

While these findings are insightful, Winter says to keep two important factors in mind: “First, it's an association, not proof that more REM prevents disease. Second, sleep stages were estimated from a wrist device worn for one week,” he points out. The popularity of biometric wearable devices has peaked; Winter cautions against leaning too heavily into the data gathered from them. “I like trackers, but staging sleep is a tough thing for them to do. My takeaway is simple: People with the lowest risks were sleeping six to eight hours, and the highest risk was among people sleeping under five [hours]. Protect your total sleep, and REM tends to follow,” says Winter.

Findings from this study, he says, are not surprising. “When you think of how foundational sleep is, it should surprise nobody. It's like the Super Size Me experiment...When an individual eats poorly, it would not be a shock to see obesity, diabetes, cholesterol, heart disease, mood, etc. worsen. Sleep is just as foundational, so when it falls apart, many things downstream follow suit.”

Dreams are the biggest clue as to whether or not you’re reaping the benefits of REM sleep. If you wake up in the middle of a vivid dream, Winter says you were probably in REM. “Many of us naturally wake up out of REM in the morning, which is why morning dreams are the ones we remember. But not remembering a dream doesn't mean you didn't have one, because anything from your alarm to checking your phone can wipe a dream away,” Winter notes.

Sleep trackers can show trends, and Winter points out that they're good at telling you how much you sleep, but he says to take the exact REM numbers with a grain of salt. “The better test is how you function. Are you focused, is your mood steady, are your car keys in the right place?”

Inconsistent sleep patterns are worth seeing a doctor for, but Winter also says that if your sleep is fragmented or if you’re suddenly dreaming more, that’s also a good time to speak to a sleep specialist to see if something deeper is going on.

REM itself isn't bad, and as already established, it's essential. However, according to Winter, nightmares mostly happen in REM. Sleep apnea is often worse during REM because our muscles are so relaxed. “Sleep paralysis happens when that REM muscle paralysis lingers for a moment as you wake up,” he explains. “It's frightening, but usually harmless. There’s also REM sleep behavior disorder, where that paralysis doesn't work, and people physically act out their dreams. That one is worth telling a doctor about,” says Winter.

Certain things can help you get a better night’s rest, but Winter says you can’t really force REM. “There's no magic device or biohack. What you can do is protect the conditions for it.” Pick a wake-up time and stick with it, even on weekends. Seek bright light in the morning and dim the lights in the evening. Keep in mind that alcohol is very disruptive for sleep, so avoid it close to bedtime.

If you have insomnia, Winter says try not to chase sleep. “The secret to great sleep is being as happy in bed awake as you are asleep. If you snore or wake up gasping, get checked for sleep apnea, because it often fragments REM. For chronic insomnia, CBT-I, a talk therapy specifically for insomnia, is the first-line treatment,” Winter concludes.

Jump-start better sleep hygiene with a few wind-down essentials.

#671. An extra year of school may lower dementia risk, even when genes raise it

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A decades-old school reform offers new evidence that additional education may reduce dementia risk, even among people with a higher genetic risk.

In 1972, Britain changed how long teenagers had to stay in school.

More than 50 years later, researchers found a possible benefit that reached well beyond the classroom: fewer dementia diagnoses among people covered by the rule.

The biggest drop appeared among people whose genes put them at higher risk.

The finding suggests that education could help change someone’s chances of developing dementia, even when they inherit a greater risk.

Silvia Barcellos, a health economist at the University of Wisconsin–Madison’s La Follette School of Public Affairs, led the study.

She is also affiliated with the University of Southern California’s Center for Economic and Social Research, where three of her four coauthors work.

Researchers often list low education as a major risk factor for dementia. People who spend more years in school tend to have better memory and thinking skills later in life, and fewer of them develop dementia.

The catch is that those same people often differ in other ways.

They may have grown up with more money or carry different genes, and even a trait like self-control could shape both how long someone studies and how they live afterward.

“We have known for a long time that people with more education are less likely to develop dementia, but it has been difficult to know whether education itself is responsible,” Barcellos said.

With dementia cases worldwide projected to reach 131.5 million by 2050, knowing whether extra schooling actually lowers risk matters for dementia prevention.

The 1972 law raised the minimum school-leaving age from 15 to 16 in England, Scotland and Wales. It applied to students born on or after September 1, 1957.

Someone born on August 31 could leave at 15, while someone born a day later had to stay another year.

Comparing people born close to that birthday helped the researchers separate the effect of schooling from other differences in their lives.

Most teenagers already stayed until 16. Still, the first group covered by the law was 14 percentage points more likely to do so than students born just before the cutoff.

Across the group covered by the law, schooling increased by a little over two months on average. That average includes people who would have stayed anyway.

The team analyzed up to 107,200 people born within four years of the cutoff.

They came from UK Biobank, a study of 502,363 volunteers who enrolled between 2006 and 2010, aged 40 to 69.

The researchers checked whether people on either side of the birthday cutoff were comparable. Their parents were equally likely to have had dementia, and the participants had similar genetic risks.

Dementia diagnoses came from hospital records, death records and participants’ own medical histories.

Hospital and death records allow researchers to follow people who stop answering surveys, something people developing dementia are more likely to do.

An earlier check found that at least 84.5% of the recorded diagnoses were correct. The diagnoses also showed links with blood proteins associated with dementia.

Among people born before the cutoff, about five in every 1,000 had a dementia diagnosis by December 2022. Those born just afterward had roughly two fewer diagnoses per 1,000.

That sounds like a small difference, but dementia was still uncommon at these ages.

Because the law added only a few months of schooling on average, the researchers also estimated the effect of a full extra year.

Their preferred estimate was a drop of about 0.88 percentage points per year of education.

The researchers said this was too large to be realistic, because it exceeded the group’s dementia rate to begin with.

The team cautioned that the estimate was imprecise because relatively few participants had developed dementia.

The findings suggest a protective effect, but its exact size remains uncertain.

The researchers gave each participant a genetic risk score combining many small DNA differences linked to Alzheimer’s disease with the APOE gene.

People who inherit two copies of the APOE4 variant face a substantially higher risk of developing Alzheimer’s disease.

In the higher-risk half, 58% carried at least one APOE4 copy, compared with 0.02% in the lower-risk half. The law could not change anyone’s genes, and it increased schooling by the same amount in both groups.

The gap in dementia diagnoses between the two groups fell from 0.18 percentage points before the cutoff to 0.02 points afterward.

The decline was clear only in the higher-risk group, but there were too few cases to rule out an equal benefit in both groups.

Study co-author Leandro Carvalho is an associate professor of economics at USC.

“For people who know they have a higher genetic risk of dementia, this is a hopeful finding,” said Carvalho.

“Genes help shape disease risk, but they do not necessarily determine the outcome.”

The study could not establish exactly how additional schooling might lower dementia risk.

The authors suggested that it might lead to more mentally stimulating jobs, higher incomes that make healthy living easier, or spending time with healthier peers.

People covered by the law were 2.3 percentage points more likely to have a household income of at least £31,000.

They were also 0.64 percentage points less likely to have had a stroke or heart attack, with that drop concentrated in the higher genetic risk group.

However, they were more likely to have the mildest stage of high blood pressure, a finding members of the team had reported previously.

A Swedish study of 1.3 million people found that a similar school reform had no effect on dementia and little effect on income. The British reform raised incomes, which the authors suggested might help explain its different results.

The oldest people covered by the law were only 65 at the end of 2022, before the ages when dementia becomes most common.

UK Biobank volunteers tend to be healthier and better off than the wider population, and the genetic analysis included only people of European ancestry.

The findings also concern people who would otherwise have left school early, so they may not apply to other levels of education or other places.

The difference in diagnoses grew clearer as the researchers added each of the last four years of health records.

“Understanding which interventions can produce these protective effects is an important direction for future research,” Carvalho said.

Following these participants for longer will help researchers see whether the difference holds as they reach ages when dementia becomes more common.

The full study was published in the journal Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences.

#1185. This Is Your Brain on Weed. It’s Actually Kind of Amazing.

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

WHEN YOU USE weed, your brain kind of goes all over the place. You likely know about how marijuana can chill you out, make you forget yourself, and induce the munchies. But there are other fascinating ways that the drug can alter your mind.

When THC binds to receptors in your hippocampus, the compound disrupts signals and impairs memory. That’s why you may forget what you were saying mid-sentence or where you put your keys when stoned.

THC also binds to receptors in the amygdala, which can help you feel chill when high—and paranoid or anxious when you’re too high.

The back of the brain, the cerebellum, is important for balance, motor skills, and time perception. When THC gets involved, you might feel clumsier and as if time is slowing.

THC disrupts the prefrontal cortex, which oversees critical thinking, problem-solving, and processing information from your senses. As a result, you’re going to find it harder to multitask, feel more impulsive, and zone out.

THC also stimulates the release of feel-good dopamine in the nucleus accumbens. If you’re a chronic, heavy user, cannabis can dull dopamine response and potentially rewire circuitry within the nucleus accumbens, making you more susceptible to addiction.

Your prefrontal cortex and limbic system contribute to imaginative ideas. THC can bind to receptors in these regions and may reduce self-critical noise, allowing for greater free expression.

Scientists believe that the anterior cingulate cortex is central to the perception of pain, and THC seems to dampen its ability—in a good way.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

Jocelyn Solis-Moreira, MS is the associate health & fitness for Men's Health and has previously written for CNN, Scientific American, Popular Science, and National Geographic before joining the brand. When she's not working, she's doing circus arts or working towards the perfect pull-up.

#1194. Weed Addiction Is a Real Thing. The World's Leading Cannabis Researcher Explains Why.

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

KEVIN HILL, MD, the guy when it comes to this stuff. He’s the chief of the Division of Addiction Psychiatry at Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center. We talked to him about all the questions you probably have about weed and addiction.

Dr. Hill: Absolutely. Most people who use cannabis don’t develop a cannabis use disorder or cannabis addiction. But there has always been a subset of individuals who do. Five years ago, the data indicated about 10 to 30 percent of adults develop a cannabis use disorder. Now the published data says 30 to 35 percent. With the rise in potency, we’re seeing a rise in the number of users who are developing problems.

MH: Does cannabis use disorder differ from other addictions?

Dr. Hill: It affects a lot of the same neurotransmitters that other substances of abuse might affect. If you use cannabis, you’re going to have a surge in dopamine. Similarly, if you use opioids, you’re going to have a surge in dopamine, but the scale of it is different. It’s why a lot of medications we use to treat addictions operate on common pathways.

MH: Is there a genetic risk of developing a weed addiction?

Dr. Hill: Yes. The genetic aspects of cannabis use disorder are probably less well developed than for other addictions, but if people have a family history of addiction of any kind, then it makes them more likely to have another substance use disorder, which could be cannabis.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

#1202. 30 Years of Legal Weed: Has It Made Us Any Healthier?

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Our product picks are editor-tested, expert-approved. We may earn a commission through links on our site. Why Trust Us?

The very highs and bizarre lows of America on medicinal marijuana.

EARLIER THIS YEAR, during a brief and mercifully abandoned period when I was considering doing stand-up as a hobby, I went to a comedy show in downtown Austin. I was legitimately shocked to find, as part of the two-drink minimum bill of fare, the option for several different flavors of $5 THC seltzers. This wasn’t “take a pull off your vape or smoke a roach in the alley before the show” territory. No, marijuana drinks were on the menu.

In the past few years, my only social activities have been poker, competitive trivia, and going to the movies, all activities people do stoned, but not activities where you can literally order drugs from the bar alongside your chicken wings. The comedy club, on the other hand, considered marijuana products as a profit center.

This was one of those lightly attended weeknight shows where the comedians banter with the audience because they haven’t prepared much material. So, during a Q&A, I brought up the fact that there were THC sodas available to order, in laminate. “Sir, this is 2026,” the host said.

This is where we are now, a full three decades after California legalized medical weed. But is that good for us?

As I write this, it’s been eight-and-a-half years since I quit marijuana, a drug that at one point made pretty much anything—listening to music, shopping for groceries, cleaning the house—fun for me.

But really, I’ve been sober since I stopped using THC, since I was hardly just smoking there at the end. I was vaping, eating, drinking, dabbing, and otherwise gorging myself on what has become an extremely potent, psychosis-inducing drug. And I wasn’t having fun.

My breaking point, which I recounted in my 2020 memoir Pothead, came at Game 2 of the 2017 World Series at Dodger Stadium, where I was so wasted on weed that I had a screaming meltdown in front of security when I found out the ticket I’d bought was fraudulent.

It was a low point, a bottom so deep and so pathetic that I should have quit right there. But I went on for another three weeks before I finally crawled to a Marijuana Anonymous meeting and asked for help.

Now, nearly a decade later, I’ve emerged from my cocoon to find that THC is everywhere. I live in a state where it’s still technically illegal, but you can find some sort of THC variant in most convenience stores and in most bars and nightclubs.

My friend is an attorney who represents the Texas Alcoholic Beverage Commission. He says his client is in a near panic. Sales are down for every beverage except for nonalcoholic mixers and those THC seltzers. THC is the beer, wine, and vodka of the present day, combined.

And so we find ourselves in a bizarre situation where people are drinking way less alcohol because it’s unhealthy for you, but they are instead ingesting THC products—either through soda, gummies, or any number of other concoctions—from unknown manufacturers, with unknown formulas and malleable dosages.

They’re also vaping, which emerging research is showing is kind of awful for our lungs. A University of Michigan study recently showed that teens who vaped cannabis were twice as likely to experience respiratory symptoms like “wheezing or whistling” in the chest than those who vaped nicotine or smoked traditional cigarettes.

Unlike traditional marijuana, this stuff isn’t “from the earth.” Who knows where it’s really from—or where it’s going?

When I quit weed in 2017, and somewhat loudly and publicly announced myself as an addict, I got the requisite amount of sympathy, but also a tremendous amount of pushback. Marijuana isn’t dangerous, people said. It isn’t addictive. It’s actually good for you.

In 2016, Gavin Newsom, then California’s lieutenant governor, spearheaded the legalization push for recreational marijuana. Cannabis sales have produced more than $8 billion in tax revenue for California since retail sales began in 2018, with a decent proportion of that funneled into teen substance-abuse prevention programs. But the state has also seen an increase in children suffering from cannabis poisoning.

It’s been a massive shift, moving from medically administered weed for veterans and cancer patients to powerful THC beverages you can buy at your bodega.

Legalization has created a new frontier of intoxicants without a centralized warning system for users. Data from a 2025 national study of U.S. adults stated that 15 percent of respondents reported daily or near-daily cannabis use.

Among those daily users, 18 percent reported experiencing symptoms consistent with cannabinoid hyperemesis syndrome, a disorder characterized by cyclic, severe nausea, intractable vomiting, and gut pain alleviated temporarily by hot baths or showers.

This translates to an estimated 7.2 million people in the United States alone, many of them teenagers, experiencing these symptoms. That number is probably vastly underestimated, given that fewer than 12 percent of sufferers have received a formal diagnosis.

Welcome to the darker side of legalized weed, a world of cannabis-induced psychosis, “scromiting” (yes, screaming while vomiting), and concoctions like “shatter” or “wax,” which contain up to 90 percent THC, as opposed to the 3 to 5 percent in joints from previous decades.

So I find myself in a somewhat odd situation. I was a relatively early victim of modern weed’s potency, as well as a relatively early unadopter, and a very early voice in the wilderness warning the world that marijuana might not be the answer to all our health concerns. But now I find myself, like a patient waking up from a long coma, in a world where THC is as prevalent as, and cheaper than, whiskey. Yet still, I find myself thinking it should be legal. So which is it? Maybe we still don’t have enough data.

Thirty years since the start of legalization might feel like a long time, but it’s really not. In terms of scientific analysis, we’re still in relatively undiscovered country. If you’re suffering from chronic pain, or dying of cancer, marijuana is a healthier option than, say, oxycodone. But for the rest of us? No one really knows.

The only thing I can be truly sure of is that it’s no longer for me.

#1229. This Is What Weed Really Did to My Body

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

How a medical marijuana card works for three real guys.

In November 1996, California became the first state to legalize medical cannabis. (Many) politics and (some) physicians promised us breakthroughs in better health. Thirty years later, how are we really doing? Men’s Health decided to find out in this special report package. Read more here.

THREE REAL GUYS. Three dramatically different mental health conditions. Here’s how a weed card works for them.

THE GUY: TJ, a 47-year-old data insights professional

THE CONDITION: Multiple sclerosis, a chronic autoimmune disease that affects the central nervous system

THE EXPERIENCE: TJ was diagnosed with MS at the age of 25. Doctors prescribed him Rebif, a drug that helped his symptoms but made him depressed. “I was immediately suicidal on that medicine,” he says. Years before medical marijuana was legalized in his state, a friend suggested pot. TJ was desperate, tried weed, and says he felt immediate relief. In 2022, he obtained his medical card. Today, TJ struggles with nerve pain and migraines but says cannabis helps him play volleyball, travel, and stay active. “I don’t know that I would have a productive and happy life if I were not treating myself that way.”

THE GUY: CJ, a 32-year-old postdoctoral researcher of addiction neurobiology

THE CONDITION: Sarcoidosis, an inflammatory disease that causes collections of immune cells, called granulomas, to form in the body. Depending on the location, granulomas can result in fatigue, joint pain, nausea, and swelling. CJ has a rare form located in his brain stem.

THE EXPERIENCE: CJ’s symptoms began in his early 20s, starting with skin issues and leading, eventually, to debilitating nausea. After doctors diagnosed him with sarcoidosis in 2021, he went from using cannabis occasionally to using it daily for nausea control. Through self-experimentation, he learned the combination of cannabinoids that worked the best. Because the ratios were harder to find in OTC products, CJ got his medical card in 2023. “This brought down my overall symptom load a huge amount,” CJ says. Along with an off-label Crohn’s disease medication, medical marijuana has brought him back to 90 percent of his old self, he says.

THE GUY: Jonah, a 41-year-old bookstore manager

THE CONDITION: Generalized anxiety disorder (GAD), a mental health condition that causes excessive fear and worry and interferes with day-to-day functioning.

THE EXPERIENCE: Jonah says he was always labeled “the weird kid.” He ran away at 16 and experienced problems with drugs, alcohol, and homelessness in his early 20s, until his mother convinced him to talk to a therapist, who diagnosed him with anxiety and recommended medical marijuana. After using cannabis for a few weeks, Jonah says, the dark fog that had made him want to act out or isolate himself began to lift. “Conversations with people became a lot easier; decision-making, especially good decisions, became a lot easier.” Sixteen years after that therapy appointment, he’s happily married and life is good, he says.

There’s so much more going on in the world of weed and health. Read more here.

Lauren Vinopal is a writer based in Chicago whose work has been published in GQ, Slate, Mel Magazine, and more.
