#1148. How to Spot the Signs of Chronic Stress and Help Your Body Recover

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Experts explain what it is and its effects on sleep, skin, hair, mood, and long-term health.

These days, a growing list of things can lead to stress. Chronic stress in particular, though, can be very damaging mentally and emotionally, and can even show up physically through major hair and skin changes. Briefly worrying about something, waking up anxious in the middle of the night, or having a lingering distracting thought can all be signs of stress. But persistence separates chronic stress from regular stress. Experts explain how chronic stress differs from other forms, how it can show up in different areas of your life and body, and how to get a grip on it before the repercussions get out of hand.

While there’s no clinical definition or diagnosis for chronic stress, Karinn A. Glover, MD, MPH, attending psychiatrist at Montefiore Einstein Psychiatry Associates, describes it as life events or dynamics where we feel (consciously or unconsciously) anxious, on edge, irritated, or fearful. “These conditions can be constant or intermittent, but they persist over an extended period. In practice, that can be four to six weeks or even decades,” she tells ELLE.

Stress has an innate negative connotation, but Dr. Michael J. Hall, MD, MSc, founder and medical director of Hall Longevity Clinic and author of Infinite Youth: Solving the Aging Equation, argues that stress itself isn’t necessarily the enemy. “It’s an ancient survival system designed simply to switch on, solve a problem, and then switch off. Chronic stress is what happens when it never switches off,” he tells ELLE. “In our modern society, the hyper-stimulus of interconnectivity that is surrounding us at all times is unnatural and causes the body to stay in a low-grade emergency for weeks, months, even years,” says Hall.

As a psychiatrist, Glover says she looks at the mind and body together when addressing stress. “That means insomnia can be an issue, as well as the other responses to chronic stress: low mood, cynicism, fatigue, chronic pain, headaches, repetitive dietary choices that result in weight gain, high cholesterol, as well as high blood pressure and even prediabetes,” says Glover.

What happens internally when the stress response stays on can be a combination of things. Hall explains that cortisol and adrenaline can remain elevated, fear-based living sets in, certain diseases can be triggered, and almost every system in the body pays a price. “Blood pressure and heart rate run higher. Blood sugar stays up, which over time pushes toward insulin resistance and fat storage around the midsection. Inflammation rises quietly. Immune function weakens, so you get sick more often and heal more slowly. Sleep becomes lighter and less restorative, digestion suffers, and hormones like testosterone, estrogen, and thyroid get chaotic,” Hall describes. He adds that research has even linked long-term psychological stress to shorter telomeres, the DNA end tips, a marker of cellular aging. “In plain terms, chronic stress makes you biologically older than your birthday,” says Hall.

Hall points out that the obvious culprits are ongoing pressures like life, work, finances, caregiving, relationship strain, and health worries. “But I see just as much hidden stress,” he adds. “The body doesn’t distinguish much between an angry email and a skipped meal. It all goes into the same bucket. In my Aging Funnel framework, I call this Load. Most people only count the emotional part and miss everything else filling the bucket,” says Hall.

Glover notes a few additional factors that can contribute to chronic stress: “Life hands us any number of situations and challenges that can be overwhelming: witnessing the suffering of a loved one or a community member, living in an unsafe neighborhood or an area that is heavily policed, facing health challenges, or employment in a very tense workplace. These are all examples of situations where we may feel intermittently powerless, vigilant about our safety, or threatened in our well-being,” says Glover. Additionally, she notes that the constant exposure to stress hormones and neurotransmitters is linked to premature aging and chronic disease.

Glover describes “regular stress” as the everyday challenges we face: commuting, caring for an infant, doing a presentation at work, or taking examinations at school. “Now, what if all those circumstances were happening in one day, every day for a month? That would be chronic stress. Anything that taps into our fight/flight/freeze reactions is usually a source of chronic stress and can affect our well-being if we are repeatedly exposed to such conditions or dynamics,” Glover explains.

Highlighting the differences, Hall argues that regular, or acute, stress is not only real, but it’s also healthy. “A tough workout, a deadline, a cold plunge, a public talk: you rise to the challenge, then you recover, and you come back stronger. That’s adaptation,” he challenges. “Chronic stress is when the recovery phase never arrives. The difference isn’t the size of the stressor. It's whether you return to baseline. Think of it as a sprint versus never being allowed to stop running, and you never can recover,” says Hall.

Both Glover and Hall have established that chronic stress has links to premature aging. Signs show up both on the body through weight gain or loss and hair loss, and in lifestyle changes like insomnia, appetite changes (increased snacking, more sweets, more salty foods, or the total opposite, a big drop in appetite), and nervous tics like absently pulling hair, twirling it to the point that it becomes damaged, and fidgeting more than usual.

Chronic stress can also show up in our posture. “We hunch our shoulders, our neck muscles start to contract unconsciously, and we may even find ourselves clenching our jaw and squeezing our pelvic floor muscles unconsciously,” Glover explains. Another sign can be the onset of digestive conditions like IBS (irritable bowel syndrome) and acid reflux, which Glover notes exemplify how interconnected mental well-being, stress responses, and physical well-being are. “The link between chronic stress and cardiovascular health is perhaps the clearest and most well-known because we see it depicted on television and because ignoring it can often be a matter of life and death,” Glover adds.

Hall backs many of the signs Glover describes, with a few additions to keep an eye out for: fatigue that sleep doesn’t fix, waking at 3 a.m., tension headaches, a tight jaw or teeth grinding, digestive issues, belly weight that won’t budge, frequent colds, low libido, irritability, and brain fog.

From a beauty perspective, Hall says the skin is often the first to tell the story. In his book Infinite Youth, he describes aging as an equation, suggesting chronic stress is one of the most powerful accelerants in it. “It's essentially load without recovery, and that wears the system down faster than time alone ever would,” says Hall. Cortisol breaks down collagen and weakens the skin barrier, so you see dullness, dryness, fine lines, and slower healing. Stress can trigger breakouts and commonly flares acne, eczema, psoriasis, and rosacea. Poor sleep shows up as dark circles and puffiness. “Many women notice increased hair shedding two to three months after a stressful period, and there’s emerging research tying stress to premature graying. Nails can become brittle. You can’t out-serum chronic stress. The glow has to come from the inside,” Hall says.

Glover says that recognizing chronic stress is the best place to begin, and working toward a solution can be rewarding. “Think about the most stressful days you’ve had lately and consider the people, conversations, or activities that were the most stressful,” she suggests. “If scrolling on your phone isn’t on your list, it should be. Yes, it’s important to be aware of current events, but how aware and how frequently?” she asks. If you’re routinely exposed to chronic stress, Glover recommends changing your settings. “Get yourself outside for some exposure to nature, or to spend time with loved ones, or to have spontaneous laughter, nutritious food, and some exercise so you can put all those stress hormones to good use,” says Glover. Go for a bike ride, walk the beach or through a park if there’s a safe place outdoors to do so.

Hall offers additional suggestions that he calls The 10 Commandments of Longevity:

Hall adds that if stress comes with persistent low mood, anxiety, chest pain, or sleep problems lasting more than a few weeks, see your physician.

Glover and Hall agree that with constant news streams and evolving life changes, avoiding stress is impossible and not always necessary. “The best we can do is minimize our exposure to chronic stress and the impact it can have on us,” says Glover.

Hall recommends a two-pronged approach to reducing chronic stress: “First, reduce the load: identify every source filling the bucket, including the hidden physical ones. Second, expand your capacity: build the resilience to handle what remains through sleep, nutrition, movement, recovery, and connection. When the load falls and the capacity rises, the body finally gets the signal that the emergency is over. That's when real repair begins, and you see it in your energy, sleep, mood, and your skin. The goal isn’t a life without stress. It's a body that knows how to come back down,” Hall explains.

Establish boundaries with the people around you. Set a firm Do Not Disturb rule at bedtime to allow for uninterrupted sleep. Reserve time for daily exercise. Connect with emotionally healthy loved ones. Create or join a supportive community, and make time for personal reflection and a wind-down routine daily. “Our bodies were built for movement and for pleasure, especially after all we do at work and to care for others. Let’s add stress relief to the list of things our body wants,” says Glover.

#469. 80 Years After It Survived WWII, a Fire Has Destroyed Hitler’s Infamous Eagle’s Nest

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Hitler rarely visited the mountaintop chalet after it was built in 1938, seven years before American soldiers claimed it for themselves.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. Why Trust Us?

On October 24, 2026, flames engulfed the mountaintop chalet that had by now become the most popular tourist spot in Obersalzberg, Germany: the Kehlsteinhaus. Dubbed the “Eagle’s Nest” by the American soldiers who later occupied it, the massive building was commissioned in 1937 by private secretary Martin Bormann for his boss, to serve as a venue for diplomatic functions and other affairs. The mountaintop building survived the war, but nearly 80 years later, was the target of suspected arson.

While authorities did apprehend a suspect not long after the fire broke out, the Associated Press reports, they will also “look into the possibility of a political motive in view of the building's history.”

The “history” in question is the Kehlsteinhaus’ original owner. Bormann’s boss, after all, was Adolf Hitler.

The massive project took 13 months to complete and cost 30 million Reichsmarks (Roughly $120 million in 1937, or $2.81 billion today), but it was ultimately a folly. Hitler only visited the Eagle’s Nest 14 times, disliking heights, distrusting the building’s ornate elevator, and frowning upon Bormann’s excess in its construction.

The book Inside the Third Reich: Memoirs by Albert Speer recounts that some around Hitler would remark that “Bormann has created a gold-rush town atmosphere [in Obersalzberg]. Only he doesn’t find any, he spends it.” In response, Hitler reportedly “regretted the hubbub” and remarked that “It’s Bormann’s doing; I don’t want to interfere.”

While Eva Braun and her family spent ample time in the mountaintop retreat, including hosting the reception for Gretl Braun’s wedding in 1944, Hitler himself preferred his summer home, Berghof, located just four miles away.

The headlines surrounding the fire focus on the Hitler connection, understandably so, as it’s perhaps one of the only Hitler-related sites that was left standing (Berghof was demolished by the Bavarian government in 1952, for example). But a new report in Stars and Stripes calls attention to some other famous occupants of the Eagle’s Nest, and what remnants of them we lost in the fires which consumed the historic site.

By May 1945, U.S. Army units reached Berchtesgaden and Obersalzberg, ultimately occupying Kehlsteinhaus, which had been abandoned but remained largely undamaged by Allied bombs. There is some debate around which unit reached the Eagle’s Nest first, but what is certain is that elements of both the 3rd Infantry Division and the 101st Airborne Division entered and occupied the mountain retreat. Amidst the latter was the company known as “Easy Company,” latter immortalized in the book and HBO miniseries Band of Brothers. The occupation of the Eagle’s Nest is depicted in the Emmy-winning series’ finale, “Points.”

“American soldiers occupied the site,” Stars and Stripes notes, “used it briefly as a command post and recreation area, and took souvenirs from the interiors.”

They also left some souvenirs behind. Kehlsteinhaus boasted a lavish red marble fireplace, a personal gift from Benito Mussolini to Hitler. That fireplace provided a fine canvas for the men of the U.S. Army, many of whom emblazoned it with their names, a little G.I. graffiti to mark their territory.

For a time, the Eagle’s Nest served as rest-and-relaxation facilities for American soldiers, before it was turned over to the State of Bavaria in 1960 and converted into a tourist attraction with a restaurant and beer garden.

It’s easy to bristle at the idea of one of Hitler’s homes being a “tourist attraction.” It’s reasonable, at first blush, to think it gauche that a restaurant operated for decades in a building built by the Nazis. One might even question if anything was truly lost when a monument of such infamy goes up in flames.

Yet, a deeper understanding of the building’s full history shows why its continued existence held such significance, and why the Bavarian government intends to rebuild once the dust has settled on the rubble. As the Army newspaper noted: “Generations after the end of the war, American veterans often returned to the Eagle’s Nest, walking the same terraces they had first seen in 1945.”

Michale Natale is a News Editor for the Hearst Enthusiast Group. As a writer and researcher, he has produced written and audio-visual content for more than fifteen years, spanning historical periods from the dawn of early man to the Golden Age of Hollywood. His stories for the Enthusiast Group have involved coordinating with organizations like the National Parks Service and the Secret Service, and travelling to notable historical sites and archaeological digs, from excavations of America’ earliest colonies to the former homes of Edgar Allan Poe.

#430. The secret to slower aging may be hiding in your gut

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Scientists have uncovered a surprising connection between the bacteria living in the human gut and the speed at which the body ages. Researchers at the University of Hawaiʻi at Mānoa found that certain gut microbes are associated with slower biological aging, while others are linked to faster aging.

The study, published in Scientific Reports, found that differences in gut bacterial communities accounted for approximately 15% of the variation in participants' biological aging rates. The results suggest that the gut microbiome, the vast community of microorganisms living in the digestive system, may provide valuable clues about aging at the molecular level.

"This study advances our understanding of the relationship between the gut microbiome and biological aging," said senior author Alika K. Maunakea of the UH Mānoa John A. Burns School of Medicine. "We found that microbial patterns in the gut were significantly associated with molecular measures of aging, even independent of chronological age. These findings open important new directions for understanding how modifiable biological systems influence long-term health trajectories and longevity."

Scientists Identify Gut Bacteria Linked to Faster and Slower Aging

To investigate the relationship between gut bacteria and aging, researchers examined stool and blood samples collected from 123 adults across a wide range of ages. They identified the bacteria present in each participant's gut and compared those findings with blood-based molecular markers used to estimate the body's rate of aging.

These biological measurements differ from chronological age, which simply reflects how many years someone has been alive. Instead, they are designed to assess how quickly aging-related changes are occurring within the body.

The researchers found a notable association between the composition of the gut microbiome and DunedinPACE, an advanced biomarker that measures the pace of biological aging.

Some bacterial species stood out for their particularly strong relationships with aging. Bifidobacterium adolescentis was among the microbes most strongly associated with slower biological aging. In contrast, Succinivibrio dextrinosolvens showed one of the strongest associations with faster aging.

These differences suggest that particular patterns of gut bacteria may offer important information about how rapidly the body is aging.

How Diet, Lifestyle, and Environment May Influence Gut Health

Lead author Braden P. Kunihiro said the discovery adds to growing evidence that the gut microbiome could play an important role in precision health research, which aims to better understand individual differences in health and disease.

Unlike chronological age, the composition of the gut microbiome can change in response to everyday influences, including diet, lifestyle, and environmental conditions. This makes it a particularly interesting area of investigation for scientists studying healthy aging.

"The gut microbiome is highly responsive to lifestyle, diet, environment and other everyday exposures," Kunihiro said. "Our findings suggest that the microbiome may reflect important aspects of biological resilience and aging in ways that could eventually help inform future wellness and healthy aging strategies."

The research also included Native Hawaiian and Pacific Islander participants, groups that have historically received limited representation in studies of aging and gut microorganisms.

Co-author Ruben Juarez explained that including these populations could help scientists investigate how social circumstances and environmental conditions leave lasting biological effects over the course of a person's life.

"The microbiome exists at the intersection of behavior, environment and human biology," Juarez said. "Studies like this help illuminate potential pathways through which lived experiences and social factors influence long-term health outcomes."

Could Changing Gut Bacteria Influence How We Age?

Although the findings reveal a meaningful relationship between gut bacteria and biological aging, researchers stress that the study does not establish cause and effect. In other words, finding certain microbes in people who age more slowly does not necessarily mean those bacteria are responsible for slowing the aging process.

Further studies will be needed to determine whether deliberately changing the gut microbiome can influence biological aging or improve long-term health.

Still, the findings offer a promising starting point for exploring how the microorganisms living inside us relate to the aging process and whether they could eventually help guide more personalized approaches to healthy aging.

"This is an important early step toward understanding the gut microbiome's relationship with healthy aging," Maunakea said. "Future research will help clarify the mechanisms involved and whether these microbial signatures may eventually contribute to precision approaches for promoting long-term health and wellness."

#417. Low testosterone may make sugar far more harmful to your liver

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Low testosterone can contribute to several health problems, and an unhealthy diet may make some of those risks even worse. One major concern is metabolic dysfunction-associated steatotic liver disease (MASLD), a condition that affects approximately 40% of adult men worldwide and has become a significant global health issue.

Fatty liver, the earliest stage of MASLD, occurs when excess fat builds up in the liver. Several factors are associated with the condition, including obesity, type 2 diabetes, low testosterone levels, and excessive fructose consumption from sugary drinks and processed foods. However, scientists have not fully understood how these factors interact or whether their combined effects are more harmful than each one individually.

How Low Testosterone and Fructose Affect the Liver

To investigate this connection, a research team from the Graduate School of Agriculture, led by graduate student Hiroki Takahashi and Associate Professor Naoki Harada, examined how reduced testosterone and high fructose intake influence liver health.

The researchers used eight-week-old male mice and divided them into six experimental groups. Some underwent castration to lower testosterone levels, while others received a sham operation. The groups included Sham/Control, Sham/Fructose, Sham/Fructose + Antibiotics, Castration/Control, Castration/Fructose, and Castration/Fructose + Antibiotics.

To determine how these conditions affected the animals, the team analyzed liver cells and tissues, blood plasma, organic acids from the cecum, and samples of gut bacteria.

The results revealed that mice with reduced testosterone levels developed heavier livers when they consumed fructose. However, this increase in liver weight was smaller in mice that also received antibiotics.

When examined separately, low testosterone and fructose intake produced only modest changes in liver triglyceride levels. When combined, however, the two factors had a much stronger effect, significantly increasing fat accumulation and worsening fatty liver.

The researchers also identified changes in the composition of gut bacteria, altered liver gene activity, and elevated levels of pyruvate in the cecum among mice exposed to both reduced testosterone and fructose.

These findings suggested that the gut microbiome could play an important role in explaining why the combination was particularly harmful.

Further experiments helped the researchers identify a possible biological explanation for the increased liver fat.

"Upon examining this mechanism, we found that changes in the gut microbiota led to increased levels of pyruvate within the intestine. Furthermore, experiments using mouse-derived primary hepatocytes revealed that pyruvate acts synergistically with fructose to promote neutral lipid accumulation in hepatocytes," explained Takahashi.

In other words, changes in gut bacteria appeared to increase intestinal pyruvate, a compound involved in energy metabolism. Laboratory experiments with liver cells isolated from mice showed that pyruvate and fructose worked together to increase fat storage in those cells.

The discovery suggests that interactions among testosterone levels, dietary fructose, and gut bacteria may help explain how fatty liver develops and worsens.

Potential New Ways to Prevent Fatty Liver Disease

Understanding this relationship could eventually help researchers identify new approaches to preventing or treating fatty liver disease. In particular, targeting the processes through which pyruvate contributes to fat accumulation may offer promising possibilities.

"Going forward, we hope to clarify the mechanism by which pyruvate promotes triglyceride accumulation to pioneer the development of therapeutic drugs and establishment of preventive methods through dietary interventions," added Professor Harada.

Although the findings come from experiments in mice and isolated mouse liver cells, they provide new insight into how hormonal changes and diet may interact to influence liver health.

The study was published in American Journal of Physiology-Endocrinology and Metabolism.

Materials provided by Osaka Metropolitan University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#1199. Build Strong Bones in Just Four Weeks With This Plan: Download the PDF Today

Bulletproof your bones with this easy-to-follow workout plan.

With aging comes a number of changes—from shifts in muscle mass and hormones to changes in sleep and bone health. Bone density naturally begins to decline around age 35, but there’s plenty you can do to support strong, healthy bones at every stage of life.

Introducing the WH+ 4 Weeks to Stronger Bones workout plan. This comprehensive program gives you the tools to build strength, support bone density, and keep your body resilient at any age.

Each week includes three strength workouts, two bone-health-focused cardio circuits (one optional), one active recovery day, and one rest day. The typical schedule will look something like this:

Saturday: Bone-Strength Circuit 2 (optional)

The only way to truly build bone strength is to load your bones consistently. Repetition is what helps them become stronger over time, so it’s important for you to find a workout schedule that you know you’ll stick with. Although each workout is assigned to a specific day, feel free to adjust the schedule to fit your life. What matters most is choosing workout days that are realistic for you and following through.

#4500. Daylight Saving Time Ends This Weekend. Here’s When (and Why) the Clocks Go Back

📅 Oct 27, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

Why do we "fall back" in November? We answer your questions.

Daylight saving time ends this weekend, with the clocks officially changing at 2 a.m. on Sunday. While it’ll still be light enough for trick-or-treating on Halloween, the time change will hit the day after, when most of the US adjusts back one hour.

The time shift is notorious for disrupting sleep patterns and schedules. Some politicians are advocating for the abolition of time changes and the permanent adoption of daylight saving time, or DST. The House passed the Sunshine Protection Act in July, but it still needs Senate approval and the president’s signature to become law — meaning that, for now, we still have dark winter hours coming.

If you, too, dislike how early it gets dark each day through winter, just remember that brighter evenings will return in mid-March.

Daylight saving time in the US ends at 2 a.m. local time on Sunday. It will return at 2 a.m. local time on March 14, 2027. The end of DST is when we “fall back,” whereas in March, we “spring forward.”

The US isn’t alone in observing daylight saving time. Check out this list of other countries that use DST, along with their start and end dates.

The official US time website shows where time zone boundaries are located.

The exact start dates for daylight saving time and standard time float a little. DST kicks off on the second Sunday of March and ends on the first Sunday of November with the return of standard time.

We spend about eight months of the year in DST. We can thank the Uniform Time Act of 1966 for bringing some order to what had been a complicated history of time changes in the US.

“Motivated by transportation improvements, this act mandated standard time within the existing time zones and established a permanent system of uniform DST, including the dates and times for twice-yearly transitions,” the Bureau of Transportation Statistics states in a history of time zones.

The country’s time zones date to the railroad boom of the late 1800s. DST formally entered the chat in 1918, but its application was inconsistent until 1966. The country tried year-round daylight saving time in January 1974 under President Richard Nixon to address the energy crisis. It didn’t go over well. Congress and President Gerald Ford restored standard time in October 1974.

If you truly hate time changes, then consider moving to Arizona (except for the Navajo Nation) or Hawaii. Those states are on standard time all year long, and their residents don’t have to deal with the physical and mental health impacts of the change. Puerto Rico, Guam, the Virgin Islands, American Samoa and the Northern Mariana Islands also skip DST.

Read more: Why Is NASA Working on a Time Zone for the Moon?

Organizations such as the National Sleep Foundation, the American Academy of Sleep Medicine and the Society for Research on Biological Rhythms are united in calling for permanent standard time, saying it’s better for human biology.

“There’s a mismatch between the outside world and our internal clocks during daylight saving time that can result in some negative health consequences,” says Joseph Dzierzewski, senior vice president of research and scientific affairs at the National Sleep Foundation.

Dzierzewski cites mental health concerns and upticks in cardiovascular events and drowsy driving. He also calls out safety issues with young children walking to school or sitting at bus stops in the dark. Those safety issues were a big reason why permanent DST didn’t last in 1974.

Time changes aren’t popular. An October 2024 YouGov poll (PDF) of more than 1,100 US adults found that 63% would like to see the changing of the clocks eliminated. Only 17% wanted to keep time changes, and 20% weren’t sure.

Efforts to end the time changes have so far failed to pass into law. The bipartisan Sunshine Protection Act, which would have made daylight saving time permanent, passed the Senate in 2022 — the new version of the bill passed the House earlier this year and was sent on to the Senate to review in July.

While many people agree on eliminating time changes, the schism between daylight saving time and standard time remains. Standard time may have a bit of an image issue compared to DST.

“Part of the issue is that people associate daylight saving time with summer. People love summer, right?” Dzierzewski said. “But the simple fact of the matter is, it would still be summer if we were on permanent standard time.”

President Donald Trump criticized time changes in a 2024 Truth Social post: “The Republican Party will use its best efforts to eliminate Daylight Saving Time, which has a small but strong constituency, but shouldn’t! Daylight Saving Time is inconvenient and very costly to our Nation.”

Bills and resolutions at the state level haven’t had real-world impacts yet. Politicians are still split on whether they want permanent daylight saving or standard time. Dzierzewski is hopeful that permanent standard time will win out.

“This is the first time in several years where I’ve seen more legislation introduced at a state level in support of permanent standard time as the solution,” he said.

If you’d like to see time changes end and want to advocate for either daylight saving or standard time, contact your state and national representatives and express your opinion.

Daylight saving time is happening, so now it’s a matter of figuring out how to deal with it. It’s not just about the day of the time change.

“You’re in a better situation to cope with the effects of the clock change if you have good sleep health generally,” Dzierzewski said. He recommends exposure to bright light in the morning, physical activity during the day, eating meals at regular times and practicing a relaxing wind-down routine at night.

The majority of Americans struggle to get the National Sleep Foundation’s recommended 7 to 9 hours of sleep for most adults. A CNET survey found that more than half of US adults use some method to cope with sleep-related challenges. Check out some expert-backed tips for improving your sleep quality.

“If you’re doing these healthy sleep behaviors, you’re probably more resilient to some of these changes,” Dzierzewski said. “If you have poor sleep to begin with, you’re probably going to feel it a little bit more.”

Establishing a better sleep routine can take time. There are two things you can do in direct response to the time change. Dzierzewski suggests adjusting your bedtime and wake-up time gradually ahead of the change. For example, you could shift your schedule by 15 minutes per day and ease yourself into DST. He also recommends getting a good dose of bright morning light on the day of the time change to help set your internal body clock.

Think of DST as another kind of wake-up call, one that can motivate you to evaluate your sleep routine and quality and make positive changes. Start with these six simple habits. And don’t forget to change your clocks. Do it the night before, and you won’t have to worry about it later.

#2494. Why Do Moms Feel Like We Need Permission To Sleep?

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A recent survey found that half of moms would rather sleep than have a spa day. So why is it so hard to give ourselves permission to rest?

My husband can fall asleep anywhere, anytime. Once, we took a day trip to Sedona, Arizona, and he fell asleep sitting on a rock. It’s a talent. And the amazing thing is... the kids actually left him alone. Me, on the other hand? I can be missing for five minutes, and they have spidey senses. I can’t remember the last time anyone let me nap without pouncing on me within a few minutes of trying.

And here’s another thing: I often sacrifice sleep for productivity. I can be exhausted at night, but if I haven’t accomplished everything on my to-do list, whether it’s remembering to email one of my kids’ teachers back, loading the dishwasher, or prepping for tomorrow’s interview, I’ll stay awake until it’s done. When my head finally hits the pillow, my mind often keeps spinning through tomorrow’s to-do list.

Apparently, I’m not the only mom doing this.

Naturepedic, an organic sleep wellness brand, recently released a survey of 1,000 U.S. mothers ages 25–45 with children under 18 living at home. Of those surveyed, 90% of moms said they struggle to quiet their busy minds at bedtime, and 48% get fewer than seven hours of sleep, with moms of kids 2 and younger averaging about 5.9 hours.

What’s even bleaker: 62% of moms feel they need someone else’s formal permission to prioritize their own sleep without feeling guilty.

Naturpedic calls this the "sleep permission gap," the disconnect between knowing we need rest and actually allowing ourselves to get it. But why are we treating sleep like a privilege we have to earn rather than a basic human need? And how do we stop this sad, nasty little habit?

Although more men are stepping up to help with household duties, unequal household responsibilities, limited support, and parenting demands can still play a role. For moms, who rarely get a break, this pressure can build up. Getting a juicy eight or nine hours of sleep? Somehow, that can feel oddly gluttonous, or like we’re wasting precious time.

"We live in a society where we increasingly measure a person's value by how much they do and how much they produce," says Lorena Bernal, founder of Live Love Better and author of It Starts With You.

She says mothers can feel selfish or lazy when they choose to rest instead of doing something for their families, adding that “in a way, mothers can feel too needed to switch off.”

For moms, nighttime is also often our only chance for “quiet time” or to catch up on things we’ve been putting off. I end up sitting on the couch a little longer, bingeing social media reels for five or 10 minutes because I don’t feel I have time to watch an actual TV series.

But this habit can also stretch to the point where I ask myself why I keep putting off sleep to Google something stupid, like how long Zac Efron and Vanessa Hudgens dated in real life (don’t judge: my daughter just discovered High School Musical).

Emily Krushefski, a licensed professional counselor and CEO of Emotional Innovations, says self-blame also serves an unexpected psychological purpose: a sense of control. In our heads, we might decide we're exhausted because we didn't manage our time well enough. She says we think that if we become more organized, productive, or disciplined, we can fix the problem.

I can relate, as I’m prone to staying up late to make the next day “easier,” which only deepens my exhaustion. "The illusion of being in charge of these complex and unrelenting demands provides a sense of agency," Krushefski explains.

She gives voice to what many of us mothers feel — that we juggle too many expectations for our families while managing our careers, other relationships, and the lovely, never-ending household responsibilities. "Sometimes the problem isn't that nobody will let us rest," says Bernal. "It's that we have become so identified with being the person who takes care of everything that we struggle to give ourselves permission to put it down."

When researchers asked what would help the mothers surveyed sleep better, 59% said someone taking over household chores, while 58% said being allowed to sleep in.

My only question is: Sleeping in? What’s that?

After years of waking up to care for babies and young children, our brains can become accustomed to nighttime vigilance, and that pattern can be hard to break as kids get older.

My kids are getting older, but I still wake consistently throughout the night. Similarly, I find that when I’m super busy, I just can’t shut off my brain when I get to bed, even if I’m exhausted. Krushefski explains that the mental load of managing a household can cause rumination, repetitive thoughts that are hard to interrupt. Our brains keep scanning for the next thing we've forgotten or need to accomplish.

Bernal likens it to a computer with too many windows open. “Each window has a different program running: the children, work, something you need to organize, a conversation you had, something you forgot to do, something you’re worried about, tomorrow’s schedule. You may have physically stopped, but all those programs are still running in the background,” she says.

“Your sleep drive builds all day, but your stress response can override it,” explains Dr. Shelby Harris, a board-certified behavioral sleep medicine specialist and psychologist with BetterSleep. “Over time, the brain can start linking bed with worry.”

We're expected to do everything for everyone, and we're still preached at about self-care. It's almost comical.

But we do have to learn to prioritize sleep. It's essential to our well-being. Chronic sleep deprivation can affect our attention, memory, emotional regulation, and decision-making, and can put you at a higher risk of type 2 diabetes and heart disease, says Harris.

One basic way to make more time for sleep is to be honest about which responsibilities are really urgent, and which can wait, Bernal says. When we do too much for our families and don't let them practice independence, it’s to our detriment and theirs. I've recently worked harder to get my kids to do their own laundry, help pack their lunches, and clean up after dinner. These are essential life skills they need for adulting anyway, and they shouldn't all fall on me!

Harris suggests spending 10 minutes earlier in the evening writing down what's on your mind, along with a next step for each item, to help take the pressure off bedtime. Dr. Joseph Dzierzewski with the National Sleep Foundation recommends getting out of bed if you've been lying awake for about 20 minutes and sleep doesn't feel close. Because the harder we try, the more it will evade us.

“No amount of sleep strategies can compensate for an absence of sleep opportunity,” warns Dzierzewski. In other words, we can try all the bedtime routines in the world, but if we don't have enough time to sleep, what's the point? For families with a partner or other support, Dzierzewski strongly recommends sharing nighttime responsibilities so each caregiver gets an uninterrupted stretch of sleep.

For persistent insomnia, it may be time to see a doctor to protect your health.

As Bernal beautifully puts it, “Sleep shouldn't be the reward you receive once you have done enough. Sleep is part of what allows you to live, love, think, work, and care for others in the first place.”

#1515. What Not Dating Is Really Like, According to Relationship Experts

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Spoiler: You can want sex and love while not actively looking for all of it.

Maybe you've deleted the dating apps. Maybe you've stopped entertaining the guy who only remembers you exist after 10 p.m. Or maybe the thought of sitting across from another stranger, explaining what you do for work and how many siblings you have sounds about as appealing as a root canal.

Whatever the reason, you've decided you're not dating right now. And apparently, it seems like everyone you tell this to has some type of question. Are you heartbroken? Have you given up on love? Aren't you lonely? And, perhaps most importantly to the nosiest people in your life, Does this mean you're not having sex?

Take one of my girlfriends, for example. After moving to New York City in 2022, she spent much of her first few years wrapped up in a relationship. Since their breakup two years ago, she hasn't really been dating. Not because she's heartbroken or has given up on love, but because she's been busy building a life of her own. She's made lasting friendships, explored new neighborhoods, picked up hobbies, and even gotten a dog. Not to distract herself from being single, but because these are things she genuinely wants to do.

She's open to love. She even checks the apps occasionally. But unless her dog's leash gets tangled with that of a particularly attractive stranger at the park, dating simply isn't a priority.

And that's the thing about not dating: It doesn't necessarily mean you're lonely, healing, closed off to romance, or even celibate. Sometimes folks simply aren't interested in dating, full stop.

So, I asked relationship experts to debunk five of the biggest misconceptions about not dating, including where celibacy fits into the picture.

There's a difference between not wanting a relationship and not wanting to date. You can absolutely want to fall in love, get married, and live out your happily-ever-after fantasy while also wanting nothing to do with dating apps, awkward first dates, or another situationship that goes nowhere.

"There's often a misconception that everyone who isn't dating doesn't want a relationship," says Alyssa Digges, a mental health counselor at Insight Therapy NYC. "Some don't, but some deeply crave a relationship, they've just burned out on modern dating."

And fair enough. Wanting a partner doesn't automatically mean you have the energy to go looking for one, especially when dating starts to feel more like an obligation than something you're genuinely excited about.

For some people, stepping away is temporary. "I actually see the opposite when people take a break from dating," says Dr. Sabrina Romanoff, PsyD, a clinical psychologist and relationship expert at Hily, of the assumption of giving up on love. "This usually stems from their intentional decision to reinvest their energy, become the best version of themselves, and eventually return to their dating life in a better position."

But here's the thing: Not everyone who's stopped dating is planning their big return to the apps. Not dating doesn't have to be a phase you eventually graduate from, and it certainly doesn't have to be a strategy for finding love.

Whether you're taking a break, leaving the door open for something unexpected, or happily keeping it closed, your decision doesn't require an expiration date.

Sure, being single can get lonely sometimes. So can being in a relationship. Having a significant other isn't some magical guarantee that you'll never feel alone again.

"Romantic relationships are only one way people can find connection," says Romanoff. "There are many other ways people can find connection and fulfillment, including with friends, family, colleagues, and by making new connections."

She also points out that plenty of people in long-term relationships experience loneliness and isolation. In other words, being coupled up and feeling connected aren't necessarily the same thing.

For someone who's not dating, connection might come from spending more time with friends, strengthening family relationships, or investing in a community. And without the time and energy that dating can demand, there may be more room for those relationships to flourish.

"I won't pretend that being single can't feel lonely sometimes, but many of the single people I work with have full, rich lives filled with love and support," says Digges.

Being single doesn't automatically mean being lonely, just as being in a relationship doesn't automatically mean being fulfilled. And while romantic intimacy is its own kind of connection, it isn't the only one worth having. A Saturday night spent with your friends–or solo–isn't inherently less meaningful than one spent with a partner.

There's a strange cultural tendency to treat being single as a problem that needs solving. If you're not dating, people assume you either can't find someone, are too picky, or have some emotional baggage keeping you from putting yourself out there.

"Being single or partnered carries significant weight in our culture as if it assigns your value or worth," says Romanoff. She explains that the longstanding idea that marriage is a woman's greatest accomplishment can be difficult to shake, even today.

And when being partnered is treated as the default, choosing not to date can seem like a decision that requires an explanation. Suddenly, everyone from your mother to your married friend who hasn't been single since college has a theory about why you're still on your own.

But being single doesn't necessarily mean you're struggling to find someone willing to date you.

"It's not that no one wants them, but rather they haven't found someone they truly want," says Digges of many of the single people she works with.

She also pushes back against the idea that being selective means your standards are too high. While superficial preferences, like a specific height or appearance, might be worth reconsidering, she argues that values-based standards are another matter entirely.

For Digges, qualities like kindness, compassion, and intelligence aren't things someone should have to compromise on just to find a partner. Having those standards might narrow your dating pool, but it can also help you avoid relationships that wouldn't have been right for you.

And for people who aren't interested in dating at all, there's no need to defend their standards, attractiveness, or relationship potential in the first place. Being single isn't evidence of a personal deficiency.

The phrase "taking a break from dating" often comes with the assumption that something terrible must have happened first. A breakup, a betrayal, a particularly brutal situationship—surely there's an ex somewhere responsible for this decision. And sometimes there is! Taking time to heal after a difficult relationship can be a perfectly valid reason to step away from dating.

But not every person who's stopped dating is nursing a broken heart, recovering from a toxic ex, or embarking on a post-break-up journey of self-discovery.

"Not dating is often a choice, not a waiting room," says licensed mental health counselor Anna Green, co-founder and chief clinical officer at Thrive Mental Health. "Plenty of adults step back from dating to rebuild friendships, focus on work, or just stop performing for apps."

My friend's experience is a perfect example. After spending so much of her early New York life in a relationship, she's finally getting to experience the city on her own terms. And while the breakup may have been what initially made that possible, it isn't what's keeping her single. She's not spending her weekends distracting herself from her ex. She's spending them doing things she actually enjoys, with people she loves.

There’s a difference between healing from a relationship and remaining single after realizing how much you enjoy life without one.

There's also a tendency to frame every dating break as an opportunity to become the best version of yourself before eventually finding a partner. And while self-reflection can be beneficial, your time outside the dating pool doesn't have to be productive, transformative, or preparation for anything.

Aimee Evnin-Bingham, LMFT, an AASECT-certified sex and relationship therapist, says that when her clients become frustrated with dating, she encourages them to revisit why they want to date in the first place. If the answer is simply that they feel they should be dating, that's worth examining.

How often are we pursuing a relationship because we genuinely want one, versus because we've been conditioned to believe we should always be looking?

That said, there's a distinction between choosing not to date because you genuinely don't want to and avoiding connection because you're afraid of it.

"The one thing I'd watch for: if not dating is really about fear, if you want connection but keep pulling away from people, that is worth talking through with someone," Green says.

The difference isn't necessarily what your dating life looks like from the outside, but how you feel about it. If you're content with your decision, great. If you're unhappy with it or feel stuck between wanting intimacy and avoiding it, that may be worth exploring. Either way, not dating doesn't automatically mean there's something you need to fix.

Now for the question everyone seems particularly interested in: If you're not dating, does that mean you're also not having sex? Not necessarily!

Despite often being lumped together, not dating and celibacy are two different things. Not dating generally means you're not actively pursuing romantic relationships, while celibacy typically refers to intentionally abstaining from sexual activity. And neither automatically requires the other.

"Not dating and celibacy are not the same thing," says Green. "Some people who aren't dating are celibate, some aren't, and some couples go through celibate stretches together. What matters is whether the choice feels like yours."

My friend, for example, hasn't actively dated in two years, but that doesn't mean she's spent those years abstaining from sex. She's had the occasional casual encounter, without feeling the need to turn it into something more. Her decision not to pursue a relationship hasn't required her to put her sex life on hold.

And she's hardly alone in that distinction. Someone who's taking a break from dating might still have casual sex, maintain a friends-with-benefits arrangement, or enjoy sexual pleasure on their own. Others might decide they don't want to engage in partnered sexual activity at all.

Evnin-Bingham emphasizes that stepping away from dating doesn't mean stepping away from pleasure. "This could be a great time to learn more about your body and what turns you on like erotic stories, listening to something sexy or practicing new ways to have solo sex," she says.

She also notes that someone who isn't dating might choose to have casual sex in a way that feels safe, playful, and aligned with their own desires. Of course, not everyone wants casual sex, and choosing not to have it doesn't automatically mean they're sexually frustrated or missing out.

Celibacy itself can mean different things to different people, too. Some define it as abstaining from partnered sex, while others include other forms of sexual activity. And people choose it for all sorts of reasons, including personal values, religious beliefs, emotional well-being, or simply not wanting sex at a particular point in their lives.

Importantly, celibacy isn't exclusive to single people. Someone in a committed relationship can also choose to abstain from sex, either individually or as part of a mutual decision with their partner.

It's also worth acknowledging that not dating can sometimes mean missing physical intimacy, even when you're perfectly happy with your decision.

"For many people, the loss of sex alone doesn't hit as hard as the loss of physical intimacy that comes with dating or being in a relationship," Digges notes. As a single woman myself, I can attest: Sometimes I find myself yearning for a good cuddle sesh more than sex.

But missing sex or physical affection doesn't mean you have to start dating again, just as wanting sex doesn't mean you have to pursue a relationship.

Your romantic choices and your sexual choices can be connected, but they don't have to be. You can be single and sexually active, partnered and celibate, or somewhere in between. None of those combinations requires a justification.

The answer is different for everyone. For some, it's deleting the apps and enjoying a few months without romantic distractions. For others, it's an indefinite decision to prioritize friendships, personal goals, or simply the freedom to do whatever they want without factoring a potential partner into the equation.

It might involve sex. It might involve celibacy. It might be a temporary break or a long-term lifestyle choice. And yes, it might occasionally involve feeling lonely, missing physical intimacy, or wondering whether you should give dating another shot. None of those experiences makes the decision any less valid.

As Green puts it, what matters most is whether the choice feels like yours.

Because not dating doesn't mean your romantic life has failed, your sex life is over, or you're sitting around waiting for someone to come along. Sometimes, you're just not dating. And that's the whole explanation.

#3301. I Rode My Bike in a Climate Chamber That Can Recreate Almost Any Weather on the Planet—Here’s My Biggest Takeaway

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Inside Italy’s Terra X Cube, a Tour de France simulation became a real-world heat test.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. How we test gear.

I looked into the giant chamber filled with strategically placed bike trainers and couldn’t help but question what I signed up for. The chamber we were walking into is called the Terra X Cube located in Bolzano, Italy, and is designed to replicate conditions from anywhere on the planet. The chamber can recreate a snowstorm on Mount Everest or a heat wave in the Sahara.

Variables like temperature, humidity, wind, precipitation, and altitude can all be controlled from a small observation room just one door over. I knew immediately when I crossed into the threshold of the chamber and the heat hit me in the face that this would be a rough trainer ride.

I was in Bolzano, Italy for an opportunity to participate in this testing in the Terra X Cube as well as tour the apparel brand Q36.5’s headquarters based in Bolzano. The apparel from Q36.5 quickly became my top choice for cycling apparel after getting the chance to test some pieces from their kit for the last few months. Our hot and humid summer in the Northeast gave a perfect testing ground for the Q36.5 Dottore jersey and bibs—and they shined. When a chance to learn more about this brand by visiting their Italian headquarters came up, I jumped on a plane—the opportunity to experience riding in a climate chamber was a special bonus.

Here I’m going to talk about the unique experience of riding in a climate chamber with the heat turned up and my biggest takeaway that you can apply to your trainer riding this season.

The test course and climate were designed to replicate the last section of Stage 2 from the 2026 men’s Tour de France ending in Barcelona. We rode with a custom workout in Zwift and our data was being tracked across the board: SRM cranks to track power, heart rate data to validate fatigue, and CORE sensors to track our heat strain throughout the workout. The room was set to a balmy 97 degrees Fahrenheit.

There was even a tester from Q36.5 in the room with us with a custom jersey. The jersey he wore was effectively two jerseys cut in half at the zipper and straight down the back. He had multiple CORE heat sensors attached around his body to track which jersey design and material was more effective at thermoregulation. One of those jersey designs will end up in a future product.

The ride was supposed to last for about 45 minutes, but our group was the first of the day and ran into some technical difficulties getting the trainers set up. In one sense this was good because we were able to have an extended warmup on the trainers, but the reality meant we spent an extra 45 minutes warming up in the already heated up chamber.

Thankfully, over the last few months, I’ve been doing heat training protocols with my CORE training sensor. I dove straight in with six weeks of active heat training, then I pivoted to strictly passive heat training with my home sauna. While I was absolutely heating up I was relatively comfortable rolling into the ride.

Once we got through our extended warmup we began the proper ride. Effectively we were shooting for an over-under workout that emulated the end of stage two at this year’s Tour de France. I had my CORE temperature data up on the screen and saw that before we were starting the ride my internal temperature was at about 99 degrees. The giant fans at the front of the room would change power to replicate the airflow during climbs versus descents, but at those temperatures it felt like sitting in front of a massive hair dryer.

As we began the official warmup my temperature steadily climbed—according to my CORE sensor I peaked just over 102 degrees. In the middle of the test I looked around the room and everyone looked like they were putting out high effort, even when we were supposed to be sitting in our recovery phase. The heat was taking its toll and our views did not inspire a boost in energy. Staring at the metallic walls while pedaling on our hamster wheel bikes did not give inspiration for an extra push for our final sprint. But as we finished up our not-so-cool cooldown I couldn’t help but think I would do it again.

After our group left the chamber the second group queued up for their turn on the bikes. The technical difficulties were overcome with our group, so they were able to jump straight into the ride after a five-minute warmup. That night we all regrouped to go through the data and we were able to see our data versus the data from some of the professionals in the stage we recreated. My biggest takeaway from the comparison to the professionals was their core temperature data. The professionals we were comparing against showed very little heat strain compared to even the fittest in our group. It’s worth noting the professionals had also already ridden over 60 miles before our route even started—those professionals were extremely heat adapted.

My biggest personal takeaway was how I could recreate this at home. When I talked to someone else in my group we discussed the similarities to this experience and heat training at home. When I do an active heat session at home I layer up in three layers top and bottom, a painter’s suit with the shins and feet cut off (so it doesn’t get caught in the cranks), and a hat or beanie for good measure. My active heat session numbers at home are surprisingly similar to this experience: typically a 45-minute warmup gets me into zone three of the heat strain index in the CORE app and then I spend about 45 minutes in this zone. This shows you don’t need a massive climate chamber to get the training benefits of heat training. Some warm layers, a painter’s suit, a CORE sensor, and a bike trainer can give you the benefits you need to develop this component of training.

Jason Griffin is Bicycling's Senior Health and Fitness Editor, bringing more than a decade of fitness experience and years in the cycling industry to every story. A former personal trainer and bike shop manager, he's lined up for crits, tackled enduros, but prefers getting lost on multi-day bikepacking adventures. Lately though, his toughest balancing act has been fitting trainer rides between family life and the next adventure.

#2722. Backpacker Rescued in Central Arizona After Floods Sweep Away His Gear

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

(Photo: Courtesy Yavapai County Sheriff's Office)

Search and rescue teams mobilized to help a backpacker in Arizona’s Yavapai County on October 24 after a swollen creek swept his camping gear away.

Fall in Yavapai County typically comes with cool, dry conditions, making it a popular time for hikers to log time on the trail. But the National Weather Service issued a flood watch for northern and central Arizona on October 21 that remained in effect through the evening of the 22, advising visitors to be wary of flash flooding potential in areas that are normally dry or low. A strong storm brought damaging winds, large hail, and flash floods across the region from a system that came from the California coast.

The man was seven days into his backpacking trip when he lost his equipment. He managed to send a message to family alerting them about his situation just before his phone’s battery died.

Rescuers with the Yavapai County Sheriff’s Office (YCSO) Aviation Team, Forest Patrol, and SAR volunteers responded to the incident, combing the area near Sheep Bridge off Bloody Basin Road for any clues to the hiker’s whereabouts. On Saturday morning, aerial crews spotted the hiker, and brought him to safety.

“Although he was exhausted, scratched up, and at his physical limits, he was in good health,” the YCSO wrote.

This incident came just one day after the Yavapai County Sheriff’s Office received a call about a group of four people who were sitting on the top of a car while floods swept through the Wet Beaver Creek crossing near Dragonshead and Culpepper Ranch roads. Deputies said that the vehicle moved about 150 feet downstream. Footage from the rescue shows swift muddied water at about the height of the vehicle’s headlights.

While rain in October is normal in central Arizona, extensive flooding is less common. Rainfall reports showed that parts of Yavapai County received more than an inch of precipitation over the course of 48 hours, putting them at a much higher risk of flash flooding.

In response to the first incident, Yavapai County Sheriff’s officials urged hikers to, “know your physical limitations, understand trail conditions, and check the weather forecast for your route.”

#1617. 3 California districts under federal probe for ethnic studies materials

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The Education Department targeted curriculum activities under student privacy laws, calling the materials “invasive questionnaires.”

The curriculum at all three districts included "Wheel of Power/Privilege" or "Mapping My Identities" activities — or both, according to the department, which described the curriculum as "invasive questionnaires" and therefore subject to the Protection of Pupil Rights Amendment. The law requires schools to notify parents and obtain their written consent before surveying students on topics that would reveal protected information.

Under the second Trump administration, student privacy laws including PPRA have been increasingly used to crack down on activities related to supporting LGBTQ+ students, like mental health screenings asking about sexual orientation or gender identity.

In its Monday announcement, the Education Department said that the ethnic studies course activities "require students to identify personal information such as their sexual orientations, gender identities, mental and physical health conditions, family economic status, race, and more."

Parents also allege that the California districts did not respond to their requests to review the ethnic studies curriculum or they only allowed parents to examine parts of it, the federal agency said.

Out of the three districts under investigation, Palo Alto Unified School District immediately responded to K-12 Dive's request for comment.

"We will cooperate fully and respond to the Department. Parents have a right to know what their children are learning and to make decisions about sensitive personal matters," said Jason Glass, superintendent of Palo Alto Unified School District, in an Oct. 26 email. "We take that seriously, along with our responsibility to teach Ethnic Studies well. Those commitments are not in conflict."

Glass said the district is reviewing the lessons identified by the federal agency and how they were taught across classrooms, including whether any personal disclosure was required, and how parent requests to review materials were handled.

"If we find something that should be done differently, we will fix it," Glass said.

A review of the 9th-grade course and its materials, which are available on the district website, include the "Wheel of Privilege" as a reference underpinning student reflection prompts. The activity, which teachers could opt to include in their lessons, depicts a wheel including gender, citizenship, skin color, education level, ability, body size, housing and wealth status, among other things that it says can influence privilege. It's noted on the document that the wheel is a "teacher example used for class reference" and that "students are not asked to complete this activity" of identifying where they land on the wheel.

However, in the district's course description, it says students may use the wheel to write a reflection "on the degree of privilege you've experienced, areas in your life where you experience privilege versus marginalization, etc."

"Reflect on one area of your identity where you experience privilege. How does this privilege manifest in your life?" the section continues. "Reflect on one area of your identity where you might not experience privilege. How can this potentially affect your experiences or opportunities?"

In a further update to K-12 Dive on Oct. 26, Glass said both of the district's high schools confirmed that students are not asked to complete the "Wheel of Power/Privilege" or "Mapping My Identities" activities named in the agency's letter. The wheel is included in the syllabus "only as a teacher example used for class reference, and it is labeled that way," Glass said.

The Trump administration's investigation into classroom curriculum materials comes after it sent a Dear Colleague letter in August saying even optional mental health screenings and surveys for students will be considered "required" under the law and subject to parental notification and consent procedures.

The letter said that "parental access to certain instructional materials, including ones used as part of a student’s educational curriculum," also fall under student privacy laws.

Students’ decisions to fill out surveys on sensitive topics can be influenced by peer pressure, fear of not participating, or wanting to please a teacher or authority figure, Miller wrote in the Dear Colleague letter.

As a result, districts will be required to provide parents a chance to review "survey" materials and obtain their written consent for student participation. Providing parents opt-outs would not fulfill the requirement of securing parental consent in this scenario, Miller said.

In early October, the administration reiterated its warning to state attorneys general, saying that "many superintendents and school officials continue patterns of noncompliance." That warning said that in addition to forms and surveys, "anything else that can be used to evaluate a student's mental health" will need written parental consent.

#640. “Strangest of Its Kind”: Scientists Uncover a Bizarre Bacterial Self-Destruct Switch

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

An unusual bacterial defense uses DNA to switch on toxin production, suggesting a new way to build molecular tools that control genes. nb An infected bacterium can protect its neighbors by destroying itself before the virus inside it has a chance to multiply. Bacterial immune systems called retrons trigger this sacrifice when they detect particular strains of phages, the viruses that infect bacteria.

Decades of research have established a familiar explanation for how this works. A toxin stays ready inside the cell, restrained by small, single-stranded DNA molecules. An invading phage disturbs that DNA, releasing the toxin to kill the cell and stop the infection from spreading through the colony.

Researchers at Gladstone Institutes have now discovered a class of retrons that reverses the relationship between DNA and the toxin. In type VI retrons, DNA switches toxin production on rather than holding an existing toxin in check. The study, published in Molecular Cell, comes from the team of Gladstone Investigator Seth Shipman, PhD, its senior author.

“The way we typically detect retrons inside uninfected cells is by looking for this single-stranded DNA,” says Karen Zhang, a PhD student in Shipman’s lab and lead author of the study. “But type VI retrons caught our attention because they didn’t appear to have this signature. It was very odd, and we wondered if they might be broken.”

Tests in bacteria carrying type VI retrons showed that the defenses still worked against phages, leaving the researchers to explain how a functioning system could lack its expected DNA signal.

“That kicked off a long molecular odyssey,” Shipman says. “Whenever we tried the experiments we normally use to study retrons, they would fail, so we kept having to think of new ways to figure out how these retrons work.”

One early breakthrough came from Spacer-seq, a tool previously developed in Shipman’s lab. It uses molecular components of CRISPR-Cas, another bacterial immune system, to create a record of gene activity inside a cell. After standard methods repeatedly failed to detect DNA made by type VI retrons in uninfected cells, the team turned to this alternative approach.

“We had just about given up hope that any DNA was being made when this approach finally revealed a very, very low level of production in uninfected cells,” Zhang says.

That trace gave the researchers a reason to keep investigating. Further experiments showed that type VI retrons produced no noticeable levels of toxin in uninfected cells, either. Phage entry triggered single-stranded DNA production to rise to the levels needed for defense. As the DNA accumulated, it interacted with an RNA molecule that had been blocking toxin production. This interaction removed the block, allowing toxin production to ramp up.

The toxin appears to be a tiny protein that embeds itself in the membrane surrounding the bacterial cell. By destabilizing that membrane, it kills the infected cell.

“It’s almost the mirror-image version of how we traditionally view retrons, because the DNA switches on toxin production instead of inhibiting an already-produced toxin,” Zhang says. “It would be fascinating to explore how bacterial evolution could have resulted in such a complex molecular system—whether type VI retrons are older or newer than other retrons, or whether they’re a stepping stone between different types.”

Retrons can also efficiently modify and deliver single-stranded DNA, capabilities that have drawn the attention of researchers developing DNA-editing tools. Shipman and his team are working to harness those abilities to rewrite disease-causing mutations inside human cells with unprecedented precision. Understanding the machinery of different retrons provides molecular designs they could adapt for that work.

“This is, by far, the strangest of the retron systems that we or anyone else has come across,” Shipman says. “These retrons operate with Rube Goldberg-level complexity, using molecular mechanisms that we would never have thought to invent, but that we’ll use in future technologies.”

The type VI machinery is already inspiring work in Shipman’s lab on synthetic sensors and switches that could control gene activity.

“This system represents a unique way to potentially build a molecular sensor and switch encoded in DNA and RNA, that no one has thought of before,” Zhang says. “Synthetic sensors and switches have many possible applications, both in treating disease and in designing experiments to better understand how certain diseases operate.”

The tiny, highly potent toxins could have medical uses as well. The scientists are working on ways to incorporate them into new antimicrobial therapies that could target and kill bacteria responsible for human disease.

“This study highlights why it’s so important for a molecular technology development lab like ours to keep looking to nature for inspiration,” Shipman says. “We can use what evolution has given us as starting points to build complex machines that would have been tremendously hard to even conceptualize.”

Reference: “Reverse transcribed ssDNA derepresses translation of a retron antiviral protein” by Karen Zhang, Matías Rojas-Montero, Darshini Poola, Josepha M. Klas, Arturo Carabias, Dennis J. Zhang, Mario R. Mestre, Ruiliang Zhao, Ana Dávila-Hidalgo, Lieza Chan, Kliment A. Verba, Guillermo Montoya, Rafael Pinilla-Redondo, Alejandro González-Delgado and Seth L. Shipman, 13 October 2026, Molecular Cell. DOI: 10.1016/j.molcel.2026.09.026

The work was supported by the National Science Foundation, the Robert J. Kleeberg Jr. and Helen C. Kleberg Foundation, the Gordon and Betty Moore Foundation, the Lundbeck Foundation, the Fulbright U.S. Student Program, the Junta de Castilla y León and European Social Fund Plus, the European Molecular Biology Organization, the Novo Nordisk Foundation, the European Research Council, and the Villum Foundation.

#1227. These Are The 7 Best Fruits to Lower Blood Pressure, According to Experts

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Meet the top picks and what experts have to say about each one.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

High blood pressure can creep up on you, leading to symptoms like chest pain, headaches, and dizziness. And since the condition is linked to more serious health complications like heart attack and stroke, it’s important to stay on top of your numbers.

Blood pressure management is complex, and usually involves making a slew of lifestyle changes, including adjusting your exercise routine, tweaking your diet, and even taking medication. But when it comes to those dietary adjustments, consuming fewer packaged and ultra-processed foods and “replacing them with whole foods and antioxidant-rich foods like fruits can be helpful,” says Jessica Cording, RD, author of The Little Book of Game-Changers.

Meet the experts: Scott Keatley, RD, is co-owner of Keatley Medical Nutrition Therapy. Jessica Cording, RD, is the author of The Little Book of Game-Changers. Keri Gans, RDN, is a dietitian and host of The Keri Report podcast.

There's some research to back up these claims too: A 2022 study in the Journal of Clinical Hypertension monitored their blood pressure at home in over 2,200 participants ages 50 to 65. They self-reported how often they ate bananas, apples, pears, and citrus fruit (response options were: not every week, once or twice a week, three to four times per week, and at least five times per week), and those who ate those fruits more frequently had significantly lower systolic home blood pressure.

Ahead, dietitians break down the best fruits that may help lower your blood pressure to regularly add to your plate.

The big draw with bananas is their potassium content; one medium-sized banana delivers about 375 milligrams of potassium, per the USDA, which helps you meet your daily goal of 2,600 milligrams of the nutrient, according to the NIH.

Why does this matter? Potassium “balances sodium in the body and relaxes blood vessel walls, directly lowering [blood] pressure,” explains Scott Keatley, RD, co-owner of Keatley Medical Nutrition Therapy. Having some sodium in the body is important, but too much will cause your body to hold onto water, according to Harvard Health. When that happens, the amount of fluid in your blood vessels increases and raises blood pressure along with it.

Blueberries contain anthocyanins, which are plant compounds that improve blood vessel flexibility and support nitric oxide production, says Keri Gans, RDN, a dietitian and host of The Keri Report podcast. Nitric oxide helps relax the smooth muscles in the blood vessels, helping them widen so blood can flow more freely, decreasing blood pressure, she explains.

This green fruit is loaded with vitamin C—59 milligrams, to be exact. (That's over half the daily recommended goal for women, which is 75 milligrams, per Mayo Clinic.) Vitamin C “enhances nitric oxide availability," Keatley says. Again, nitric oxide helps to relax the smooth muscles in your blood vessels, lowering blood pressure along with it. It also helps to relax the blood vessels, making it easier for blood to flow through and lowering blood pressure as a result.

Like kiwi, grapefruit is rich in vitamin C, as one half of a grapefruit has 39 milligrams of the nutrient, per the USDA. “Compounds [like vitamin C and potassium] in grapefruit can improve the elasticity of blood vessels and reduce oxidative stress, which helps the circulatory system function more efficiently and lowers blood pressure,” Keatley says.

Grapes are loaded with polyphenols, which are naturally-occurring chemical compounds found in plants, Cording explains. These support the elasticity of your blood vessels and reduce oxidative stress in the body, lowering the risk of inflammation—which is also a risk factor for high blood pressure, Gans adds.

Watermelon contains an amino acid called L- citrulline. It boosts the production of nitric acid in your body, helping to widen those blood vessels and lower your blood pressure, Keatley says.

Like bananas, avocados pack a potassium punch—576 milligrams is in 100 grams, or 3/4 of a cup, per the USDA—to help with blood pressure management, Keatley says. They may also help with weight management thanks to the fact the contain healthy fats that will keep you full and satiated—and that can indirectly help to reduce blood pressure, he says.

Remember: High blood pressure is complex, and consuming more fruit alone is unlikely to make it to drop. However, eating more nutritiously can support your other efforts to bring it down. If you have high blood pressure, it’s important to work closely with your healthcare provider to come up with a comprehensive plan to help turn things around.
