#3913. The Power of Loving Awareness

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The most trusted resource for mindfulness and meditation.

Mindfulness can’t always change the outcome when life gets hard, but showing up with loving awareness can ease suffering and deepen our connection to each other.

Life is hard, and it might get harder. So, where could mindfulness or loving awareness fit in when survival feels tenuous and ultimately temporary?

Recently, I listened to a good friend sharing painful accounts of his health crisis, the loss of his sister to cancer, and how his work is suffering as a result. It all sounded pretty grim. I could sense his anxiety and distress, so I told him that sometimes I cheer myself up with this image: It’s like we’re on the Titanic and we’re all going down, but it’s ok because we have our arms around each other as we sink into the ocean.

After a moment of stunned silence, my friend said, “Is that your idea of comforting me?”

After a moment of stunned silence, my friend said, “Is that your idea of comforting me? That we’re on the Titanic as it’s sinking!!?” And then, because he knows I practice mindfulness, he added: “Is this your idea of mindfulness?”

I paused to reflect for a moment and replied, “Well, I guess I’m being mindful that this is my thought, and that by acknowledging it I can gently and kindly be present to whatever it brings up. Besides, I’m focusing on the part that we all have our arms around each other.”

I mean, c’mon. Yes, there is so much unhinging crap that we have to wade through every day. And most of us have very little control over important things that affect our safety and well-being, so it makes sense that uncertainty brings up worry and anxiety.

So, how could mindfulness hope to solve this? Good question!

For decades, all my plans to drink more water, practice the piano, and regularly go to the gym never seemed to gain traction. But I’ve kept returning to a mindfulness practice, even though it can be irritating to become more aware, like when I realize I’m judging, or that I just want to keep judging or gossiping, or stay impatient or demanding. It’s easier, often, just to carry on with the slime.

It can be irritating to become more aware, like when I realize I’m judging, or that I just want to keep judging or gossiping, or stay impatient or demanding.

But then, much to the chagrin of that part of me that just wants to scream into people’s faces, years of practicing mindfulness cause me to stop and look at whatever strong thoughts and emotions I’m laying myself open to and ask this question: “Will leaning into hate and divisiveness take me closer to, or further from, who I really want to be?”

Not to mention that rage-venting also does NOT make those bad feelings go away. Actually, it usually makes them worse. The ragey-ness supports a dark narrative, blocks nourishing positive interactions, and cuts me off from more complicated emotions, the ones I could see, welcome, and learn from if I had enough calm and presence.

So, a mindfulness question: What meaning arises when we show up with loving awareness…no matter what’s happening?

My mother lies dying in front of me. There are no words left to exchange. I hold her hand quietly for hour upon hour. She does not appear to be conscious, but I figure, you never know. I hold her close, no cell phone scrolling, just loving presence. I hope this might help her feel that she is safe and surrounded by caring love…wherever she is.

I have a thought: I am ferrying her from this side to the other. And I don’t want her to be afraid, since nothing can stop her from going.

I have a thought: I am ferrying her from this side to the other. And I don’t want her to be afraid, since nothing can stop her from going. Every once in a while, I tell her that everything is OK, and that she can go anytime she needs to.

At the end, at her end, there was nothing to do. There was only loving awareness. “Hello, Mom. Fran. Frances. You’re not alone. Do you feel my hand? Love is here with you. And, we are taking that ferry together, as far as I am allowed to go.”

I could not stop my mother from dying. Not only that, I’ve not been able to stop the climate crisis, or prevent so much gut-wrenching harm.

Mindfulness might not change an outcome, but it can reduce suffering by increasing loving connection.

Sometimes loving awareness might mean finding your voice, or your courage, and taking action against harms. Sometimes it might come down to bravely bearing witness to others’ suffering. Mindfulness might not change an outcome, but it can reduce suffering by increasing loving connection.

In fact, in my experience, that is a superpower.

You might meet people who practice mindfulness and still aren’t very nice. Well, mindfulness isn’t necessarily going to immediately make any of us a nicer or more loving or less judgy person. That requires choosing to take the actions that loving awareness illuminates, over and over again.

Practicing mindfulness can help us step back and hold ourselves in loving awareness before we lose our cool. And by using a mindful approach, we can learn to break habits like viewing everything and everyone as the enemy. We can learn how to intentionally engage with our vagus nerve to help regulate the body’s stress response. It can be as ordinary as extending your out-breath, a simple technique for helping the body stabilize.

When we are already feeling rocky, automatic thoughts and fears often trigger a threat response, appropriate when there’s danger to escape from. But without mindful awareness, we risk remaining caught in that response long after it’s helpful. When we get stuck in a dark narrative, when we don’t accept the invitation that loving awareness offers us, we can end up unintentionally zapping ourselves with adrenaline and cortisol, amping up our stress over and over, which you may have noticed is very depleting.

When we practice loving awareness, we are inviting ourselves to notice whatever shows up, without judgment. This might include how unpleasant it can feel to sit in so-called silence. At first, we might be overwhelmed by the busy buzzing blender of thoughts going on in our heads. You can picture your thoughts as grains of sand in a jar of muddy water. Shake the jar up and notice how long it takes for all the grains to settle. So, a few minutes of focused meditation might help some of your thoughts settle. But there are so many! That means you might not notice much difference at first.

Practice includes noticing what you’ve been ignoring, like unpleasant sensations of tightness and tension in your body, or difficult emotions you’ve spent years suppressing.

There are a million ways to practice mindfulness. For instance, you might try hugging yourself, or placing a kind hand on your cheek or forehead or belly. When you do, notice how a tender touch increases your loving awareness.

This means having space for all the wildness, and choosing to hold it all with love. That’s the power of loving awareness.

Elaine Smookler is a Registered Psychotherapist (RP) and teacher and was a founding faculty member at the Centre for Mindfulness Studies in Toronto teaching clinical programs including MBCT and MBSR. She also taught Mindfulness throughout the University of Toronto’s Faculty of Medicine, was a founding teacher for the Mindfulness Thinktank at Sick Kids hospital and a longtime faculty member at the University of Toronto’s School of Continuing Studies where she taught her own program, “A Mindful Way through the Creative Process” and part of the certificate program, “Mindfulness Informed End-of-Life Care.”

Elaine has offered mindfulness training to every kind of participant including: parole Officers, the Catholic School Board, Summer Camp Owners, Hair Stylists, Children, Teens, Employee Assistance programs and a broad corporate audience. She is also a guide on the app “Dawn Personalized Cancer Stewardship Program.”

She has a focused psychotherapy practice offering clients support and precise tools to help them find the kindest, gentlest, most successful way forward to meet the difficulties, challenges and obstacles that life is constantly offering up. She also explores mindfulness themes as a key-note speaker, columnist, standup comedian, actor and singer. www.elainesmookler.com

#657. Scientists Reverse 60 Years of Aging in Human Cells, Turning 80-Year-Old Cells Into 20-Year-Olds

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Cells from an 80-year-old were reprogrammed to a molecular age below 20, and the slow-motion reset could help reveal how cellular rejuvenation works.

What if scientists could turn back the biological clock of an 80-year-old human cell, making it behave like one from someone younger than 20? Researchers in Germany have accomplished just that, reversing more than 60 years of molecular aging while transforming blood cell precursors into neural stem cells capable of producing neurons.

The discovery, made by scientists at the University Hospital of Bonn and the University of Bonn, could provide new insights into one of biology’s biggest mysteries: how aging can be reversed at the cellular level. Unlike previous methods that reset a cell’s molecular age relatively quickly, this approach works gradually, allowing researchers to watch the rejuvenation process unfold over more than 100 days.

Published in Aging Cell, the study offers scientists an unusual opportunity to investigate what drives cellular rejuvenation, what slows it down, and whether those mechanisms could eventually help address age-related diseases such as Alzheimer’s.

Most cells in the body carry essentially the same genetic instructions, yet develop into specialized types with different jobs. Their identity depends largely on which instructions they use. Under normal conditions, a blood cell cannot simply become a neuron.

Researchers can redirect a cell’s identity through reprogramming, using combinations of proteins called transcription factors to change gene activity and guide its development. This approach has already allowed scientists to grow nerve tissue from skin cells, which could eventually contribute to treatments for neurodegenerative diseases.

The Bonn team used reprogramming to turn red blood cell precursors into neural stem cells. In earlier experiments, neurons produced through this approach formed connections with existing neurons after transplantation into mouse brains.

“In our current study, however, we focused on a different phenomenon: namely, the observation that cells become significantly rejuvenated during reprogramming,” says Prof. Dr. Oliver Brüstle, director of the Institute of Reconstructive Neurobiology at the University Hospital of Bonn and a member of the Transdisciplinary Research Area (TRA) Life & Health at the University of Bonn.

To track rejuvenation, the researchers examined chemical modifications to DNA that change with age. These modifications influence how genetic information is used without rewriting the DNA sequence itself (epigenetics). Epigenetic clocks use patterns in these markers to estimate a cell’s molecular age.

After reprogramming, cells from an 80-year-old registered an age below 20, a reduction of more than 60 years after 50 days.

“We also know that this epigenetic rejuvenation ensures that the cells actually behave like young cells,” says Brüstle.

Scientists had previously observed rejuvenation during a two-step reprogramming process. A specialized cell is first returned to a pluripotent stem cell state, giving it the potential to become virtually any cell type. Researchers then direct it to become a neural stem cell, with a much narrower range of developmental possibilities.

Unlike the conventional two-step approach, which produces rapid rejuvenation, the Bonn team converted blood cell precursors directly into neural stem cells without passing through a pluripotent stage. The cells’ molecular clocks reset gradually, allowing researchers to track the process for more than 100 days.

That extended timeline gives scientists an opportunity to examine the process as it happens and test what speeds it up or holds it back.

“Rejuvenation that extends over such a long period of time is ideal as an experimental model: since the epigenetic clocks are slowly reset over several weeks, we can use this model to investigate which factors and active substances accelerate or slow down the rejuvenation process.”

For Brüstle, understanding those controls is particularly relevant to brain research: “After all, age is the most important risk factor for neurodegenerative diseases such as Alzheimer’s!”

Reference: “Protracted Fate Acquisition and Epigenetic De-Aging During Induced Neural Stem Cell Conversion of Human Blood Cells” by Lea Jessica Berg, Julia Franzen, Andreas Buness, Rachel Konang, Chao Sheng, Michael Peitz, Andreas Till, Wolfgang Wagner and Oliver Brüstle, 3 October 2026, Aging Cell. DOI: 10.1111/acel.70751

The study was funded by the EU’s Horizon 2020 program, the German Research Foundation (DFG), and the Federal Ministry of Research, Technology, and Space (BMFRT).

#1274. Doctors Just Discovered a New Type of Depression

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

NEARLY ONE IN four cases of depression may fall into this category. A study published in the British Journal of Psychiatry identified “circadian depression,” and people with it have an internal 24-hour clock that’s out of sync with their external environment.

Like really out of sync. We’re not talking about jet lag. This is when your body’s internal timing network just can’t regulate your body’s systems.

It's important to get this out of the way upfront: Circadian depression isn't in the DSM-5—the “psychiatry bible” mental health professionals use to make diagnoses. In other words, it's not an official condition yet. But it’s important to know about. We had mental health experts break it down.

CIRCADIAN DEPRESSION IS a subtype of depression caused by circadian disruption, which is when your body’s circadian rhythm doesn't align with your external environment. People with circadian depression have disrupted sleep-wake cycles, lower levels of daytime activity, low energy, and weight gain during depression episodes, according to the study.

People who were classified in the study as having circadian depression were more likely to have severe symptoms of depression, including:

People with circadian depression also seem to be more likely than those with other forms of depression to have other conditions like ADHD, anxiety, bipolar disorder, obesity, or insulin resistance.

THE STUDY’S RESEARCHERS analyzed clinical and genetic data from more than 10,000 Australians with depression. To determine whether people had the proposed subtype, circadian depression, rather than general depression, they looked at factors like a person's social jetlag (the mismatch between their internal clock and external schedule), whether they were night owls, how tired they felt in the morning, and whether they experienced excessive daytime sleepiness. They also looked at whether people had poor responses to antidepressant treatment, and people with severe symptoms that were a mix of bipolar disorder, depression, and somatic disorders.

About 23 percent of participants fell into the category of circadian depression. This is more than a label. It’s important to recognize because, as the researchers pointed out, people with circadian depression tend to be less responsive to traditional antidepressant medications. That means treatment tailored to their subtype is especially important.

A FEW THINGS are happening here. First is people’s sleep schedules getting thrown off, possibly from chronic inflammation and a messed-up metabolism. There is prior evidence of a link between circadian rhythm and these two factors (though researchers are still on the fence on what causes what).

“The body’s internal clock helps coordinate changes in the brain throughout the day," says Hillary Ammon, PsyD, clinical psychologist at the Center for Anxiety and Women’s Emotional Wellness. That includes a feeling of alertness and motivation during conventional waking hours. When the timing is off, these coordinated changes are also affected.

“Increased tiredness, low motivation, or decreased focus can all be factors that lead to depression and also be traits of depression,” says Ammon. Having an internal clock that's off schedule also impacts your metabolism and stress hormone cortisol.

Increased cortisol release at night can lead to an increase in unease and difficulty sleeping. Changes in metabolism rates can cause weight gain. Weight gain and decreased activity are classic symptoms of depression.

The concept of circadian depression is not an entirely new idea, says Aaron P. Brinen, PsyD, assistant professor of psychiatry and behavioral sciences at Vanderbilt University Medical Center. “We've known that there is a link to sleep in a number of different psychiatric disorders, including depression,” he says. “When people are depressed, their sleep gets disrupted and vice-versa—when sleep is disrupted, you're more likely to get depressed.”

SINCE CIRCADIAN DEPRESSION was just discovered, there is no set treatment for it yet. But doctors who treat depression say the new discovery is a huge reminder that depression requires tailored treatments.

In the study, for example, the researchers found that people with circadian depression didn’t respond as well to standard antidepressants like SSRIs or SNRIs. In fact, people who meet the criteria for circadian depression might experience more side effects on these medications. Their suggestions? Treatments like light therapy and melatonin may reset the body's internal clock and ease symptoms.

Therapists spend a bit of time with the client anyway trying to pinpoint the drivers of their depression, says Brinen. “If it's being driven by sleep or sleep disruption, you're going to target that.”

The basics around mental health still stand. If you're having symptoms of depression, talk with a mental health professional about an evaluation and appropriate treatment. Plus, don’t be an armchair therapist and try to diagnose yourself. “I would be more mindful of the symptoms versus the diagnosis,” says Ammon.

Your provider can give you a full evaluation and recommend appropriate next steps tailored to your situation. “It may be that you need to be more thoughtful about sleep, travel, and work schedules,” Brinen says. It’s “just like when a person who is naturally hypoglycemic needs to carry snacks. You want to make sure you're prioritizing sleep.”

Korin Miller is a seasoned freelance writer specializing in general wellness, sexual health and relationships, and lifestyle trends, with work appearing in Men’s Health, Women’s Health, Everyday Health, Food & Wine, and more. She has a master’s degree from American University and lives by the beach.

#1263. What Happens To Your Body When You Cut Back—But Don’t Quit—Alcohol?

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

If you’ve spent any time on social media recently, your feed is probably packed with people talking up how amazing they feel without alcohol in their lives. But while a growing body of research has found that no amount of alcohol is technically good for you, it’s understandable to still want to enjoy the odd glass of wine or cocktail with friends.

There are a few studies out there on the potential health impacts of avoiding alcohol, but what happens if you just cut back? An all-or-nothing approach doesn’t work for some people, so it’s fair to wonder if you’d still be getting health perks by going this route.

We tapped two doctors who study the impact of alcohol on the body to learn more.

Meet the experts: Megan Strowger, PhD, a post-doctoral research associate at University at Buffalo; Emily A. Gordon, MD, MPH, assistant professor of medicine and program director of Addiction Medicine Fellowship at Rutgers New Jersey Medical School

You’ll still get plenty of health perks if you just have the odd drink, says Megan Strowger, PhD, a post-doctoral research associate at University at Buffalo who recently studied the impact on the body of cutting out alcohol for a month. In fact, she says cutting back can have a “profound impact” on your health, especially if you’ve been a heavy drinker in the past.

Strowger points to an older clinical trial that found people had lower blood pressure, better liver function, and reported a better quality of life within four months of cutting back on their drinking. That shows that “even a moderate reduction in consumption can lead to meaningful improvements for both the body and mind,” Strowger says.

It’s not a bad idea to leave room for a little alcohol in your life if you enjoy it and don’t struggle with alcohol use disorder, says Emily A. Gordon, MD, MPH, assistant professor of medicine and program director of Addiction Medicine Fellowship at Rutgers New Jersey Medical School. “There is a benefit to having a glass of wine to relax and enjoy time with friends, as long as it’s not chronic use,” she says. “The mental health benefits need to be weighed along with the physical.”

There is no hard and fast number, but there is some general guidance to consider. “The World Health Organization categorizes drinking into different ‘risk levels,’ and research shows that moving down just one or two of these levels can lead to significant physical health improvements and fewer alcohol-related complications a year later,” Strowger says.

Gordon points out that there are healthy drinking limits in place (no more than two drinks a day for men and one drink a day for women). “But they are probably higher than they should be,” she says. “As long as you’re moderating your intake, it’s likely healthy.”

If you really want to focus on health, Gordon suggests just trying to minimize how much you drink. “Ultimately, the healthiest approach is to keep consumption as low as possible, as there is no level of use that is considered entirely risk-free,” she says.

You may be just fine deciding you’re only going to drink here and there, but some people prefer a little more guidance. If you fall into the second camp, Gordon recommends thinking about when you tend to drink. “Is it to ease a little anxiety, socially, or out of boredom?” she says. “Then, try to think of healthier things that can be substituted.” That might mean incorporating daily stress-reducing walks into your day, taking up a new hobby, or diving into all the NA options out there to find something you enjoy that you can sip while you socialize with friends.

Tracking how often you drink (including the type and number of drinks you have) can be helpful, too, Strowger says. “This habit is associated with sustained reductions in drinking even a month later,” she says.

If you respond well to clear boundaries, Strowger recommends giving yourself a specific limit on drinks you’ll have in each sitting or per week. “However, it is important to practice self-compassion,” she says. “If you don't always hit your targets, simply carry on and try again during the next week or occasion.” It can even be helpful to practice ways to politely decline when someone offers you a drink after you’ve hit your personal limit, she says.

Ultimately, just do your best. “In general, the less you drink, the better,” Gordon says. “But we do need to recognize the importance of socializing and spending time with friends.” Basically, don’t feel bad about catching up with your crew over a glass of wine. It’s unlikely to hurt your health—and could even give your mental health a boost.

#431. NASA’s Perseverance Offers Clues to Protect Crews From Mars Dust

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Over 1,645 Martian days, NASA’s Perseverance Mars rover measured the fine and ultrafine Martian dust particles found clinging to samples of spacesuit materials that are carried aboard the rover, including some that are small enough to penetrate the lungs and enter the bloodstream. The findings will be published Tuesday, Oct. 27, in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences and could inform spacesuit and habitat design, dust-removal procedures, and crew health standards for future NASA missions to Mars.

“NASA has a long history of turning challenges into workable solutions and of finding creative ways to use the data that we have to answer questions beyond what it was originally collected for,” said Lindsay Hays, acting director, Planetary Science Division, NASA Headquarters in Washington. “Dust has been a surface-exploration challenge since the Apollo program. Perseverance is giving our engineers and flight surgeons measurements they can use to improve our existing designs well before the first American astronauts arrive on Mars.”

Lunar dust scratched visors, degraded outer spacesuit layers, and damaged their seals during NASA’s Apollo missions, and some crew members reported symptoms similar to hay fever.

Rounded by wind-driven collisions with other particles, dust grains on Mars are less abrasive than those on the Moon. They also are enriched in sulfur, chlorine, and iron and contain more silica than lunar dust. Regional and global dust storms on Mars can keep the grains recirculating throughout the atmosphere as well.

The rover carries a set of five samples of spacesuit material. The swatches, each three-quarters of an inch (20 millimeters) square, are part of a calibration target used to test the settings of SHERLOC (Scanning Habitable Environments with Raman and Luminescence for Organics and Chemicals), an instrument on Perseverance’s robotic arm. The calibration target for another instrument that provided the dust measurements, called PIXL (Planetary Instrument for X-ray Lithochemistry), carries a sample of Teflon and of basaltic glass.

Because Teflon contains no elements that PIXL can directly detect, whatever elemental signals the X-ray spectrometer picks up from that sample comes from dust resting on it.

The team also analyzed swatches of Teflon and Vectran, two materials used in spacesuits, on SHERLOC’s calibration target. NASA sent the swatches to Mars to study how spacesuit materials hold up on the planet’s surface.

“Perseverance’s main job is to search for signs of ancient microbial life, but the mission was also designed to help prepare for human explorers. That effort includes technology demonstrations like MOXIE (Mars Oxygen In-Situ Resource Utilization Experiment) and Ingenuity, as well as the spacesuit samples,” said Katie Stack Morgan, Perseverance’s project scientist at NASA’s Jet Propulsion Laboratory in Southern California.

While earlier measurements on Mars resolved dust particles down to about 3 micrometers across, PIXL detected two categories of even smaller particles: fine particles measuring less than 2.5 micrometers (a human hair is about 70 micrometers wide) and ultrafine particles around 100 nanometers (about 700 times thinner than a human hair). Particles on PIXL’s Teflon target and SHERLOC’s Vectran swatch averaged about 2 micrometers. On PIXL’s basaltic glass and SHERLOC’s Teflon swatch, dust particle sizes ranged down to about 100 to 300 nanometers.

Static electricity appears to determine which size dust particles stick to which surface. Dust grains become charged as they collide in the air, and different materials on the calibration target attract grains of different sizes. The finest particles stayed attached through years of driving and drilling. Organic polymers like SHERLOC’s Teflon spacesuit sample are common in the outer layers of spacesuits, and the results show they readily collect fine dust.

The study also found that some materials hold less dust than others, which could guide future choices of materials and protective coatings for spacesuits and habitats. Designs such as suitports, which let astronauts enter a suit attached to the outside of a habitat, and dual-chamber airlocks could reduce how much dust gets inside astronauts’ living quarters.

“The existence of Mars dust at these ultrafine sizes has been predicted for a long time, and PIXL gave us the chance to measure it directly on Mars,” said Scott VanBommel, a Perseverance scientist at Washington University in St. Louis and the paper’s lead author. “The larger clumps, the Martian dust bunnies, get shaken off over time. But the smallest particles stay behind, and those are the ones that pose the greatest challenge for human support systems.”

Chemically, the dust matches the iron- and sulfur-rich composition that earlier missions measured. The PIXL instrument also found the dust to be enriched in phosphorus and detected signs of calcium sulfate.

On Earth, airborne particles, or particulate matter, below 2.5 micrometers (that is, fine particles) are tied to a range of negative health effects because they can penetrate deep into the lungs and enter the bloodstream. Studies have shown that ultrafine particles (those no larger than 100 nanometers) can travel from the nasal cavity to the brain along the olfactory nerve.

Martian dust is composed predominantly of silicate minerals and carries chemically reactive compounds, including perchlorates that can disrupt thyroid function and reactive iron that induces cellular damage. Scientists believe that the health risks of Mars dust depend on the quantity of dust and length of exposure.

The PIXL results are the first on-site measurements of fine and ultrafine particles on Mars, and this research can inform broader efforts to refine indoor dust-exposure limits for astronauts, develop safe habits, and establish ways to remove dust while exploring Mars.

NASA is already working on solutions to extraterrestrial dust hazards, using ground test facilities and lunar missions as proving grounds for future Mars explorers. In March 2025, the agency tested an Electrodynamic Dust Shield, which used electric fields to clear lunar regolith from glass and thermal radiator surfaces on the Moon. Developed at NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida, it landed on the Moon aboard Firefly Aerospace’s Blue Ghost Mission 1. At NASA’s Johnson Space Center in Houston, engineers at the Lunar Development and Test Facility use vacuum chambers and lunar regolith simulant to test spacesuit components, mechanisms, and a Handheld Lunar Electrostatic Dust Mitigation tool ahead of Artemis missions to the lunar surface.

Managed for NASA by Caltech, JPL built and manages operations of the Perseverance rover on behalf of the agency’s Science Mission Directorate as part of NASA’s Mars Exploration Program portfolio. NASA JPL also built PIXL and SHERLOC.

https://science.nasa.gov/mission/mars-2020-perseverance

#1266. Experts Are Raising Concerns About Quartz Countertops—but It's Not as Scary as It Sounds

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Designers weigh in on the current state of countertop materials.

When it comes to “timeless” design, investing in the mainstay home elements that transcend trendiness may be harder than you think. While repainting a room may be necessary every few years to keep your space au courant, redoing kitchen countertops is far more of an investment and life disruption. Ideally, it’s a decision you’ll only have to make once during your time in a home. Yet, designers are now saying you might want to think twice before choosing this stone.

For years, quartz countertops have become as much of a kitchen staple as salt, pepper, and olive oil: The stone can have dramatic veining that rivals the beauty of marble, but the material is considerably more economical and durable. However, the sentiment on quartz countertops is shifting—and ahead, the pros are telling us why.

“For years, engineered stone was hailed as the ideal countertop solution because of its durability, consistency, and low maintenance,” explains Barrett Oswald, an interior designer in New York’s Westchester County. “While those qualities certainly have their appeal, we’ve always felt that quartz lacks the depth, warmth, and individuality of natural stone.”

She isn’t the only one who wants to quit with the quartz. The National Kitchen & Bath Association just released an annual trend report that showed 78 percent of designers are sourcing quartzite while fewer individuals are selecting quartz. Meanwhile, California is reportedly preparing to ban the production of engineered quartz as crystalline silica has been linked to workers developing silicosis, a lung disease that’s often irreversible and even fatal.

Though Kilian Camp of Élan Design House currently sees banishing quartz as a regional move, she thinks it’s only a matter of time before the rest of the country follows suit.

“California is definitely much more stringent on health codes than other parts of the country and it is usually a few years ahead of trends hitting the middle of the country,” the San Diego-based designer explains. “I expect the East Coast will take precautions soon to protect the fabricators.”

For Camp, quartz’s decline in popularity is due to one reason: There are simply better options. Though many of her clients are drawn to quartz for its affordability, she usually tries to steer them away from it.

“I don't love how it looks and I think real stone is much better,” she says. “Once I educate them on how real stone can be just as durable and take them to a slab yard to see the difference, they usually want real stone at least in the kitchen and primary bathroom.”

Despite its supposedly durable reputation, designer Jessica Risko Smith says the material does show some signs of age and can take on stains. “Clients often ask for it because they want a worry-free, bulletproof surface in a hardworking kitchen,” says the Santa Barbara–based designer. “In my experience, they expect unrealistic performance from it, and that creates a false sense of security.”

Steven Cooper of Cooper Pacific Kitchens in West Hollywood agrees: “I always tell clients quartz is stain-resistant, not stain-proof.”

Quartz might be falling out of favor, but the pros insist there are plenty of other great options. Natural stones like marble and quartzite might require more maintenance, but they simply can’t be beat in terms of aesthetics.

“We’re seeing clients increasingly embrace materials that develop character and patina over time, rather than surfaces designed to look the same forever,” Oswald says. “There’s a growing appreciation for the natural imperfections, movement, and subtle changes that make a home feel authentic and lived-in.”

Smith says everything ages, so don’t stress over a nick along the edge or a slightly more honed spot where you roll out dough for family pizza night. “Those are signs of a life lived with a material,” she explains. “Natural stone was formed over a lifetime and once it’s in your home, that life continues.” Though marble is classic, Smith says soapstone and granite are suitable for a low-maintenance kitchen.

If natural, and often pricey, stone is out of the question, both Camp and Cooper recommend porcelain slabs. “They’re a great option if someone is on a budget or to use in a shower or on a fireplace,” she shares. “Porcelain is incredibly durable and the veining looks much more real than quartz.”

#1282. The 9 Best Fall Fragrances, After 2 Months of Editor Testing

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

From warm gourmands to subtle skin scents, these are the autumn fragrances worth spritzing.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Fall is my favorite time of the year to switch up my fragrance rotation. As the temperatures drop and cozy knitwear replaces my breezy linen dresses, my perfumes shift toward richer and warmer scents. But with more than 3,000 fragrances at Ulta alone, discovering a new fall fragrance that fits your vibe can be a bit overwhelming. That’s where I come in as our scent-obsessed beauty editor who owns nearly 200 perfume bottles. For this story, I spent two months wearing 52 fragrances to help you find options for all occasions, moods, and budgets. I judged how seamlessly their notes developed on my skin, their longevity, and if the price are justified. I also asked my fellow WH editors to sniff my wrist more times than I’d ever imagined doing, but only 9 stood out as worth owning.

Some days I’m drawn to subtle skin scents that remind me of wrapping myself in a cashmere blanket. Other times, I want sophisticated florals, woody blends, or daring perfumes for nights out.

Ahead are the nine best fall fragrances, ranging from six new releases to a cult classic that deserves its flowers from the beauty community. These unexpected finds just might become your new signature scent throughout fall. I like a few so much that I’ll continue sporting them during the holiday party season.

After testing dozens of perfumes, I keep coming back to The Baroness—it's also the first one I recommend to friends and coworkers. This new fall release smells sophisticated, without overwhelming. I usually rotate my fragrance wardrobe based on my mood, location, and outfit, but this is one I constantly reach for throughout the season.

What sets it apart is how the pairing of orange blossom and chantilly adds a subtly sweet freshness. Most traditional fall fragrances feature leather and musk. The Baroness offers floral brightness that couples seamlessly with warm sandalwood. It’s an unexpected combo that has me sniffing my inner wrists throughout the day.

I knew this perfume was on my Mount Rushmore of fall fragrances when I wouldn’t stop thinking about it while wearing other scents. Whoops! Penhaligon’s is also a niche British brand, so you’ll likely be the only one in your friend group sporting this fall fragrance. It’s definitely my top recommendation for a signature scent.

Despite its princely price tag, a bottle of The Baroness is truly worth the upfront splurge. For starters, it lasts from my morning commute to the early afternoon. I just allow the remaining scent to linger and touch up before heading out of the office. The packaging is also beautiful; the glass feels weighty in my hand and the vintage-inspired gazelle lid is a real statement piece.

I didn’t expect a dermatologist to create one of my favorite fall perfumes, but Alexis Granite, MD, nailed it with Peace Mood. It’s a low-key mood booster for when I crave moments of serenity during the chaotic pre-holiday season. I dab on the stick fragrance to my pulse points—inner wrists and neck—and enjoy a subtle blend of violet, orris, vetiver, cedarwood, and cacao. Its unique jelly texture prevents spills during travel, so I like to stash one in my TSA beauty bag.

Skip the boring gift set and opt for this statement-making perfume from Drake instead. The stunning circular bottle includes an elevated stand. Cloudar is more than just aesthetics. It converted me into a fan of woody perfumes. This one dries down to a mix of tonka bean—imagine vanilla’s cooler, older cousin—vetiver grass, and atlas cedar that has strangers asking me what scent I’m wearing.

A bonus: If you order the featured full-size bottle from Ulta, you’ll get a Cloudar deluxe mini bottle from now until November 7. You can keep one in your purse for travel, on your work desk before heading out to happy hour, or proudly display it at home as I do.

Perfume isn’t just limited to your body. I’ve been testing Beyoncé’s Cécred Temple Oud for a couple of months, and I consistently get compliments whenever I spritz it on my strands. No, really. My fellow WH editors, friendly girls in line for the bar bathroom, and my picky friends always ask why I smell so good, and this is the secret. A long-lasting spice blend, night-blooming jasmine, oud, and warm musk add a mysterious touch that’s ideal for autumn.

I genuinely don’t know a single person who likes Nécessaire more than I do. The brand helmed by Into the Gloss cofounder Nick Axelrod crafted my all-time favorite body wash, and the lotion, serum, hand cream, and body scrub are practically daily essentials. Olibanum is Nécessaire’s first foray into eau de parfum, and it couldn’t have picked a more standout scent to launch with. It's a complex yet comforting aroma. The warmth of olibanum (another name for frankincense) pairs beautifully with a subtle, creamy grown-up version of vanilla. Notes of fig leaf and blackcurrant bud add a juicy, green brightness that balances the depth of the warmer notes.

Cozy girl season is here, and Well Loved by perfume expert and ISIPCA graduate Funmi Monet is a warm, sheer skin scent that’s like a hug from your bestie. A comforting mix of shea butter, buttercream, and cardamom reminds me of the soft warmth of applying a nourishing body cream, while apricot adds a subtle fruity twist. Together, these notes are soothing and feminine, without being sugary-sweet. Well Loved shines on its own and when combined with other aromas, including the warm floral notes of YSL Libre.

Libre is like confidence in a bottle. Warm vanilla, lavender, and orange blossom transition seamlessly from morning to night, though I prefer spraying this one on for daytime adventures because it’s unexpected. The typical morning-appropriate scent is made with fresh and light notes, with subtle projection.

Like most YSL perfumes, Libre is bold, but the balance of freshness and creamy sweetness prevents it from feeling overpowering. It’s the fragrance equivalent of Le Smoking, designer Yves Saint Laurent’s iconic women’s tuxedo jacket. Like Le Smoking, Libre is timeless for all occasions, ranging from a night out to the office. Just consider wearing one or two sprays to work to avoid overwhelming your coworkers.

Angels’ Share is my go-to gourmand fragrance. Boozy cognac, tonka bean, oak wood, cinnamon essence, praline, and vanilla make it the perfect perfume for crisp fall days. If the brand ever released an Angels’ Share body wash and lotion, I’d sign up for preorder stat. The aroma is truly that epic.

One thing to keep in mind: I only need a small amount of perfume for long-lasting wear, so this splurge-worthy scent can easily last me a year even with liberal applications. It’s definitely one of the most popular perfumes in my collection, with friends always spraying it on whenever they come over.

Of all of BornToStandOut’s impressive scents, Drunk Lovers is my favorite. It makes a bold first impression, so it’s what I reach for on special occasions and late-night adventures. The boozy, sweet, and warm notes are alluring enough to grab attention, yet feel intimate. When it dries down, the woody, ambery, and spicy ingredients like cardamom, add a more traditional fall-appropriate touch without smelling like a Thanksgiving dinner party.

#3464. Yoga vs. Pilates: Which Is the Better Cross-Training Workout for Runners?

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Should you roll out a yoga mat or step onto a reformer? Three experts weigh in.

We earn a commission for products purchased through some links in this article. Why Trust Us?

Yoga and Pilates have become fitness mainstays for good reason: Both are low-impact activities that work small muscles you didn’t even know you had. (Seriously, have the backs of your knees ever been sore?) They’re also effective forms of cross-training—especially for runners.

Incorporating practices like these into your routine can help you stay healthy, build endurance, and reduce your risk of injury. That’s because running is a one-directional movement, meaning you can develop imbalances, says Shannon Rowbury, a BowFlex fitness adviser and three-time U.S. Olympic middle-distance runner and medalist. Running is also repetitive, which means that any imbalances can amplify over time, she continues.

🏃🏻‍♀️ Learn how to balance running with complementary workouts that build strength and help prevent injuries in the Runner’s World Guide to Cross-Training.

“Think about your car alignment—if it’s off, even just a little, over many miles a small wobble can lead to a major breakdown,” Rowbury says. “Cross-training is a fantastic resource for runners. Not only does it offload some pounding if your muscles and joints need some rest, but it also activates and strengthens the muscles that might tend to be overpowered by our prime movers.”

Both yoga and Pilates can achieve this, but in different ways. So, which activity deserves a dedicated spot in your training schedule? We break down how yoga and Pilates stack up for runners, their advantages and disadvantages, and how to choose the right fit for your goals.

Yoga can be a great complement to running, particularly for mobility, balance, body awareness, and recovery, according to Shavonne Wood, a certified yoga and Pilates instructor and marathon runner. When certain muscles feel tight, yoga can provide a structured way to move through different ranges of motion rather than just doing a few static stretches after a run, she explains.

In addition to physical benefits, there are mental benefits as well. Training for a race can become very performance-focused in terms of pace, distance, heart rate, workouts, and race goals, Wood says. Yoga can give runners an opportunity to slow down, focus on their breathing, and move without another performance metric attached to it.

Not to mention, yoga helps runners become more in tune with their bodies, allowing them to recognize when they might need a rest day from running or when they need to pull back to prevent burnout, overtraining, and injuries, according to Theresa DeLorenzo, yoga therapist, Pilates instructor, and owner of Nutrition for Optimal Performance.

Yoga varies enormously, according to Wood. A gentle mobility-focused class and a very intense power yoga class are completely different training stimuli. Runners need to consider where that session fits into their overall training load, she explains.

Wood also cautions against assuming that having more flexibility is always better. “Runners need adequate mobility, but they also need stability and strength,” she says. “Someone who is already very mobile may not benefit from continually pushing deeper into stretches, and they may be better served by focusing on controlled movement and strength through their available range.”

Rowbury agrees, saying that many of the more popular yoga movements are static stretches—held for 30 seconds or more—that increase range of motion. “If you’re a runner who tends to be stiff, you might need this. But if you’re seeking speed, you need some tension in the tendons to create that springy rubber-band effect. As a result, yoga might not be the best before a speed session,” she says.

Rowbury is a huge fan of Pilates, saying it had a positive impact on her running—especially postpartum. “I think of it as my core connector,” she says. “Before Pilates, my legs, arms, and core were each strong, but they weren’t well connected. Through Pilates, I was able to improve power transfer.”

Think about the running motion and how your arms swing in counterbalance to your legs—that power is transferred through the core, Rowbury says.

“I started doing Pilates postpartum in 2018,” Rowbury says. “After about a year of consistent work, I was running faster 200s as a 34-year-old mom than I ever ran in high school, college, or as a pro. Typically, you lose speed as you age, but Pilates has unlocked my speed by enabling me to activate my deeper core and connect all my parts.”

Research backs this up: A small 2018 study in PLOS One found that those who followed a 12-week Pilates program ran a faster 5K afterward than those who didn’t do Pilates workouts.

A limitation of Pilates is that it shouldn’t necessarily replace traditional strength training, according to Wood. “Runners still benefit from progressive resistance training and appropriately loading muscles and bones,” she says. “I see Pilates as an excellent complement to running and strength training rather than a complete substitute for strength work.”

Additionally, just like yoga, the quality and intensity of Pilates classes vary considerably. A class should be appropriate for the runner’s ability, injury history, and overall training load, Wood says.

Both yoga and Pilates are excellent additions to any runner’s cross-training routine, according to DeLorenzo, as long as you don’t have any preexisting back or neck injuries. Regardless, anyone looking to start a new workout routine should get the green light from their healthcare provider first.

With that being said, Pilates may have a slight edge over yoga, according to Wood. “Pilates provides more of the strength, stability, and movement-control components that complement the repetitive nature of running particularly well,” she says. “It can also incorporate a significant mobility and stretching component, so runners can still get some of the flexibility and range-of-motion benefits they may be looking for from yoga while also working on strength and stability.”

Rowbury suggests aiming for one or two Pilates sessions per week and two or three yoga sessions per week to keep your core engaged and maintain mobility and range of motion.

Ultimately, though, the best cross-training activity is the one you enjoy enough to do consistently, according to Wood. “If a runner loves yoga and hates Pilates, I would much rather see them consistently practice yoga than the theoretically ‘perfect’ activity they never actually do.”

Danielle Zickl is a freelance writer who has 10 years of experience covering fitness, health, and nutrition. She's a graduate of Ithaca College. You can find her work here on Women's Health, and in many other publications including PS, SELF, Well+Good, Runner’s World, Outside RUN, Peloton, Men’s Fitness, and more.

#686. Why Do Some People Get Fevers After Vaccines? Scientists May Have Found the Answer

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The microbes in your gut may help explain why you develop a fever after a vaccine while someone else feels fine.

Two people can receive the same vaccine and have very different experiences. One develops a fever and feels miserable for a day, while the other barely notices anything. Scientists have long sought to understand why these reactions vary so widely, and new research suggests that part of the answer may lie in the trillions of microbes living in our intestines.

An international research team found that people who developed stronger fevers after vaccination already showed signs of heightened inflammation and distinctive gut microbial activity before receiving their shots. When researchers transferred their gut microbes into mice, the animals also developed stronger inflammatory responses to vaccination. The findings point to a bacterial protein called flagellin as a possible contributor and raise intriguing questions about whether diet could influence how the body responds to vaccines.

Vaccination prevents infectious diseases and protects public health, but concerns about fever and flu-like symptoms can discourage people from getting vaccinated. Understanding why these reactions vary could help researchers find ways to reduce them and address a source of vaccine hesitancy.

The gut microbiome is a plausible place to look because its trillions of microorganisms continually communicate with the immune system, helping shape its baseline activity. Because microbial communities differ between people, the same vaccine may encounter very different immune starting points.

To investigate, researchers led by Ruth Ley at the Max Planck Institute for Biology Tübingen established the µHEAT (Microbial-Human Ecology and Temperature) study. With support from Meral Esen at the Institute of Tropical Medicine in Tübingen, they recruited healthy young adults receiving a SARS-CoV-2 vaccine in 2021 and 2022.

Participants provided fecal samples for microbial analysis and blood samples for immune measurements. They also regularly recorded their temperature during the week before and the week after vaccination.

Organizing the study during the pandemic required the team to recruit participants before they had already received their vaccines, while keeping visits and measurements manageable.

“It was a challenging time to set up a new human study, with the pandemic ongoing,” says lead author Kelsey Huus, who led µHEAT during her postdoctoral research and is now an assistant professor at the University of Ottawa in Canada. “We didn’t want to burden participants with too many measurements or visits, and it was a bit of a race against time to get the study organized before everyone was already vaccinated. But it was absolutely worth it, because the project yielded some very exciting results.”

Some participants developed substantial fevers. Before vaccination, their gut microbes showed greater activity in genes involved in bacterial movement, particularly those used to make whip-like swimming structures called flagella.

Flagella are built from proteins called flagellins. Because pathogens such as Salmonella also produce flagellins, the immune system recognizes these proteins as potential danger signals and responds with inflammation. Blood and fecal measurements suggested that participants who developed stronger fevers already had higher levels of inflammation before vaccination.

The human observations revealed an association, but the researchers needed an experiment to test whether the microbes themselves could influence vaccine responses. They transferred participants’ fecal microbiomes into mice that lacked intestinal microbes of their own, then vaccinated the animals.

Mice receiving microbes from participants with stronger fever responses developed more severe inflammation than those receiving microbes from people with milder responses. The effect depended partly on the animals’ ability to sense flagellin, providing evidence that the bacterial protein contributed to the response.

“Vaccination provides a controlled way to understand why people can respond so differently to the same immune challenge,” says Ley, director of the Department of Microbiome Science. “Our findings show that microbial flagellin is one contributor to this baseline immune state, providing a potential link between differences in the gut microbiome and differences in immune responses.”

The team next explored what might encourage gut microbes to produce more flagellin. Participants eating plant-based diets tended to have weaker fever responses, making diet a candidate for further investigation.

“We could see that certain food additives increased flagellin production by gut microbes in vitro,” explains Benoit Chassaing, Inserm research director at Institut Pasteur. “Interestingly, these findings show that components of an industrialized diet may promote the growth of flagellated gut bacteria and increase overall flagellin production.”

Animal experiments offered further evidence. “We found that a high-fat Western-style diet enhanced inflammatory vaccine responses in mice,” says co-author Hirohito Abo of Georgia State University in Atlanta.

“While we could not show that diet determines vaccine responses in humans, our study findings suggest that diet is one factor capable of modifying a pro-inflammatory microbiome,” Huus says. “Interventional studies in people will be needed to determine whether dietary changes can alter microbial flagellin production in humans and, in turn, vaccine responses like fever.”

Future trials could investigate whether interventions targeting the microbiome can reduce strong fevers after vaccination. More broadly, the study suggests that gut microbes help establish an immune state that may influence responses to challenges beyond vaccines.

“Ultimately, this work adds to growing evidence that the gut microbiome plays a central role in human immune health,” Huus adds. “Understanding these interactions could now open new opportunities to improve how we predict, prevent, and manage individual differences in immune responses.”

Reference: “The human gut microbiome primes fever after vaccination” by Kelsey E. Huus, µHEAT Study Group‡, Hirohito Abo, Yi Han Tan, Ezgi Atay, Héloïse Rytter, Ronald Keller, Silke Dauser, Dai Long Vu, Meghan B. Azad, Rob Knight, Alfred Ke, Larisa Lotoski, Marc-André Langlois, Rong Liu, Alexander V. Tyakht, Nicholas Youngblut, Sang-Moo Kang, Julie Parsonnet, Lisa Maier, Benoit Chassaing, Peter G. Kremsner, Andrew T. Gewirtz, Meral Esen and Ruth E. Ley, 8 October 2026, µHEAT Study Group. DOI: 10.1126/science.aea7733

#695. Scientists Discover a Protective Gene That Could Help Keep the Brain Young

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Boosting a protective gene helped aging mice preserve memory, suggesting that strengthening the brain’s own defenses could offer a way to delay cognitive decline in people.

Some older adults remain cognitively resilient even when their brains show signs of Alzheimer’s disease. Researchers investigating the biological mechanisms behind this resilience have identified a gene called Pla2g2f that may help protect the aging brain from memory loss.

In a study published in Nature Neuroscience, researchers found that increasing Pla2g2f activity in aging mice protected against synapse loss, inflammation, and disruptions in fat metabolism. Boosting the gene in middle-aged mice before substantial cognitive decline developed also changed the trajectory of age-related memory loss.

The study’s lead and senior authors are Cinzia Vicidomini, PhD, and Amar Sahay, PhD, respectively, of the Mass General Brigham Department of Psychiatry, the Harvard Stem Cell Institute, and the Broad Institute of Harvard and MIT.

As the brain ages, protective and harmful biological processes compete to influence its health. When harmful changes become dominant, synapses deteriorate, inflammation increases, and problems with lipid metabolism can interfere with normal brain function.

“As humans age, both protective and harmful biological processes shape brain health. When the balance shifts in favor of harmful processes, age-related changes in the brain can accelerate, including synapse loss, disruptions in lipid metabolism and chronic inflammation,” said Vicidomini.

To identify factors that might counteract these changes, the researchers searched for genes activated in response to synapse loss in the hippocampus, a brain region essential for memory. They focused on dentate granule cells, neurons involved in forming and processing memories.

Their investigation identified Pla2g2f, a gene that produces a secreted enzyme called a phospholipase. Its role in the brain had previously been unknown.

The researchers discovered that Pla2g2f levels increased in the hippocampus during aging and under conditions of brain inflammation. The gene was also expressed at higher levels in older adults whose brains showed signs of Alzheimer’s disease pathology but who remained cognitively resilient.

These findings suggested that Pla2g2f could be part of a natural protective response that helps the brain withstand age-related damage.

To investigate the gene’s function, the team used genetic and viral tools to decrease or increase Pla2g2f activity in the brains of aging mice.

Reducing the gene’s activity worsened several features of brain aging, including synapse loss, inflammatory changes in microglia, increased reactivity in astrocytes, and impaired formation and maturation of new neurons. The mice also experienced disruptions in lipid metabolism, accumulation of fat droplets in brain cells, and greater memory impairment.

Increasing Pla2g2f activity had the opposite effect. It protected against many of these changes, helped maintain healthy communication between neurons, and preserved memory.

“Remarkably, boosting Pla2g2f in middle-aged mice before they developed substantial cognitive decline altered the trajectory of age-related memory loss,” said Sahay.

The results suggest that strengthening this protective mechanism before significant damage occurs may be especially valuable.

The researchers also uncovered a connection between Pla2g2f and the regulation of fats in the brain.

Microglia, the brain’s immune cells, help clear waste, maintain synapses, and support neurons. During aging and Alzheimer’s disease, however, these cells can accumulate lipid droplets, making them less effective and contributing to chronic inflammation.

Experiments using human microglia derived from induced pluripotent stem cells showed that neuronal PLA2G2F could influence these immune cells. Increasing its activity reduced the accumulation of lipid droplets and cholesterol esters, a form of stored cholesterol. Both are commonly found at elevated levels in microglia associated with Alzheimer’s disease.

Further experiments showed that disrupting the enzyme’s catalytic site prevented its ability to mobilize lipid droplets and reduce inflammatory changes in brain cells. This suggests that the enzyme’s biochemical activity is important for its protective effects.

Together, the findings point to PLA2G2F as a protective signal released by neurons that helps maintain healthy lipid metabolism and limit inflammation across brain cells.

Aging is the greatest risk factor for Alzheimer’s disease, making the discovery of natural mechanisms that preserve memory particularly important.

The researchers believe that increasing Pla2g2f activity could eventually offer a way to strengthen cognitive resilience and potentially delay the onset of mild cognitive impairment or Alzheimer’s disease.

“Our findings suggest that increasing Pla2g2f activity during aging could potentially help maintain cognitive resilience and delay or prevent the onset of mild cognitive impairment and Alzheimer’s disease,” said Sahay.

However, the evidence for memory protection currently comes from animal experiments. Although the researchers also demonstrated beneficial effects in human microglia, it remains unknown whether increasing Pla2g2f activity would safely protect memory in people.

“Additional studies will be needed to determine whether this approach can be safely translated into therapies for patients,” Sahay said. “We are actively exploring potential ways to safely increase Pla2g2f levels in the aging brain and in Alzheimer’s disease as a possible therapeutic strategy.”

Reference: “An aging-sensitive secreted phospholipase confers neuroprotection and cognitive resilience” by Cinzia Vicidomini, Travis D. Goode, Ruilin Yu, Iyanah Mehta, Kathleen M. McAvoy, Connor H. Beveridge, Caitlin E. Randolph, Sanjay N. Iyer, Matheus B. Victor, Noelle Leary, Liam Evans, Michael J. Steinbaugh, Zon Weng Lai, Marina C. Lyon, Manuel Rico Fernández San Silvestre, Giorgia Tosoni, Dilara Ayyildiz, Shannan Ho Sui, Kei Yamamoto, Makoto Murakami, Evgenia Salta, Li-Huei Tsai, Gaurav Chopra and Amar Sahay, 15 October 2026, Nature Neuroscience. DOI: 10.1038/s41593-026-02465-z

This paper was supported by Simons Collaboration on Plasticity and the Aging Brain (AN-NC-AB-Research-00811243).

Disclosure: Sahay and Vicidomini are named co-inventors on U.S. patent PCT/US2023/01690, vectors and methods for the treatment of neurodegeneration, delaying cognitive decline and improving memory.

#708. A Simple Diet Change Could Transform Kidney Disease Treatment, 10-Year Study Finds

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

For kidney patients with acid buildup, a decade-long trial suggests fruits and vegetables may offer what a standard treatment did not: fewer hospital visits without higher overall costs.

When kidneys lose their ability to function properly, the consequences can extend far beyond waste buildup. People with chronic kidney disease can also develop excess acid in their blood, a condition known as metabolic acidosis that can contribute to worsening kidney function and other serious complications. Doctors commonly treat this imbalance with sodium bicarbonate, but new research suggests that a surprisingly simple dietary approach could offer greater long-term benefits.

In a decade-long clinical trial, researchers found that patients who received fruits and vegetables to help neutralize excess acid experienced substantially fewer hospitalizations and emergency department visits than those receiving usual care. O

ver 10 years, the 36 patients in the dietary group had just 14 hospitalizations and 20 emergency visits, compared with 64 and 88, respectively, among the 36 patients receiving usual care. Only four patients in the dietary group required dialysis, compared with 16 in the usual-care group, raising intriguing questions about the role of nutrition in protecting kidney health.

The trial enrolled 108 adults with stage 3 chronic kidney disease (CKD) and metabolic acidosis, the medical term for excess acid buildup in the body. Researchers randomly assigned participants to three groups of 36, receiving fruits and vegetables, oral sodium bicarbonate, or usual care. The dietary approach used base-producing fruits and vegetables, which help counter acidity. Both the dietary treatment and sodium bicarbonate were intended to reduce dietary acid by half, allowing researchers to compare two approaches with the same acid-reduction goal.

Participants were followed annually for 10 years. Across all three groups, researchers recorded 115 hospitalizations and 166 emergency department visits, and 34 patients needed dialysis. Despite sharing the dietary treatment’s acid-reduction goal, sodium bicarbonate showed no difference from usual care in hospitalizations, emergency department visits, or progression to dialysis.

“Treatment of metabolic acidosis with fruits and vegetables was associated with more favorable long-term clinical outcomes and health care utilization than standard treatment with sodium bicarbonate or usual care, without increasing overall health-system or societal costs,” said corresponding author Nimrit Goraya, MD, of Baylor Scott and White Health.

The findings will be presented at ASN Kidney Week 2026, October 22–25. The trial’s comparison of individual treatments also raises the question of how dietary interventions might affect care and spending across a larger population.

“These findings support evaluating dietary interventions in chronic kidney disease not only at the individual-patient level but also at the population level, where the cumulative clinical and economic benefits may be substantial. They also highlight the potential quality-of-life and health-system value of investing in fruits and vegetables as a treatment strategy, particularly when favorable clinical outcomes can be achieved without increasing overall costs.”

#22. Sudan paramilitary drone strikes cause mass power cuts in eastern states

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Rapid Support Forces strike five power stations in three states controlled by Sudanese army

Sudan’s paramilitary Rapid Support Forces have launched a coordinated drone attack against five power stations, causing mass power outages across the country’s east, a government official said.

The drones hit two power stations each in the states of Al-Jazira and Gedaref and one in White Nile state, “resulting in power outages across large parts of these three states”, the official said on condition of anonymity.

All three states are controlled by the Sudanese army, which has been at war with the RSF, its former ally, since April 2023.

The United Nations has repeatedly warned that regular attacks on power stations threaten health and services for millions of people who are already facing the world’s largest hunger crisis.

Babiker Youssef, a resident of Al-Fao, in Gedaref, said the power had been out there since early morning.

“My son is diabetic, his insulin has to be stored in the fridge,” he said over the phone, adding that he feared he would not be able to replace the medication if it spoiled. “We have no idea when the power will come back,” he said.

Electricity has been cut for days at a time after past paramilitary drone strikes on power stations and hydroelectric dams.

Sudan’s war is currently being fought primarily via drones, with both sides launching sophisticated attacks across the country, killing dozens of civilians at a time.

In the first three weeks of October, drone strikes killed more than 300 people and injured hundreds more, according to a UN tally.

Africa’s third largest country, home to an estimated 50 million people, has been ravaged by the war, which aid workers estimate has killed more than 200,000 people.

The majority of Sudan’s hospitals have been forced to close, at least 8 million children are out of school and swathes of the country cannot access basic services.

Abdelhafez Daafallah, a 54-year-old farmer, said by phone from Al-Junaid town in Al-Jazira: “The electricity had just started to improve three months ago, the power station the militia had destroyed was just repaired and we had hope our irrigation pumps would finally start running again.

“We hoped we would be able to finally get back to farming, but now the station has been hit,” he said, frustrated after nearly four years unable to work his land.

Farmers in Al-Jazira have struggled to rehabilitate and replant their land since the army recaptured the state from the RSF last year.

To the east, in Gedaref, Ibrahim Mohammed, 37, a grocer, said he was “already facing power cuts for hours before the strike”. “Now I have chicken in the fridge, we either lose our entire stock, or will be forced to sell it off at any price, which is still a loss,” he said.

Sudan is also grappling with unprecedented inflation and its currency has lost more than 25% of its value in recent weeks.

#2160. An Illustrated Guide to Famous (and Infamous) Figures from the History of Psychiatry

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

My writing career has been like a time-travel cruise studded with real-life celebrities. In my Sano Ichiro samurai detective series set in 17th and 18th century Japan, I got up close and personal with the shogun. In my two Charlotte Bronte novels, I hobnobbed with the famous author from Haworth, England and none other than Queen Victoria. I began my Sarah Bain series with a backstage pass to the Jack the Ripper investigation, starring some of the most famous cops in police history.

My new novel, Resurrection Kill, is set in a psychiatric hospital in upstate New York in 1953, a world just as dark as feudal Japan and Victorian England. The psychiatric profession had its own luminaries, who weren’t necessarily well known to the public but were revered—or reviled—among its own membership. Here I’ll introduce some major players. I’m a comics artist as well as a suspense novelist, so I’ve drawn these portraits of them.

Meet Dr. Frieda Fromm-Reichmann (1889 – 1957). A German psychiatrist and contemporary of Sigmund Freud, she immigrated to America during World War II. She invented the concept of the “schizophrenogenic mother,” theorizing that psychological disorders stem from mothers who neglected, overprotected, or otherwise mistreated their children. Her legacy is generations of moms getting blamed for all their kids’ problems. Dr. Emma Koffka is her fictional counterpart in Resurrection Kill.

And then there’s Dr. Walter Jackson Freeman (1895 – 1972), a neurologist who popularized the use of lobotomy for psychiatric patients. His form of lobotomy involved hammering an instrument that resembled an icepick through an eye socket into the brain.

Dr. Freeman traveled around the country, performing (sometimes fatal) lobotomies on thousands of patients. He even made house calls—drive-by lobotomies. What a sweetheart! In Resurrection Kill, he struts his stuff as Dr. Bradley Buscher (a.k.a. Dr. Brain-Butcher).

Last but not least is Dr. Ugo Cerletti (1877 – 1963), nominated for the Nobel Prize for his pioneering development of electroconvulsive therapy, a.k.a. shock treatment. While ECT nowadays can be an effective treatment for disorders that are resistant to other therapies, during the early years it was unregulated, primitive science that often caused brain damage. A major character in Resurrection Kill was the unfortunate recipient of recklessly deployed shock treatment. Here, I gave Dr. Cerletti a taste of his own medicine.

Laura Joh Rowland is the award-winning author of the samurai detective Sano Ichirō mystery series set in seventeenth-century Japan, as well as a historical suspense series starring Charlotte Brontë. Her work has been published in twenty-one countries; nominated for the Anthony Award, the Hammett Prize, and the Barnes & Noble Discover Great New Writers Award; won RT Magazine's Reader's Choice Award; and been included in the Wall Street Journal's list of the five best historical mystery novels. Laura holds a bachelor of science and a master of public health degree from the University of Michigan. She is a former aerospace scientist, a painter, and a cartoonist. She lives in New York City with her husband Marty.

#1631. How educators are breaking down attendance barriers

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Two reports examine what’s working — and what’s not — as schools try to combat chronic absenteeism.

Here's what working in schools to reduce chronic absenteeism: building family engagement, fostering student connectedness, having dedicated attendance teams and implementing comprehensive responses.

Here's what's not working as effectively as those methods: vigorously using truancy laws to get students back in classrooms and conducting surveys to better understand why students are chronically absent.

These are the conclusions of two reports released today, one of which was based on research into evidence-based practices to tackle attendance problems.

Despite progress made over the past few years to reduce chronic absenteeism, most schools still have students missing class at above pre-pandemic levels, according to the attendance experts behind the reports.

In 2019 — the year before the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic — the chronic absenteeism rate was 15.4%. That number rose to 28.3% in 2022 and then fell to 22.6% in 2025, according to a 45-state analysis by the American Enterprise Institute. Student chronic absenteeism is broadly defined as missing 10% of the school year, or about 18 days for any reason, including excused and unexcused absences.

Both reports find that it is important for schools to understand why their students are not attending school. The joint release of the two reports is meant to help education leaders and their partners commit to proven strategies to increase attendance, which education experts say benefits students' engagement and learning.

School leaders pointed to the use of multiple evidence-based strategies to boost attendance as an effective approach to tackling absenteeism, according to the analysis of principal surveys. That analysis was conducted by the Everyone Graduates Center, Partnership for Student Success and the GRAD Partnership. The groups analyzed two surveys of principals during the 2024-25 school year about why some schools continue to struggle with chronic absenteeism while others have made attendance improvements.

The survey findings show that substantial reductions in chronic absenteeism are more common in schools that have multiple effective attendance strategies that are aligned with the root causes of why their students are absent. These schools also have the capacity to implement these strategies at scale.

Principals who said their school had four or more effective attendance strategies were more likely to report substantial reductions to chronic absenteeism (77%) compared to school leaders reporting just one effective strategy (41%).

One approach, however, was at the top of the principals' list of effective attendance strategies: focusing on school connectedness. That includes improving student-teacher and student-peer relationships in addition to creating supportive school environments.

“The nation’s school principals show that reducing chronic absenteeism is a challenging task but it can be done," said Robert Balfanz, director of the Everyone Graduates Center at Johns Hopkins University, in an Oct. 26 statement. "Doing so requires weaving together and successfully implementing multiple evidence-based strategies aligned to the specific reasons driving absenteeism in each school.”

In the second report, published by FutureEd and Attendance Works, researchers provide a collection of more than 30 evidence-based strategies to address students’ various barriers to attendance, including those that focus on early childhood education and social-emotional supports.

Some of these attendance barriers are unique to a post-COVID school environment, such as the shift in parental attitudes to keep their children home from school due to potential illnesses or the assumption that schoolwork can be done at home digitally.

Meanwhile, some traditional attendance barriers remain challenging to schools, such as chronic illnesses, transportation struggles and family demands, the report said.

The report, known as the 2026 Attendance Playbook, organizes suggested inventions into tiered levels of support, including universal prevention measures for all students at the first tier and intensive interventions for individual students at the third and top tier.

The first tier of interventions, for example, focus on preventing absenteeism by building engagement with students and families and through rigorous and relevant instruction, in addition to other measures like effective communications, and safe and reliable transportation. Offering access to summer and after-school programs is one approach to improving attendance during the regular school day and year, the report said.

The third tier, which provides most intensive support, could include customized interventions to meet each student's housing, health and other needs. Suggested strategies for addressing student housing needs include outreach to local education foundations for resources and the use of Title I, Part A homeless set-aside funds to increase staff capacity and services.

“To reduce today’s elevated levels of chronic absence, educators and their partners can accelerate their progress by building off what works and not reinventing the wheel," said Hedy Chang, CEO and president of Attendance Works, in an Oct. 26 statement.

Clarification: This story was updated with information about the reports' findings on the importance of schools' understanding why students are absent.
