#2177. Brazil: Lula Should Focus on Human Rights in New Term

📅 Oct 26, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Click to expand Image President-elect Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva voting in the second round of the 2022 presidential election, São Bernardo do Campo, Brazil. © 2022 Rovena Rosa/Agência Brasil

(São Paulo, October 25, 2026) – Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, who has been reelected as Brazil’s president, should use his new term to tackle chronic human rights problems, Human Rights Watch said today.

President Lula defeated the Liberal Party candidate, Flávio Bolsonaro, in a runoff election on October 25, 2026, official results from the Superior Electoral Court show. Lula received 51.7 percent of the vote, while Bolsonaro received 48.2 percent, with 99.9 percent of the ballots counted, according to the Superior Electoral Court.  

“During his current term, President Lula has supported democratic institutions and made important progress on protecting forest and local communities who depend on it for their survival, but had a mixed record in other areas,” said César Muñoz, Brazil director at Human Rights Watch. “His administration should address the record high numbers of killings by the police and other public security problems, and more consistently support human rights policies at home and abroad.”

News media reported reduced public transportation services in several states, which may have barred voters from reaching voting stations on October 25. Federal police were reportedly investigating possible electoral crimes. The Lula campaign asked for an extension in the voting time in some states due to those disruptions. The Bolsonaro campaign also asked for an extension in other areas of the country due to rain. 

As the polls were closing, a Supreme Court justice extended the voting time for two hours. Shortly after, the Electoral Court, and later the Supreme Court justice, established that the extension applied to voting stations that were still open.

After taking office in January 2023, President Lula took important steps to repair some of the damage that the previous administration had inflicted on democratic institutions, the environment, and human rights protections overall. 

Yet, chronic human rights problems continued to plague the country, including abuse by the police, which disproportionately affects Black Brazilians. Killings by police reached a record high—with 6,589 people killed in 2025—and had kept rising in 2026 as of September, driven by failed public policy strategies that have allowed the unchecked use of lethal force by police. 

Abusive police practices undermine public security—a primary concern for Brazilians, according to many polls—by feeding mistrust and making communities less willing to collaborate with police, while fueling a cycle of violence that endangers both civilians and police officers. They also create an environment of impunity for excessive use of force in which police corruption and involvement with organized crime flourish.

While public security is primarily the responsibility of states in Brazil, the federal government has a crucial role to play in coordinating and promoting public security policies that are more effective at dismantling criminal organizations, protecting the public from violence, and upholding human rights.

An important measure to improve investigations that President Lula should support is to remove forensic services from police, to ensure independent analysis in police abuse cases and other crimes.

During an interview on October 15, President Lula defended trying and punishing as adults 16- and 17-year-old children accused of certain serious crimes. The measure would violate international law, which says that children should be subject to justice systems that are distinct from those for adults. That proposal would also undermine rather than strengthen the country’s efforts to reduce crime, Human Rights Watch said. Children who break the law can and should be held accountable, but in an individualized manner that promotes their rehabilitation. 

On the environment, President Lula should reverse inconsistencies that endanger the rights of Brazilians. His administration brought Amazon deforestation to the lowest level on record, according to official data, and resumed demarcating Indigenous territories. Yet, it took insufficient steps to regulate the cattle industry, historically the largest driver of deforestation and its related human rights abuses.

His administration also initiated a long-awaited national program to reduce the use of pesticides. However, the administration continued to allow the use of highly hazardous pesticides, many of which are banned for use in the European Union and elsewhere because of proven harm to health and the environment. This puts Brazilians at risk of acute poisoning and other serious health impacts, particularly Indigenous, Afro-descendant, and small farmers’ communities. 

Major corruption scandals involving a range of politicians, political parties, and institutions have unfolded in recent years. The Lula administration should promote measures to strengthen the fight against corruption, including working with Congress to ensure transparency and oversight over budget allocations.

Other long-standing human rights problems that President Lula should prioritize include gender-based violence, severe obstacles to abortion access, violence against human rights and environmental defenders, and the institutionalization of people with disabilities due to lack of sufficient community-based support services.

Some incidents marred the electoral campaign. Non-profit organizations compiled 134 cases of threats and violence against candidates and politicians from various parties, in the first 50 days of electoral campaign. 

Brazilian media also reported that Meta, which operates Facebook, Instagram, and WhatsApp, restricted some content and features that promoted Lula’s candidacy in the lead up to the election. Meta said it had experienced technical issues but contended that it did not favor one candidate over another. Brazil’s electoral court ordered Meta to take measures to ensure equal treatment of the candidates. 

Meta’s Oversight Board—an external body that can overturn Meta’s moderation decisions and provides non-binding policy guidance—expressed concern about recurrent failures in the company’s “election integrity efforts,” citing the “breakdown” that harmed Lula’s campaign. 

“President Lula, who cannot run for office again in 2030, has a chance to leave a mark on the country by seeking solutions to long-standing problems that make a real difference in protecting rights and liberties for all Brazilians,” Muñoz said.

#2185. South Korea: Medication Abortion Rules Too Restrictive

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Click to expand Image A box of Mifepristone combined with Misoprostol tablets, May 2026. © 2026 Soumyabrata Roy/NurPhoto via AP Photo

(Tokyo) – A South Korean government proposal would allow medication abortion in South Korea in 2027, but overly restrictive regulations will continue to impair the right of women and girls to safe and affordable abortion services, Human Rights Watch said today. National Assembly members have questioned government officials about the plan during a parliamentary audit. 

Seven years after South Korea’s Constitutional Court ruled the country’s decades-long abortion ban unconstitutional, effectively decriminalizing abortion and ordering legal reform by the end of 2020, the government has yet to amend the law to explicitly guarantee abortion rights. The government’s new proposal would finally establish a regulatory framework for medication abortion using Mifegymiso, a combination of Misoprostol and Mifepristone recognized by the World Health Organization (WHO) as the safest and most effective regimen. But access to the medication will remain unnecessarily impaired. 

“Allowing use of abortion pills is an important step, but South Korean lawmakers need to ensure that regulations aren’t so restrictive that abortions are inaccessible and unaffordable,” said Susanne Bergsten, acting women’s rights research assistant at Human Rights Watch. “Access to safe abortion is essential to protecting the autonomy, health, and rights of women and girls.” 

The draft rules would, for the first two years, permit medication abortions only up to nine weeks of pregnancy and specify that only doctors in large hospitals can prescribe and dispense the medication. If no side effects are found, it would likely be distributed more widely after the trial period. 

These restrictions are inconsistent with best practices, which endorse use of these medications up to 12 weeks, and with WHO recommendations that a broad range of health workers be authorized to manage medical abortion. Blocking many qualified providers from prescribing and dispensing the medication could hinder patients from receiving timely and critical care. Denying or delaying abortion access violates the rights to life, health, privacy, and freedom from discrimination. 

Mifepristone is approved by around 100 countries worldwide, and has been used safely in France and China for nearly 40 years. Major complications requiring hospitalization following medication abortion are very rare and occur in less than 1 percent of cases. The WHO also recommends telemedecine models of medical abortion care, in which prescriptions can be sent directly to pharmacies for self-administration at home. It considers this approach as safe for pregnancies of up to 12 weeks, provided the pregnant person has “accurate information, quality medicines and support from a trained health worker (if she needs or wants it during the process).” 

South Korea is obligated under international human rights law to ensure that all women and girls in the country can readily access timely, affordable, and safe abortion services. 

To meet these obligations, the National Assembly urgently needs to enact legislation that clarifies the decriminalization of abortion, and amends South Korea’s Mother and Child Health Act to ensure that the rights of pregnant people are protected in all areas relevant to safe abortion care, including health care, counseling, and support services.

Abortions should be available at a pregnant person’s request, without requirements for parental or partner consent, notification or involvement, or accompaniment for adolescents or women with disabilities. Lawmakers should ensure that abortion services are covered by the National Health Insurance plan, to avoid costs becoming a barrier that prevents people from accessing abortion services. 

The government currently operates a crisis counseling system under the Special Act on Crisis Pregnancy and Protected Childbirth, enacted in 2023 for women and girls with unwanted or unintended pregnancies. However, the system has been criticized for not providing neutral advice and failing to provide adequate information about abortion, delaying access to abortion care. 

The law, which was intended to address unregistered births, also failed to introduce a comprehensive framework to ensure that all women in South Korea can register their child's birth, access pre- and postnatal care, receive financial support and services, and be free from the stigma and discrimination often faced by single mothers. The government should take genuine measures to support all pregnant people, whether they choose to continue or terminate a pregnancy by ensuring access to healthcare services throughout their pregnancy, at childbirth, and for childcare support. 

The government should also improve support and access to information for women and girls experiencing unintended or unwanted pregnancies. A 2023 report by Moimnet (Network to Assure Rights for Safe Abortion for All) found that many people face difficulties obtaining reliable abortion information. The government should provide unbiased information about reproductive health and abortion care, and ensure that schools deliver comprehensive, age-appropriate sexuality education. People seeking abortions should be able to make informed and autonomous decisions with support from healthcare providers who offer unbiased and respectful care. 

“South Korea’s National Assembly should swiftly align abortion legislation with international human rights standards to protect patients and providers,” Bergsten said. “Without safe and affordable access to abortion services, women and girls cannot fully experience their autonomy and reproductive rights.”

But its effects may be particularly important during the teenage years, when the brain is still developing.

Recent research suggests that using cannabis at this age may have consequences that last well beyond adolescence.

Other studies suggest that negative effects on the brain and mental health may extend across the lifespan.

#3486. Are You Restricting Your Breathing? 5 Ways to Learn Better Breath Awareness.

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

In Yoga Journal’s Archives series, we share a curated collection of articles originally published in past issues beginning in 1975. This article about restricting your breath first appeared in the November-December 1992 issue of Yoga Journal. You can find more of our Archives here.

Our breath is constantly rising and falling, ebbing and flowing, entering and leaving our bodies. Full body breathing is an extraordinary symphony of both powerful and subtle movements that massage internal organs, oscillate joints, and alternately tone and release muscle tissue.

The waves of our breath also carry emotional content, sexual stirrings, and messages from the psyche, and they open us to our own creative potential. All human movement is informed by this internal oceanic tide. Learning to move with our breath, rather than against it or without its support, can transform our yoga practice and our life. When we move in harmony with our breath, our outer actions will be in accord with our deep inner experience.

Because respiration is primarily regulated by involuntary controls through the central nervous system, we might wonder why anyone should bother to “work” with their breath. After all, our bodies are breathing automatically day and night. But is calm, rhythmic breathing the usual state of your body? My observations as a yoga teacher have led me to conclude that shallow breathing and chronic breath holding are so prevalent in our culture that a person who can breathe freely is rare. This is a frightening state of affairs, considering the impact poor breathing has upon our health, well-being, and quality of life.

What does it mean to breathe in a healthy, functional way? This question is more difficult to answer than one might think. As pioneering breath-worker Ilse Middendorf points out, at one end of the spectrum is the unconscious, involuntary breath; at the other end is breathing that is controlled and regulated by the will, such as the classic pranayama exercises. In between these two extremes lies the “essential” breath, a conscious flow that arises out of the depth of our being and dissolves effortlessly back into our core. To access this essential breath, we must first be able to focus on and perceive our own breathing process; that is, we must make the unconscious conscious.

Some schools of yoga have incorporated breath and movement by designating specific patterns of regulation—for example, inhaling for 10 counts as you lift your arms. While this approach may give the practitioner a certain confidence by providing a structure to rely on, it does not offer access to the essential breath.

Other schools treat the breath as something that is added (if at all) only after the asanas are perfected. This latter approach is analogous to trying to learn to sail without knowing what wind is. If we ignore the breath, we will rely on willpower to create and sustain our poses.

When our will is driven from an external reference point—a mental concept of how the poses should look or how our breath should move—we will practice to become who we think we should be, rather than discovering, through the self-acceptance that is implicit in free breathing, who we actually are.

Movement arises from breathing, and breathing is stimulated and shaped by movement. Each informs the other. Therefore, to learn basic movement we have to learn to perceive the fundamental flow of the breath—not in an artificial, controlled form, but in its purest state.

Even the most advanced yoga practice is of little practical use if we can’t extend our awareness into our everyday lives. After you’ve practiced observing your breath in controlled situations, pick one of the following exercises and work with it for at least a week before you continue to the next one.

Record the activities and situations in which you notice yourself breathing shallowly or holding your breath. Especially note your interactions with people with whom you are uncomfortable or anxious. Observe whether the other person breathes shallowly or quickly or holds her breath. Are you matching her breath pattern? Do you hold your breath during discussions that involve conflict or stressful topics, or when you find it difficult to tell someone the truth?

Choose one simple activity in which you have regularly noticed yourself holding your breath. It should be an activity where there are no time constraints or pressures, such as making your bed. Practice allowing your breath to move freely as an integral part of the activity.

Set aside one meal a day in which you do not feel any time constraints. Let yourself breathe slowly as you eat. Notice how it feels to allow your belly to release as you chew and swallow your food. Observe whether your meal was more enjoyable. If you tend to overeat or have digestive problems, did monitoring your breathing help you stop eating when you felt your stomach becoming full? How did you feel after your meal—energized, fatigued, satisfied?

Practice reading a short paragraph aloud while allowing the breath to support your words. Read at a speed that is congruent with your breath capacity, letting yourself pause when necessary. Then proceed to monitoring your breath during telephone conversations. When you feel ready, watch yourself as you talk with a casual acquaintance. Your graduation exam for this exercise is to practice breathing during an argument. How does this practice change the way you interact with others and the outcome of your interactions?

Punctuate your day with moments where you check your breathing. You might use a cue, like observing your breath every time you hear a phone ring or see your favorite color. Try not to chastise yourself if you find yourself holding your breath. Just note the situation you are in, notice how you are feeling, and gently return to a more relaxed breath pattern.

#1301. This Woman Lifted Weights 5 Times a Week for 4 Weeks—Here’s How Her Body Changed

📅 Oct 25, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Makari Espe swapped two 20-min weights sessions a week for five 30-min sessions—and documented the results.

If ever you’ve wondered exactly what upping the amount of weight training you do could do for your body, let Canadian YouTuber Makari Espe’s story give you an idea. Already accustomed to 20-minute strength sessions twice a week, she committed to five 30-minute workouts every week for four weeks. The aim? To become “harder, better, faster, and stronger.”

To put her results to the test, she recorded her strength before and after the challenge, from how long she could hold a plank to how many chin-ups and push-ups she could do. Along the way, she also documented the reality of training five days a week on her YouTube channel. By the end of those 28 days, she wasn’t just feeling stronger—she could perform more reps, hold exercises for longer, and lift heavier weights. Here’s what happened.

“My current weightlifting routine is 20 minutes a day, two times per week, but I want to be harder, better, faster, and stronger. So, for the next month, I'm going to lift weights five times a week for 30 minutes.”

“Basically, the idea is to do two big muscle groups per workout so that each muscle group is rested by the time you work it out again and we're not all out here hating our lives. For example, it’s 7:00 in the morning and today we're going to be doing back and biceps. Tomorrow I'll be doing legs and shoulders, and then day three is a rest day.”

“I didn't come up with this routine by myself, by the way. I'm not a personal trainer and I'm very lazy when it comes to planning my own workouts. I wish this was a sponsor, but it's not. I'm just here to tell you that I'm getting all of my workouts from the Peloton app. Woo! This week I'll be doing the beginner 5-day program, and for the next 3 weeks I'll be doing these intermediate programs.”

Espe performed a baseline strength test at the start of her 28-day challenge and the same test at the end of the 28 days. She recorded the following results:

Before:1:30 plank / 1-minute wall sit / 2 chin-ups / 10 push-ups / 25 × 10-lb shoulder presses.

After four weeks:2-minute plank / 2-minute wall sit / 4 chin-ups / 15 push-ups / 25 × 15-lb shoulder presses.

As Espe’s body got used to the change in training frequency, she noticed her soreness increased. That post-workout pain has a name: delayed-onset muscle soreness (DOMS), the stiffness and tenderness that can kick in a day or two after a challenging or unfamiliar session as your muscles repair and adapt.

By week two, she said, “My triceps are killing me more so than any other muscle group this whole challenge so far, my body is sore in many places and I'm very tired.” She added that her hamstrings were “so sore” and her “legs hurt so bad.” By week three, she said, “The place where it hurts right now is my shoulder blades; it feels like somebody hit me with a hammer.”

The DOMS might have intensified, but she did work out an effective salve: active recovery. For Espe, that meant gentle cycling, swimming, and stretching, which helped increase blood flow to the sore areas and to ease the stiffness without adding to her training load.

She said: “It's 7:30 a.m. and I just went for a bike ride and now I'm going to go for a swim. I've been wanting to do that every day this week and I finally did it, and it was glorious. Best morning ever. And now I'm going to go stretch. The swim was amazing for many reasons, but the main one was it was so nice on my sore muscles.”

She didn’t set out with aesthetic goals, and it was her performance that really improved. On week three, she said: “I've crossed the threshold and I’ve found the joy in weightlifting again. I'm doing it because I know I can and I'm super strong. I just feel really strong and that's awesome.” A week later, by week four, she added, “I actually feel even stronger than I did as a teenager.”

As her fatigue increased in those initial weeks, by week three, she decided to take stock of what she was putting into her body—and her energy soared: “I'm putting so much more good fuel into my body which is giving me more energy to perform in the gym,” she said.

As the challenge came to an end, she noticed that her mindset had improved as well as her physical strength. “This is going to sound really cheesy, but it's because not only do I feel physically stronger, but I feel stronger in my brain. Like, I feel like I'm more resilient for having been able to push through all of the self-doubt,” she said.

“I will say that roughly 3 and 1/2 hours every week is a lot of free time to be able to put towards working out, but ultimately it makes me feel so much better outside of the gym, and that's kind of why I do all this.”

“So if you decide to do a challenge like this, absolutely take pride in your physical accomplishments, but please be sure to notice the benefits outside of the gym too.”

As Women’s Health UK’s fitness director and a qualified Pilates and yoga instructor, Bridie Wilkins has been passionately reporting on exercise, health and nutrition since the start of her decade-long career in journalism.

After earning a first-class degree in journalism and NCTJ accreditation, she secured her first role at Look Magazine, where she launched the magazine’s health and fitness column, Look Fit, before going on to become Health and Fitness writer at HELLO!

Since, she has written for Stylist, Glamour, Cosmopolitan, Marie Claire, Elle, The Metro, Runner’s World and Red. Today, she oversees all fitness content across Women's Health online and in print, spearheading leading cross-platform franchises, such as ‘Fit At Any Age’, which showcases the women proving that age is no barrier to exercise.

She has also represented the brand on BBC Radio London, plus various podcasts and Substacks – all with the aim to encourage more women to exercise and show them how. Outside of work, find her trying the latest Pilates studio, testing her VO2 max for fun (TY, Oura), or posting workouts on Instagram.
